## Transcript

00:00:00 --> 00:00:02
- Where are we right now, Paul?

00:00:02 --> 00:00:05
- Lex, we are in the middle of nowhere.

00:00:06 --> 00:00:08
- [Lex] It's the Amazon jungle.

00:00:07 --> 00:00:09
There's vegetation, there's insects,

00:00:09 --> 00:00:11
there's all kinds of creatures.

00:00:10 --> 00:00:14
A million heartbeats, a million eyes.

00:00:13 --> 00:00:16
So really, where are we right now?

00:00:15 --> 00:00:19
- [Paul] We are in Peru,

00:00:18 --> 00:00:22
in a very remote part of the Western Amazon basin.

00:00:21 --> 00:00:24
And because of the proximity

00:00:23 --> 00:00:25
of the (indistinct) cloud forest

00:00:24 --> 00:00:27
o the lowland tropical rainforest,

00:00:26 --> 00:00:30
we are in the most biodiverse part of planet Earth.

00:00:29 --> 00:00:33
There's more life per square acre, per square mile out here

00:00:32 --> 00:00:34
than there is anywhere else on Earth,

00:00:33 --> 00:00:37
not just now, but in the entire fossil record.

00:00:40 --> 00:00:45
- The following is a conversation with Paul Rosolie,

00:00:44 --> 00:00:47
his second time on the podcast.

00:00:46 --> 00:00:48
But this time

00:00:47 --> 00:00:52
we did the conversation deep in the Amazon jungle.

00:00:51 --> 00:00:54
I traveled there to hang out with Paul

00:00:53 --> 00:00:58
and it turned out to be an adventure of a lifetime.

00:00:57 --> 00:01:00
I'll post a video

00:00:59 --> 00:01:04
capturing some aspects of that adventure in a week or so.

00:01:03 --> 00:01:07
It included everything from getting lost

00:01:06 --> 00:01:08
in dense, unexplored wilderness

00:01:07 --> 00:01:10
with no contact to the outside world,

00:01:09 --> 00:01:15
to taking very high doses of ayahuasca

00:01:14 --> 00:01:17
nd much more.

00:01:17 --> 00:01:20
Paul, by the way,

00:01:19 --> 00:01:22
aside from being my good friend,

00:01:21 --> 00:01:25
is a naturalist, explorer, author,

00:01:24 --> 00:01:28
and is someone who has dedicated his life

00:01:27 --> 00:01:30
to protecting the rainforest.

00:01:29 --> 00:01:34
For this mission, he founded Junglekeepers.

00:01:33 --> 00:01:37
You can help him if you go to junglekeepers.org.

00:01:37 --> 00:01:42
This trip for me was life-changing.

00:01:41 --> 00:01:44
It expanded my understanding of myself

00:01:43 --> 00:01:48
and of the beautiful world I'm fortunate to exist in

00:01:48 --> 00:01:51
with all of you.

00:01:50 --> 00:01:53
So I'm glad I went

00:01:52 --> 00:01:57
and I'm glad I made it out alive.

00:01:57 --> 00:02:00
This is a Lex Fridman podcast.

00:01:59 --> 00:02:02
To support it, please check out our sponsors

00:02:01 --> 00:02:03
in the description.

00:02:02 --> 00:02:07
And now, dear friends, here's Paul Rosolie.

00:02:07 --> 00:02:10
I can't believe we're actually here.

00:02:09 --> 00:02:11
- I can't believe you actually came.

00:02:10 --> 00:02:13
- And I can't believe you forced me to wear a suit.

00:02:12 --> 00:02:14
(Paul laughs)

00:02:13 --> 00:02:16
- That was the people's choice, trust me.

00:02:15 --> 00:02:18
- All right, we've been through

00:02:17 --> 00:02:20
quite a lot over the last few days.

00:02:19 --> 00:02:22
- We've been through a bit.

00:02:21 --> 00:02:24
- Let me ask you a ridiculous question.

00:02:24 --> 00:02:27
What of all the creatures right now

00:02:26 --> 00:02:31
if they wanted to, could cause us harm?

00:02:30 --> 00:02:34
- The thing is, the Amazon rainforest has been described

00:02:33 --> 00:02:36
as the greatest natural battlefield on Earth

00:02:35 --> 00:02:39
because there's more life here than anywhere else,

00:02:38 --> 00:02:42
which means that everything here is fighting for survival.

00:02:41 --> 00:02:44
The trees are fighting for sunlight,

00:02:43 --> 00:02:46
the animals are fighting for prey.

00:02:45 --> 00:02:47
Everybody's fighting for survival.

00:02:46 --> 00:02:49
And so everything that you see here,

00:02:48 --> 00:02:51
everything around us will be killed, eaten,

00:02:50 --> 00:02:53
digested, recycled at some point.

00:02:52 --> 00:02:57
The jungle is really just a giant churning machine of death.

00:02:56 --> 00:03:00
And life is kind of this moment of stasis

00:02:59 --> 00:03:02
where you maintain this collection of cells

00:03:01 --> 00:03:04
in a particular DNA sequence

00:03:03 --> 00:03:07
and then it gets digested again and recycled back

00:03:06 --> 00:03:09
and renamed into everything.

00:03:09 --> 00:03:13
And so the things in this forest,

00:03:12 --> 00:03:15
while they don't want to hurt us,

00:03:14 --> 00:03:17
there are things that are heavily defended

00:03:16 --> 00:03:19
because, for instance, a giant anteater needs

00:03:18 --> 00:03:21
claws to fight off a jaguar.

00:03:20 --> 00:03:24
A stingray needs a stinger on its tail,

00:03:23 --> 00:03:27
which is basically a serrated knife with venom on it

00:03:26 --> 00:03:29
o deter anything that would hunt that stingray.

00:03:28 --> 00:03:31
Even the catfish have pectoral fins

00:03:30 --> 00:03:36
that have razor, long steak knife-sized defense systems.

00:03:36 --> 00:03:38
Then you have of course the jaguars,

00:03:37 --> 00:03:40
the harpy eagles, the piranha,

00:03:39 --> 00:03:42
the candiru fish that can swim up a penis,

00:03:41 --> 00:03:43
lodge themselves inside.

00:03:42 --> 00:03:45
It's the Amazon rainforest.

00:03:44 --> 00:03:48
The thing is, as you've learned this week,

00:03:47 --> 00:03:50
nothing here wants to get us,

00:03:49 --> 00:03:52
with the exception of maybe mosquitoes.

00:03:52 --> 00:03:55
Every other animal just wants to eat

00:03:54 --> 00:03:57
and exist in peace.

00:03:56 --> 00:03:58
That's it.

00:03:57 --> 00:04:01
- But there is, each of those animals, like you described,

00:04:00 --> 00:04:04
have a kind of radius of defense.

00:04:03 --> 00:04:05
- [Paul] Yeah. - So if you

00:04:04 --> 00:04:09
accidentally step into its home.

00:04:08 --> 00:04:11
- [Paul] Yeah. - Into that radius,

00:04:10 --> 00:04:11
it can cause harm.

00:04:10 --> 00:04:13
- Or make 'em feel threatened.

00:04:12 --> 00:04:14
- Make 'em feel threatened.

00:04:13 --> 00:04:16
There is a defense mechanism that is activated.

00:04:15 --> 00:04:18
- Some incredible defense mechanisms.

00:04:17 --> 00:04:22
I mean, you're talking about 17-foot black caiman crocodiles

00:04:21 --> 00:04:25
that with significant size that could rip you in half.

00:04:24 --> 00:04:27
Anacondas, the largest snake on Earth.

00:04:26 --> 00:04:30
Bushmasters that can grow up to be nine

00:04:29 --> 00:04:31
to I think even 11 feet long.

00:04:30 --> 00:04:34
And I've caught bushmasters that are thicker than my arms.

00:04:33 --> 00:04:37
- So for people who don't know bushmaster snakes,

00:04:36 --> 00:04:37
what are these things?

00:04:36 --> 00:04:38
- These are vipers.

00:04:37 --> 00:04:41
It's the, I believe it's the largest viper on Earth.

00:04:40 --> 00:04:43
- Venomous? - Extremely venomous

00:04:42 --> 00:04:44
with hinged teeth,

00:04:43 --> 00:04:46
tissue-destroying venom.

00:04:45 --> 00:04:48
Like, if you get bitten by a bushmaster,

00:04:47 --> 00:04:50
they say you don't rush

00:04:49 --> 00:04:51
and try and save your own life.

00:04:50 --> 00:04:53
You try to savor what's around you.

00:04:52 --> 00:04:54
Look at, look around at the world,

00:04:53 --> 00:04:58
smoke your last cigarette, call your mom, that's it.

00:04:57 --> 00:05:00
- So that moment of stasis that is life

00:04:59 --> 00:05:03
is going to end abruptly when you interact

00:05:02 --> 00:05:03
with one of those.

00:05:02 --> 00:05:05
- Yeah, I even have,

00:05:04 --> 00:05:08
even this seemingly.

00:05:07 --> 00:05:11
- Can I just pause at how incredibly beautiful it is

00:05:10 --> 00:05:13
that you could just reach to your right

00:05:12 --> 00:05:14
and grab a piece of the jungle.

00:05:13 --> 00:05:15
(Lex and Paul laugh)

00:05:14 --> 00:05:15
- It's like a,

00:05:14 --> 00:05:18
even this seemingly beautiful little fern,

00:05:17 --> 00:05:20
if you go this way on the fern, you're fine.

00:05:19 --> 00:05:21
As soon as, ow, as soon as you go this way,

00:05:20 --> 00:05:24
there's invisible, little spikes on there if you wanna.

00:05:24 --> 00:05:27
- Oh, I see, I feel that.

00:05:26 --> 00:05:28
- Yeah, see that?

00:05:27 --> 00:05:29
It's like everything is defended.

00:05:28 --> 00:05:29
If you're driving on the road

00:05:28 --> 00:05:30
and you have your arm out the side,

00:05:29 --> 00:05:32
or if you're on a motorcycle going through the jungle

00:05:31 --> 00:05:33
and you get one of these,

00:05:32 --> 00:05:36
it'll just tear all the skin right off your body.

00:05:35 --> 00:05:38
It's kind of doing that to me now.

00:05:37 --> 00:05:39
- So what would you do?

00:05:38 --> 00:05:43
Like, we were going through the dense jungle yesterday

00:05:44 --> 00:05:47
and you slide down the hill, your foot slips,

00:05:46 --> 00:05:48
you're sliding down. - [Paul] Yeah.

00:05:47 --> 00:05:50
- And then you find yourself staring

00:05:49 --> 00:05:51
a couple feet away from a bushmaster snake.

00:05:50 --> 00:05:53
What are you doing?

00:05:52 --> 00:05:56
You're, for people who somehow don't know,

00:05:55 --> 00:05:58
are somebody who loves, admires snakes,

00:05:57 --> 00:06:01
who has met thousands of snakes,

00:06:00 --> 00:06:04
has worked with them, respects them,

00:06:03 --> 00:06:05
celebrates them.

00:06:04 --> 00:06:08
What would you do with a bushmaster snake face to face?

00:06:07 --> 00:06:10
- Face to face, this has happened,

00:06:10 --> 00:06:12
that I've been there.

00:06:11 --> 00:06:13
- It's nice.

00:06:12 --> 00:06:15
- I've come face to face with a bushmaster

00:06:14 --> 00:06:17
and there's two things,

00:06:16 --> 00:06:18
there's two reactions that you might get.

00:06:17 --> 00:06:21
One is if the bushmaster decides that it's vacation time,

00:06:20 --> 00:06:23
if it's sleeping, if he just had a meal,

00:06:22 --> 00:06:25
they'll come to the edges of trails

00:06:24 --> 00:06:27
or beneath a tree and they'll just circle up,

00:06:26 --> 00:06:29
little spiral, big spiral,

00:06:28 --> 00:06:31
big pile of snake on the trail and they'll just sit there.

00:06:30 --> 00:06:33
And one time there was a snake sitting

00:06:32 --> 00:06:36
on the side of a trail beneath a tree for two weeks.

00:06:35 --> 00:06:38
This snake was just sitting there resting,

00:06:37 --> 00:06:40
digesting its food out in the open, in the rain,

00:06:39 --> 00:06:42
in the sun, in the night.

00:06:41 --> 00:06:45
Didn't matter, you go near it, barely even cracked a tongue.

00:06:46 --> 00:06:49
Now the other option is that you get a bushmaster

00:06:48 --> 00:06:51
that's alert and hunting

00:06:50 --> 00:06:53
and out looking for something to eat

00:06:52 --> 00:06:54
and they're ready to defend themselves.

00:06:53 --> 00:06:56
And so I once came across a bushmaster

00:06:55 --> 00:06:57
in the jungle at night,

00:06:56 --> 00:07:01
and this bushmaster turned its head towards me,

00:07:00 --> 00:07:04
looked at me and made it very clear,

00:07:03 --> 00:07:04
I'm gonna go this way.

00:07:03 --> 00:07:06
And so I did the natural thing

00:07:05 --> 00:07:07
that any snake enthusiast would do,

00:07:06 --> 00:07:09
and I grabbed its tail.

00:07:08 --> 00:07:10
Now, 11 feet later by the head,

00:07:09 --> 00:07:12
the snake turned around and just said

00:07:11 --> 00:07:14
if you wanna meet God, I can arrange the meeting.

00:07:13 --> 00:07:15
I will oblige.

00:07:16 --> 00:07:19
And I decided to let the bushmaster go.

00:07:18 --> 00:07:21
And so it's like that with most animals.

00:07:20 --> 00:07:23
You know, a jaguar will turn and look at you

00:07:22 --> 00:07:24
and just remind you of how small you are.

00:07:23 --> 00:07:27
- Like, what did you see in the snake's eyes?

00:07:26 --> 00:07:29
What, how did you sense that this is not the right,

00:07:28 --> 00:07:33
this is not, this is gonna be your end if you proceed?

00:07:32 --> 00:07:34
- His readiness.

00:07:33 --> 00:07:36
I wanted to get him by the tail

00:07:35 --> 00:07:37
and show him to the people that were there

00:07:36 --> 00:07:39
and maybe work with the snake a little bit.

00:07:38 --> 00:07:42
As an 11-foot snake, he, the snake turned around

00:07:41 --> 00:07:45
and made it very clear, like not today, pal.

00:07:44 --> 00:07:46
It's not gonna happen.

00:07:45 --> 00:07:46
- It was in the eyes and the movement

00:07:45 --> 00:07:48
and the tension of the body?

00:07:47 --> 00:07:48
- It was the movement.

00:07:47 --> 00:07:50
It was the movement and the S of the neck.

00:07:49 --> 00:07:52
It was as if you pushed me and I went,

00:07:51 --> 00:07:54
"Let's go, make my day." - [Lex] Yeah.

00:07:53 --> 00:07:56
- Like, he just looked a little bit too.

00:07:55 --> 00:07:57
- [Lex] Yeah. - Too ready.

00:07:56 --> 00:07:58
(Lex laughs)

00:07:57 --> 00:07:59
He's like, I love this.

00:07:58 --> 00:08:00
- [Lex] Okay, all right, so you know.

00:07:59 --> 00:08:01
- You just know, you just know.

00:08:00 --> 00:08:04
Whereas like the snake you met last night.

00:08:03 --> 00:08:05
- [Lex] Yeah, beautiful snake.

00:08:04 --> 00:08:06
- Such a calm little thing.

00:08:05 --> 00:08:08
He just focuses on eating baby lizards

00:08:07 --> 00:08:10
and little snails and things.

00:08:09 --> 00:08:12
And that snake has no concept of defending itself.

00:08:11 --> 00:08:13
It has no way to defend itself.

00:08:12 --> 00:08:16
So even a, even something the size of a blue jay

00:08:15 --> 00:08:18
could just come and just peck that thing in the head

00:08:17 --> 00:08:19
and swallow it.

00:08:18 --> 00:08:20
And it's a helpless, little snake.

00:08:19 --> 00:08:23
So it's really, it kind of depends on the animal.

00:08:22 --> 00:08:24
It depends on the mood you catch 'em in.

00:08:23 --> 00:08:26
Each one has a different temperament.

00:08:25 --> 00:08:29
- The grace of its movement was mesmerizing, curious almost.

00:08:28 --> 00:08:33
Maybe I'm anthropomorphizing, projecting onto it,

00:08:32 --> 00:08:34
but it was. - The tongue flicking

00:08:33 --> 00:08:35
was a sign of curiosity.

00:08:34 --> 00:08:36
It was trying to figure out what was going on.

00:08:35 --> 00:08:38
It was like, why am I on this treadmill of human skin,

00:08:37 --> 00:08:39
you know?

00:08:38 --> 00:08:41
They're just trying to get to the next thing.

00:08:40 --> 00:08:43
Trying to get hidden, trying to get away from the light.

00:08:42 --> 00:08:45
- Also, the texture of the scales is really fascinating.

00:08:44 --> 00:08:47
I mean, it's my first. - Oh, that's right.

00:08:46 --> 00:08:48
- The first snake I've ever touched is so interesting.

00:08:47 --> 00:08:51
It was just such an incredible system

00:08:50 --> 00:08:54
of muscles that are all interacting together

00:08:53 --> 00:08:56
to make that kind of movement work

00:08:55 --> 00:08:59
and all the texture of its skin, of its scales.

00:08:58 --> 00:09:01
What do you love about snakes?

00:09:00 --> 00:09:03
From my first experience with a snake

00:09:02 --> 00:09:06
to all the thousands of experiences you had with snakes,

00:09:05 --> 00:09:08
what do you love about these creatures?

00:09:07 --> 00:09:12
- I think it's, when you just spoke about it it was,

00:09:11 --> 00:09:14
that's the first snake you've met.

00:09:13 --> 00:09:15
And it was a tiny, little snake in the jungle

00:09:14 --> 00:09:18
and you spoke about it with so much light in your eyes.

00:09:17 --> 00:09:20
And I think that because we've been programmed

00:09:19 --> 00:09:22
to be scared of snakes,

00:09:21 --> 00:09:25
there's something wondrous that happens in our brain.

00:09:24 --> 00:09:28
Maybe it's just this joy of discovery,

00:09:27 --> 00:09:30
that there's nothing to be scared of.

00:09:29 --> 00:09:32
And whether it's a rattlesnake that is dangerous

00:09:31 --> 00:09:33
and that you need to give distance to,

00:09:32 --> 00:09:36
but you look at it from a distance and you go, whoa.

00:09:35 --> 00:09:38
Or it's a harmless little grass snake

00:09:37 --> 00:09:41
that you can pick up and enjoy and give to a child.

00:09:40 --> 00:09:44
It's, they're just these strange, legless animals

00:09:43 --> 00:09:46
that just exist.

00:09:45 --> 00:09:47
You know, they don't even have eyelids.

00:09:46 --> 00:09:48
They're so different than us.

00:09:47 --> 00:09:50
They have a tongue that senses the air

00:09:49 --> 00:09:54
and they, to me, are so beautiful.

00:09:53 --> 00:09:57
And I've my whole life been defending snakes from humans.

00:09:56 --> 00:09:59
And it's, they seem misunderstood.

00:09:58 --> 00:10:01
I think they're incredibly beautiful.

00:10:00 --> 00:10:04
There's every color and variety of snakes.

00:10:03 --> 00:10:06
There's venomous snakes, there's tree snakes,

00:10:05 --> 00:10:07
there's huge, crushing anacondas.

00:10:06 --> 00:10:12
It's just, of the 2,600 species of snakes

00:10:11 --> 00:10:13
that exist on Earth,

00:10:12 --> 00:10:16
there's just such beauty, such complexity

00:10:15 --> 00:10:18
and such simplicity.

00:10:17 --> 00:10:21
They're just, to me, I'm,

00:10:20 --> 00:10:24
I feel like I'm friend with snake

00:10:23 --> 00:10:27
and they rely on me to protect them from my people.

00:10:27 --> 00:10:29
- Friend with snake.

00:10:28 --> 00:10:30
- Me friend snake.

00:10:31 --> 00:10:34
You said some of them are sometimes aggressive,

00:10:33 --> 00:10:36
some of them are peaceful.

00:10:35 --> 00:10:39
Is this a mood thing, a personality thing, a species thing?

00:10:38 --> 00:10:40
Is it, what is it?

00:10:39 --> 00:10:41
- So the, as far as I know,

00:10:40 --> 00:10:43
there's only really two snakes on Earth

00:10:42 --> 00:10:44
at could be aggressive

00:10:43 --> 00:10:48
'cause aggression indicates offense.

00:10:47 --> 00:10:50
And so a reticulated python has been documented

00:10:49 --> 00:10:51
as eating humans.

00:10:50 --> 00:10:54
Anacondas, although while it hasn't been publicized,

00:10:53 --> 00:10:55
they have eaten humans.

00:10:55 --> 00:11:01
Every single other snake from boa constrictor to bushmaster,

00:11:00 --> 00:11:03
to spitting cobra to grass snake

00:11:02 --> 00:11:04
to garter snake to everything else,

00:11:03 --> 00:11:08
every single other snake does not want to interact with you.

00:11:08 --> 00:11:10
They have no interest.

00:11:09 --> 00:11:12
So there's no such thing as an aggressive snake

00:11:11 --> 00:11:14
once you get outside of anaconda and reticulated python.

00:11:13 --> 00:11:16
Aggression could be trying to eat you, that's predation.

00:11:15 --> 00:11:20
But for every other snake, a rattlesnake,

00:11:19 --> 00:11:23
if it was there, would either go escape and hide itself,

00:11:22 --> 00:11:26
or it would rattle its tail and tell us don't come closer.

00:11:25 --> 00:11:28
A cobra will hood up and begin to hiss and say,

00:11:27 --> 00:11:29
"Don't approach me.

00:11:28 --> 00:11:33
I'm asking you nicely not to mess with me."

00:11:32 --> 00:11:35
And most other snakes are fast or they stay in the trees

00:11:34 --> 00:11:36
or they're extremely camouflaged.

00:11:35 --> 00:11:39
But their whole MO is just don't bother me.

00:11:38 --> 00:11:40
I don't wanna be seen, I don't wanna be messed with.

00:11:39 --> 00:11:43
In fact, all I wanna do is be left alone.

00:11:42 --> 00:11:45
And once in a while, I just want to eat.

00:11:44 --> 00:11:48
And by the way, when you see a snake drink,

00:11:47 --> 00:11:50
your heart will break.

00:11:49 --> 00:11:51
It's like seeing, it's the only thing

00:11:50 --> 00:11:53
that's cuter than a puppy.

00:11:52 --> 00:11:56
Like, watching a snake touch its mouth to water

00:11:55 --> 00:11:58
and just, you just see that little mouth going

00:11:57 --> 00:11:59
as they suck water in.

00:11:58 --> 00:12:01
And it's like, it's just so adorable,

00:12:00 --> 00:12:04
watching this scaled animal just be like, I need water.

00:12:03 --> 00:12:07
- In a state of vulnerability.

00:12:06 --> 00:12:09
- [Paul] Yeah, yeah. - Bro, there's nothing cuter

00:12:08 --> 00:12:10
than a little puppy with a tongue like.

00:12:09 --> 00:12:11
(Lex slurps tongue)

00:12:10 --> 00:12:12
- Baby ball python. - [Lex] Right.

00:12:11 --> 00:12:13
- Baby king cobra, man, come on.

00:12:12 --> 00:12:15
- I'll have to take your word for it.

00:12:14 --> 00:12:15
(Lex laughs)

00:12:14 --> 00:12:16
- [Paul] Baby elephant.

00:12:15 --> 00:12:17
- So what are,

00:12:16 --> 00:12:18
they're like at a puddle and they just take it in?

00:12:17 --> 00:12:20
- They can be at a puddle and they just take it in.

00:12:19 --> 00:12:22
Or one time in India, I was with a snake rescuer

00:12:21 --> 00:12:25
and we found this nine-foot king cobra,

00:12:24 --> 00:12:26
this god of a snake.

00:12:25 --> 00:12:28
They're Ophiophagus hannah is their Latin name,

00:12:27 --> 00:12:30
and they're snake eaters.

00:12:29 --> 00:12:33
They're the king of the snakes, the largest venomous snake.

00:12:32 --> 00:12:37
And the people that called the snake rescuer,

00:12:36 --> 00:12:39
'cause that's a profession in India,

00:12:40 --> 00:12:42
you know, it had gotten into their kitchen

00:12:41 --> 00:12:43
or their backyard.

00:12:42 --> 00:12:45
And so we showed up and we got the snake.

00:12:44 --> 00:12:47
And the snake rescuer, he knew,

00:12:46 --> 00:12:49
he looked at the snake and he went to me, he said,

00:12:48 --> 00:12:50
"You know, why do you think this snake would go in a house?"

00:12:49 --> 00:12:52
And he was quizzing me.

00:12:51 --> 00:12:54
And I actually went, "You know, I don't know,

00:12:53 --> 00:12:55
is it warm, is it cold?"

00:12:54 --> 00:12:57
You know, like sometimes cats like to go into

00:12:56 --> 00:12:58
the warm cars in the winter.

00:12:57 --> 00:13:01
And he was like, "He's thirsty."

00:13:00 --> 00:13:02
He goes, "Watch this."

00:13:01 --> 00:13:05
He took a water bottle, poured it over the,

00:13:04 --> 00:13:06
now the snake is standing up.

00:13:05 --> 00:13:07
The snake stands up three feet tall.

00:13:06 --> 00:13:09
This is a huge king cobra with a hood,

00:13:08 --> 00:13:10
terrifying snake to be around.

00:13:09 --> 00:13:12
He leans over to the snake

00:13:11 --> 00:13:14
and the snake is standing there trusting him.

00:13:13 --> 00:13:16
And he takes a water bottle

00:13:15 --> 00:13:17
and pours it onto the snake's nose.

00:13:16 --> 00:13:19
And the snake turns up its nose

00:13:18 --> 00:13:21
and just starts drinking from the water bottle.

00:13:20 --> 00:13:25
Human giving water to snake, big, scary snake.

00:13:24 --> 00:13:28
But this human understood snake gets water,

00:13:27 --> 00:13:31
snake gets released in jungle, everybody's okay.

00:13:30 --> 00:13:34
- So sometimes the needs are simple,

00:13:33 --> 00:13:35
they just don't have the words

00:13:34 --> 00:13:37
to communicate them to us humans.

00:13:36 --> 00:13:38
- Yeah.

00:13:37 --> 00:13:40
- And is it disinterest or is it fear,

00:13:39 --> 00:13:42
almost like they don't notice us?

00:13:41 --> 00:13:44
Or is it we're a source,

00:13:43 --> 00:13:46
the unknown aspect of it,

00:13:45 --> 00:13:49
the uncertainty is a source of danger?

00:13:48 --> 00:13:52
- Well, animals live in a constant state of danger.

00:13:51 --> 00:13:54
Like if you look at that deer that we saw last night,

00:13:53 --> 00:13:56
it's stalking through the jungle,

00:13:55 --> 00:13:57
wondering what's going to eat it,

00:13:56 --> 00:13:59
Wondering if this is the last moment it's gonna be alive.

00:13:58 --> 00:14:02
It's like animals are constantly terrified of

00:14:01 --> 00:14:03
that this is their last moment.

00:14:02 --> 00:14:05
- Yeah, just for the listener,

00:14:04 --> 00:14:07
we're walking through the jungle late at night,

00:14:06 --> 00:14:11
so it's darkness except our headlamps on.

00:14:10 --> 00:14:13
And then all of a sudden, Paul stops.

00:14:12 --> 00:14:15
He's like, "Shh,"

00:14:14 --> 00:14:17
and he looks in the distance and he sees two eyes.

00:14:16 --> 00:14:20
He's, I think you thought is that a jaguar

00:14:19 --> 00:14:21
or is that a deer?

00:14:20 --> 00:14:24
And it was moving its head like this,

00:14:23 --> 00:14:27
like scared or maybe trying to figure it,

00:14:26 --> 00:14:29
trying to localize itself, trying to figure out.

00:14:28 --> 00:14:30
- Trying to see around you.

00:14:29 --> 00:14:31
- You're doing the same to it,

00:14:30 --> 00:14:33
the two of you like moving your head.

00:14:32 --> 00:14:35
- [Paul] Yeah. - And like deep

00:14:34 --> 00:14:38
into the jungle, like, I don't know.

00:14:37 --> 00:14:39
It's pretty far away through the trees

00:14:38 --> 00:14:40
you could still see it.

00:14:39 --> 00:14:42
It was fascinating. - 30 feet or so, yeah.

00:14:41 --> 00:14:43
- That's the thing to actually mention.

00:14:42 --> 00:14:45
I mean the, with headlamp you see

00:14:44 --> 00:14:47
the reflection in their eyes.

00:14:46 --> 00:14:48
It's kind of incredible. - [Paul] Yes.

00:14:47 --> 00:14:49
- Just to see a creature,

00:14:48 --> 00:14:51
to try to identify a creature

00:14:50 --> 00:14:53
by just the reflection from its eyes.

00:14:52 --> 00:14:54
- Yeah, and so the cats,

00:14:53 --> 00:14:57
sometimes you'll get like a greenish or a bluish glow

00:14:56 --> 00:14:58
from the cats.

00:14:57 --> 00:15:01
The deer are usually white to orange,

00:15:00 --> 00:15:04
caiman, orange, nightjars, orange,

00:15:03 --> 00:15:05
snakes can usually be like orange,

00:15:04 --> 00:15:08
moths, spiders sparkle.

00:15:07 --> 00:15:09
And so you have all these different,

00:15:08 --> 00:15:11
as you walk through the jungle,

00:15:10 --> 00:15:13
you can see all these different eyes.

00:15:12 --> 00:15:16
And when something large looks at you like that deer did,

00:15:15 --> 00:15:18
your first thing is what animal is this

00:15:17 --> 00:15:21
that I am staring back at?

00:15:20 --> 00:15:23
'Cause through the light you kind of get,

00:15:22 --> 00:15:26
you see the reflection off the bright light off the leaves.

00:15:25 --> 00:15:28
And I couldn't tell at first 'cause I actually,

00:15:27 --> 00:15:30
the, those big, bright eyes, it could have been an ocelot,

00:15:29 --> 00:15:32
it could have been a jaguar, it could have been a deer.

00:15:31 --> 00:15:34
And then when it did this movement,

00:15:33 --> 00:15:35
that's what the cats do.

00:15:34 --> 00:15:37
They try to see around your light.

00:15:37 --> 00:15:40
I thought maybe Lex Fridman's here, we're gonna get lucky.

00:15:39 --> 00:15:42
It's gonna be a jag right off trail.

00:15:41 --> 00:15:44
- Your definition of lucky is a complicated one.

00:15:43 --> 00:15:47
- Yeah. - That's a fascinating process

00:15:46 --> 00:15:49
when you see those two eyes try to figure out what it is.

00:15:48 --> 00:15:53
And it is trying to figure out what you are in that process.

00:15:52 --> 00:15:54
Let's talk about caiman. - [Paul] Sure.

00:15:53 --> 00:15:56
- We've seen a lot of different kinds of size.

00:15:55 --> 00:15:58
We've seen a baby one, a bigger one.

00:15:57 --> 00:16:02
Tell me about these 16-foot plus apex predators

00:16:01 --> 00:16:04
of the Amazon rainforest.

00:16:03 --> 00:16:07
- The big, bad, black caiman,

00:16:06 --> 00:16:11
which is the largest reptilian predator in the Amazon

00:16:10 --> 00:16:12
except for the anaconda,

00:16:11 --> 00:16:15
they kind of both share that notch of apex predator.

00:16:15 --> 00:16:19
They were actually hunted to endangered species level

00:16:18 --> 00:16:22
in the '70s 'cause their leather,

00:16:21 --> 00:16:23
black, scale leather.

00:16:23 --> 00:16:26
But they're coming back.

00:16:25 --> 00:16:28
They're coming back and they're huge

00:16:27 --> 00:16:29
and they're beautiful.

00:16:28 --> 00:16:32
And I was walking near a lake and I never understood

00:16:31 --> 00:16:34
how big they could get except for I was walking

00:16:33 --> 00:16:37
near a lake last year and I was following the stream,

00:16:36 --> 00:16:38
you know, and it's like when you're following

00:16:37 --> 00:16:40
a little stream and there's just a little trickle of water,

00:16:39 --> 00:16:41
and all of a sudden this river otter had been running

00:16:40 --> 00:16:43
the other direction on the tree, on the stream.

00:16:42 --> 00:16:45
River otter comes up to me and I swear to God,

00:16:44 --> 00:16:47
this animal looked at me and went, "Hey."

00:16:46 --> 00:16:48
And I went, "Hey."

00:16:47 --> 00:16:49
He was like, didn't expect to see me there.

00:16:48 --> 00:16:51
And he turned around, he like did a little spin,

00:16:50 --> 00:16:53
started running down the stream.

00:16:52 --> 00:16:54
Then he turned around and he, you could tell,

00:16:53 --> 00:16:55
he was like, "Let's go."

00:16:54 --> 00:16:57
And I, you know, I'm not anthropomorphizing here.

00:16:57 --> 00:17:00
The animal was asking me to come with him.

00:16:59 --> 00:17:01
So I followed the river otter down the stream.

00:17:00 --> 00:17:02
We started running down the stream

00:17:01 --> 00:17:04
and the river otter looks at me one more time,

00:17:03 --> 00:17:06
is like, "Yo", jumps into the lake.

00:17:05 --> 00:17:08
And I'm like, what does he want me to see?

00:17:07 --> 00:17:11
Now in the lake, this river otter is doing dives

00:17:10 --> 00:17:13
and freaking out and going up and down and up and down

00:17:12 --> 00:17:14
and they're very excited.

00:17:13 --> 00:17:16
They're screaming, they're screeching.

00:17:16 --> 00:17:20
All of a sudden, and I've never seen anything like this

00:17:19 --> 00:17:22
except for in like "Game of Thrones",

00:17:21 --> 00:17:24
this croc head comes flying out of the water.

00:17:23 --> 00:17:27
All of the river otters were attacking

00:17:26 --> 00:17:30
this huge, black caiman, 16 feet.

00:17:29 --> 00:17:31
- [Lex] Wow. - Head half the size

00:17:30 --> 00:17:32
of this table.

00:17:31 --> 00:17:34
And she was thrashing her tail around,

00:17:33 --> 00:17:36
creating these huge waves in the water,

00:17:35 --> 00:17:39
trying to catch an otter, and they're so fast.

00:17:38 --> 00:17:40
- [Lex] Yeah. - That they were

00:17:39 --> 00:17:42
zipping around her, biting her and then going around.

00:17:41 --> 00:17:44
And this otter, swear to God, interspecies,

00:17:43 --> 00:17:46
looked at me and went, "Watch this."

00:17:45 --> 00:17:47
(Lex laughs)

00:17:46 --> 00:17:47
We're fucking with this caiman.

00:17:46 --> 00:17:49
- [Lex] Yeah. - It was amazing.

00:17:48 --> 00:17:50
And I, for the first time,

00:17:49 --> 00:17:52
I got to stand there watching this incredible

00:17:51 --> 00:17:54
interspecies fight happening.

00:17:53 --> 00:17:55
They weren't trying to kill the caiman,

00:17:54 --> 00:17:57
they were just trying to mess with it.

00:17:56 --> 00:17:59
And the caiman was doing his best

00:17:58 --> 00:18:01
o try and kill these otters.

00:18:00 --> 00:18:02
And they were just having a good time

00:18:01 --> 00:18:06
in that sick, sort of hyper-intelligent animal,

00:18:05 --> 00:18:07
like wolf sort of way where they were just going,

00:18:06 --> 00:18:08
you can't catch us.

00:18:07 --> 00:18:10
- Yeah, like intelligence and agility

00:18:09 --> 00:18:13
versus like raw power and dominance.

00:18:12 --> 00:18:17
I mean, I got to handle some smaller caiman

00:18:16 --> 00:18:19
d just the power they had.

00:18:19 --> 00:18:22
You know, you scale that up to imagine

00:18:21 --> 00:18:25
what a 16-foot, even a 10-foot,

00:18:24 --> 00:18:26
any kind of black caiman,

00:18:25 --> 00:18:28
the kind of power they deliver.

00:18:27 --> 00:18:29
Maybe can you talk to that?

00:18:28 --> 00:18:33
Like, the power they can generate with their tail,

00:18:32 --> 00:18:35
with their neck, with their jaw.

00:18:34 --> 00:18:37
- Yeah, alligators and caiman and crocodiles have

00:18:36 --> 00:18:39
some of the strongest bite forces on Earth.

00:18:38 --> 00:18:41
Think a saltwater crocodile wins

00:18:40 --> 00:18:43
as the strongest bite force on Earth.

00:18:42 --> 00:18:47
And you got to hold about a foot,

00:18:46 --> 00:18:49
was it a four-foot spectacled caiman?

00:18:48 --> 00:18:51
And you got to feel, I mean,

00:18:50 --> 00:18:54
you're a black belt in jiu-jitsu.

00:18:53 --> 00:18:57
How do you compare the explosive force you felt

00:18:56 --> 00:19:00
from that animal compared to what a human can generate?

00:19:00 --> 00:19:04
- It's difficult to describe in words.

00:19:03 --> 00:19:05
There's a lot of power.

00:19:04 --> 00:19:07
And we're talking about the power of the neck,

00:19:06 --> 00:19:09
like the, what is it?

00:19:08 --> 00:19:10
I mean, there's a lot.

00:19:09 --> 00:19:11
It could generate power all up and down the body.

00:19:10 --> 00:19:14
So probably the tail is a monster,

00:19:13 --> 00:19:17
but just the neck and, you know,

00:19:16 --> 00:19:21
not to mention the power of the bite, that,

00:19:20 --> 00:19:22
and the speed too.

00:19:21 --> 00:19:25
Because the thing I saw and got to experience is

00:19:24 --> 00:19:27
how still and calm,

00:19:26 --> 00:19:28
at least from my amateur perspective.

00:19:27 --> 00:19:30
- [Paul] Yeah. - It seems calm,

00:19:31 --> 00:19:33
still.

00:19:32 --> 00:19:37
And then from that sort of zero to 60 could just go wild.

00:19:36 --> 00:19:38
- Just thrashing.

00:19:38 --> 00:19:42
- And then there's also a decision it makes

00:19:41 --> 00:19:45
in that split second whether, as it thrashes,

00:19:44 --> 00:19:49
is it going to kinda bite you on the way or not?

00:19:48 --> 00:19:51
- Hmm and that's where,

00:19:50 --> 00:19:54
that's where of the four species of caiman

00:19:53 --> 00:19:54
that we have here,

00:19:53 --> 00:19:57
you see differences in their personalities as a species.

00:19:56 --> 00:19:58
- [Lex] Yeah. - And so you can like,

00:19:57 --> 00:20:00
just like you know like generally,

00:19:59 --> 00:20:03
Golden Retrievers are viewed as a friendly dog, generally.

00:20:02 --> 00:20:05
Not every single one of them, but as a rule.

00:20:05 --> 00:20:10
Spectacled caiman, puppies.

00:20:09 --> 00:20:12
You released one in the river and it did nothing.

00:20:11 --> 00:20:14
It didn't bite one of your fingers, it just swam away.

00:20:15 --> 00:20:18
We dropped one in the river and what did it do?

00:20:17 --> 00:20:19
It chose peace.

00:20:18 --> 00:20:21
Now I had a smooth-fronted caiman a few weeks ago,

00:20:20 --> 00:20:24
and this was probably about a three and a half footer.

00:20:23 --> 00:20:25
Not big enough to kill you,

00:20:24 --> 00:20:27
but very much big enough to grab one of your fingers

00:20:26 --> 00:20:28
and just shake it off your body,

00:20:27 --> 00:20:30
just death roll it right off.

00:20:29 --> 00:20:32
And as I was being careful,

00:20:31 --> 00:20:34
totally different caiman than the one that you got to see.

00:20:33 --> 00:20:35
This one has spikes coming off it.

00:20:34 --> 00:20:38
They're like leftover dinosaurs.

00:20:37 --> 00:20:42
It's like they evolved during the dinosaur times

00:20:41 --> 00:20:42
and never changed.

00:20:41 --> 00:20:44
They have spikes and bony plates

00:20:43 --> 00:20:47
and all kinds of strange growths that you don't see

00:20:46 --> 00:20:48
on the other, smoother caiman.

00:20:47 --> 00:20:51
And I tried to release this one without getting bitten

00:20:50 --> 00:20:54
and I threw it into the stream, gently into the water,

00:20:53 --> 00:20:56
just went wow and tried to pull my hands back.

00:20:55 --> 00:20:58
And as I pulled my hand back,

00:20:57 --> 00:21:00
this caiman in the air turned around

00:20:59 --> 00:21:02
and just tried to gimme one parting blow

00:21:01 --> 00:21:03
and just got one tooth, whack,

00:21:02 --> 00:21:05
right to the bone of my finger.

00:21:04 --> 00:21:09
And bone injury feels different than a skin injury.

00:21:08 --> 00:21:13
So you instantly, and it just reminds you of,

00:21:12 --> 00:21:15
that's a caiman with a head this big.

00:21:14 --> 00:21:16
And it hurt.

00:21:15 --> 00:21:18
And I know that it could have taken off my finger.

00:21:17 --> 00:21:20
Now if you scale that up to a black caiman,

00:21:20 --> 00:21:23
it's rib crushing.

00:21:22 --> 00:21:25
It's zebra head-removing size, you know?

00:21:24 --> 00:21:28
Just meat destroying.

00:21:27 --> 00:21:31
It's nature's metal sort of, you know,

00:21:30 --> 00:21:33
just raw power.

00:21:32 --> 00:21:37
- So what's the biggest croc you've been able to handle?

00:21:36 --> 00:21:39
- We were doing caiman surveys for years

00:21:38 --> 00:21:41
and we would go out at night and you want to figure out

00:21:40 --> 00:21:44
what are the populations of black caiman, spectacled caiman,

00:21:43 --> 00:21:45
smooth-fronted caiman, dwarf caiman.

00:21:44 --> 00:21:49
And the only way to see which caiman you're dealing with

00:21:48 --> 00:21:50
is to catch it 'cause a lot of times,

00:21:49 --> 00:21:51
you get up close with the light

00:21:50 --> 00:21:52
and you can see the eyes at night,

00:21:51 --> 00:21:55
but you can't quite see what species it is.

00:21:54 --> 00:21:58
For instance, this past few months we found

00:21:57 --> 00:22:00
two baby black caiman on the river,

00:21:59 --> 00:22:01
which is unprecedented here.

00:22:00 --> 00:22:03
We haven't seen that in decades.

00:22:02 --> 00:22:06
So it's important that we monitor our croc population.

00:22:05 --> 00:22:09
So I started catching small ones.

00:22:08 --> 00:22:10
In "Mother of God", I write about the first one

00:22:09 --> 00:22:11
that me and JJ caught together,

00:22:10 --> 00:22:14
which was probably a little bigger than this table.

00:22:13 --> 00:22:19
And probably mid-20s bravado and competition with

00:22:19 --> 00:22:23
other young males of my species led to me

00:22:23 --> 00:22:26
trying to go as big as I could.

00:22:26 --> 00:22:29
And I jumped on a spectacled caiman

00:22:28 --> 00:22:32
that was slightly longer than I am.

00:22:31 --> 00:22:35
And I'm 5'9", so I jumped on this

00:22:34 --> 00:22:39
probably six foot croc and quickly realized

00:22:38 --> 00:22:43
that my hands couldn't get around its neck

00:22:42 --> 00:22:46
and my legs were wrapped around the base of its tail.

00:22:45 --> 00:22:49
And the thrash was so intense that as it took me one side,

00:22:48 --> 00:22:52
I barely had enough time to realize what was happening

00:22:51 --> 00:22:54
before it beat me against the ground.

00:22:53 --> 00:22:55
My headlamp came off,

00:22:54 --> 00:22:56
so now I'm blind, in the dark,

00:22:55 --> 00:22:58
laying in a river in the Amazon rainforest,

00:22:57 --> 00:23:00
hugging a six-foot crocodile.

00:23:00 --> 00:23:03
And I went, "JJ".

00:23:02 --> 00:23:04
(Lex laughs)

00:23:03 --> 00:23:05
As I always do.

00:23:04 --> 00:23:07
But I, in that moment, before I even let go,

00:23:06 --> 00:23:08
I knew I couldn't let go of the croc

00:23:07 --> 00:23:09
because if I let go of the croc,

00:23:08 --> 00:23:11
I thought she was gonna destroy my face.

00:23:10 --> 00:23:12
So I said, "Okay, now I'm stuck here.

00:23:11 --> 00:23:15
If I just stay here, I can't release her.

00:23:14 --> 00:23:16
I need help."

00:23:15 --> 00:23:19
But I was like, I'm never ever, ever, ever gonna try

00:23:18 --> 00:23:21
and solo catch a croc this big again.

00:23:20 --> 00:23:23
I was like, this is, I knew in that moment I was like,

00:23:22 --> 00:23:24
this is good enough.

00:23:23 --> 00:23:24
- So anything longer than you.

00:23:23 --> 00:23:26
- [Paul] No. - You don't control the tail.

00:23:25 --> 00:23:28
You don't have, you have barely control of anything, really.

00:23:27 --> 00:23:29
- Yeah, and that's a spectacled caiman.

00:23:28 --> 00:23:32
A black caiman is a whole other order of magnitude there.

00:23:31 --> 00:23:34
It's like saying like, oh, you know,

00:23:33 --> 00:23:36
I was play fighting with my Golden Retriever

00:23:35 --> 00:23:39
sus I was play fighting with like, you know,

00:23:39 --> 00:23:42
what's the biggest, scariest dog you could think of?

00:23:41 --> 00:23:45
The dog from Sandlot, a giant, gorilla dog thing.

00:23:44 --> 00:23:48
Like a Malamute something, something huge.

00:23:47 --> 00:23:49
What do they call, Mastiffs?

00:23:48 --> 00:23:51
- Yeah, Mastiffs. - [Paul] Mastiffs.

00:23:50 --> 00:23:52
- I mean, you mentioned dinosaurs.

00:23:51 --> 00:23:55
What do you admire about black caiman?

00:23:54 --> 00:23:58
What, they've been here for a very, very long time.

00:23:57 --> 00:24:01
There's something prehistoric about their appearance,

00:24:00 --> 00:24:03
about their way of being,

00:24:02 --> 00:24:04
about their presence in this jungle.

00:24:03 --> 00:24:08
- With crocodiles, you're looking at this mega survivor.

00:24:07 --> 00:24:10
They're in a class with sharks

00:24:09 --> 00:24:12
where it's like they've been here so long.

00:24:11 --> 00:24:14
When you talk about multiple extinctions,

00:24:13 --> 00:24:16
you talk about the Sixth Extinction,

00:24:15 --> 00:24:17
Earth's going through all this stuff.

00:24:16 --> 00:24:20
The crocodiles and the cockroaches have seen it all before.

00:24:19 --> 00:24:24
They're like, man, we remember what that comet looked like.

00:24:23 --> 00:24:25
And they're not impressed.

00:24:24 --> 00:24:27
- Yeah, they have this, they carry this wisdom.

00:24:26 --> 00:24:28
- [Paul] Yeah. - In their power.

00:24:27 --> 00:24:30
- [Paul] Yeah. - In the simplicity

00:24:29 --> 00:24:31
of their power, they carry the wisdom.

00:24:30 --> 00:24:34
- Yeah, and they're just sitting there in the streams

00:24:33 --> 00:24:34
and they don't care.

00:24:33 --> 00:24:36
And even if there's a nuclear holocaust,

00:24:35 --> 00:24:39
you know that there would just be some crocs sitting there,

00:24:38 --> 00:24:40
dead-eyed in that stagnant water

00:24:39 --> 00:24:43
waiting for the life to regenerate so they could eat again.

00:24:42 --> 00:24:45
- It's gonna be the remaining humans

00:24:44 --> 00:24:48
versus the crocs and the cockroaches.

00:24:47 --> 00:24:50
And the cockroaches are just background noise.

00:24:49 --> 00:24:52
- Yeah, they'll always be there.

00:24:51 --> 00:24:53
Sons of bitches.

00:24:53 --> 00:24:56
- You know, we're talking about individual black caiman

00:24:55 --> 00:24:57
d caiman and different species of caiman

00:24:57 --> 00:25:01
but whenever they're together and you see multiple eyes,

00:25:00 --> 00:25:03
which I've gotten to experience,

00:25:02 --> 00:25:04
it's quite a feeling.

00:25:03 --> 00:25:06
There's just multiple eyes looking back at you.

00:25:05 --> 00:25:08
- [Paul] Mm-hmm.

00:25:07 --> 00:25:10
- Of course, for you that's

00:25:10 --> 00:25:12
immediate excitement.

00:25:13 --> 00:25:16
You immediately go towards that.

00:25:15 --> 00:25:17
You wanna see it, you want to explore it,

00:25:16 --> 00:25:19
maybe catch them, analyze what the species is,

00:25:18 --> 00:25:20
all that kind of stuff.

00:25:19 --> 00:25:22
- [Paul] Yeah. - What's,

00:25:21 --> 00:25:24
can you just describe that feeling

00:25:23 --> 00:25:26
when they're together and they're looking at you?

00:25:25 --> 00:25:29
So head above water, eyes reflecting the light.

00:25:28 --> 00:25:32
- Yeah, so the other night Lex and I were

00:25:31 --> 00:25:36
in the river with JJ surviving a thunderstorm.

00:25:35 --> 00:25:38
We're in the rain and we had covered our

00:25:39 --> 00:25:42
equipment with our boats.

00:25:41 --> 00:25:43
And the only thing that we could do was

00:25:42 --> 00:25:47
get in the river to keep ourselves dry.

00:25:46 --> 00:25:49
And so we were in the river at night, in the dark,

00:25:48 --> 00:25:52
no stars, just a little bit of canopy silhouetted

00:25:51 --> 00:25:53
with all this rain coming down.

00:25:52 --> 00:25:55
It was such a din you could hardly hear anything.

00:25:54 --> 00:25:57
And all the way down river,

00:25:56 --> 00:26:00
I just see this caiman eye in my headlamp light.

00:26:00 --> 00:26:05
And I started walking towards it because I was like,

00:26:04 --> 00:26:06
this is even better.

00:26:05 --> 00:26:08
We can catch a caiman while we're in this thunderstorm

00:26:07 --> 00:26:10
in the Amazon River.

00:26:09 --> 00:26:13
And when JJ went, "Paul, it's too far."

00:26:13 --> 00:26:17
JJ very rarely, very rarely,

00:26:16 --> 00:26:18
like he'll make a suggestion.

00:26:17 --> 00:26:20
Like, he'll usually go like, "Maybe it's far."

00:26:19 --> 00:26:21
(Lex laughs)

00:26:20 --> 00:26:24
But in that situation, deep in the wilderness,

00:26:23 --> 00:26:26
unknown caiman size, he went,

00:26:25 --> 00:26:27
"Paul, it's too far.

00:26:26 --> 00:26:30
Don't leave the three of us right now."

00:26:29 --> 00:26:31
- [Lex] Yeah. - We were too far out

00:26:30 --> 00:26:32
o take risks.

00:26:31 --> 00:26:33
We're too far out to be

00:26:32 --> 00:26:34
walking along the river bed at night.

00:26:33 --> 00:26:37
Because then, you know, right here at the research station,

00:26:36 --> 00:26:40
if you step on a stingray, you get evaced.

00:26:39 --> 00:26:43
Out where we went, nothing.

00:26:42 --> 00:26:45
So for me, seeing those eyes,

00:26:44 --> 00:26:47
I think I've become so comfortable

00:26:46 --> 00:26:51
with so many of these animals that I may have crossed into

00:26:50 --> 00:26:54
the territory where I feel so comfortable

00:26:53 --> 00:26:56
with many of these animals that

00:26:55 --> 00:26:57
hey just don't worry me anymore.

00:26:56 --> 00:27:00
I mean, you were, I looked at you in a raft

00:26:59 --> 00:27:02
while you had a sizable,

00:27:01 --> 00:27:05
probably about 12-foot black caiman right next to your raft.

00:27:04 --> 00:27:06
I watched its head go under.

00:27:05 --> 00:27:08
- Bubbles, bubbles. - The bubbles.

00:27:07 --> 00:27:09
It was all coming up right next to your raft

00:27:08 --> 00:27:11
as he was just moving along the bottom of the river

00:27:10 --> 00:27:13
'cause he looked at me, went under,

00:27:12 --> 00:27:15
and then my raft passed and yours came over him.

00:27:14 --> 00:27:17
So now I'm looking back and your raft is

00:27:16 --> 00:27:20
going over this black caiman and I'm going,

00:27:19 --> 00:27:22
I'm not worried at all.

00:27:21 --> 00:27:23
I was not worried.

00:27:22 --> 00:27:25
I was not worried that the caiman would freak out.

00:27:25 --> 00:27:28
I was not worried that he would try to attack you.

00:27:27 --> 00:27:31
I knew 100% that caiman just wanted us to go

00:27:30 --> 00:27:32
so he could go back to eating fish.

00:27:31 --> 00:27:33
- [Lex[ Yeah. - That's it.

00:27:32 --> 00:27:35
- Man, it's humbling.

00:27:34 --> 00:27:37
It's humbling, these giant creatures.

00:27:36 --> 00:27:40
And especially at night, like you were talking about.

00:27:40 --> 00:27:44
And for me it's both scary and just beautiful

00:27:43 --> 00:27:46
when the head goes under.

00:27:45 --> 00:27:48
- Hmm. - Because like underwater,

00:27:47 --> 00:27:51
it's their domain so anything can happen.

00:27:50 --> 00:27:53
So what is it doing that its head is going under?

00:27:52 --> 00:27:57
It could be bored, it could be hungry,

00:27:56 --> 00:27:58
looking for some fish.

00:27:57 --> 00:28:00
It could be maybe wanting to come closer

00:27:59 --> 00:28:01
to you to investigate.

00:28:00 --> 00:28:04
Maybe you have some food around you.

00:28:03 --> 00:28:06
Maybe it's an old friend of yours and just wants to say hi.

00:28:05 --> 00:28:07
I don't know.

00:28:06 --> 00:28:08
- I have a few on the river,

00:28:07 --> 00:28:10
old friends. - [Lex] Okay.

00:28:09 --> 00:28:12
- No, when we see their heads go under, it's just,

00:28:12 --> 00:28:14
they're just getting outta the way.

00:28:13 --> 00:28:16
We're shining a light at them and they're going

00:28:15 --> 00:28:17
why is there a light at night?

00:28:16 --> 00:28:20
I'm uncomfortable, head under.

00:28:19 --> 00:28:21
So these caiman, again,

00:28:20 --> 00:28:23
you think of it as this big, aggressive animal,

00:28:22 --> 00:28:26
but I don't know anybody that's been eaten by a black caiman

00:28:25 --> 00:28:28
d the smaller species, smooth-fronted caiman,

00:28:27 --> 00:28:30
dwarf caiman, spectacled caiman,

00:28:29 --> 00:28:31
they're not gonna eat anybody.

00:28:30 --> 00:28:34
Again, at the worst, if you were doing something

00:28:33 --> 00:28:36
inappropriate with a caiman,

00:28:35 --> 00:28:39
like you jumped on it and were trying to do research

00:28:38 --> 00:28:42
and it bit your hand, it could take your hand off.

00:28:41 --> 00:28:43
But that's the only time.

00:28:42 --> 00:28:44
I've been walking down the river

00:28:43 --> 00:28:47
and stepped on a caiman and the caiman just swims away.

00:28:46 --> 00:28:49
And so in my mind, caiman are just these,

00:28:48 --> 00:28:52
they're peaceful dragons that sit on the side of the river.

00:28:51 --> 00:28:54
And so to me, they are my friends

00:28:53 --> 00:28:57
and I worry about them because two months ago

00:28:56 --> 00:28:58
we were coming upriver

00:28:57 --> 00:29:02
and on one of the beaches was a beautiful,

00:29:01 --> 00:29:05
about five-foot black caiman with a big machete cut

00:29:04 --> 00:29:07
right through the head.

00:29:06 --> 00:29:09
The whole caiman was wasted, nothing was eaten,

00:29:09 --> 00:29:12
but the caiman was dead.

00:29:11 --> 00:29:14
- What do you think that was?

00:29:13 --> 00:29:16
- Curious humans.

00:29:15 --> 00:29:18
- [Lex] Just committing violence?

00:29:17 --> 00:29:19
- Yeah, just loggers,

00:29:18 --> 00:29:21
people who aren't from this part of the Amazon

00:29:20 --> 00:29:25
because a local person would either eat the animal

00:29:24 --> 00:29:26
or not mess with it.

00:29:25 --> 00:29:28
Like, Pico would never kill a caiman for no reason

00:29:27 --> 00:29:31
because it doesn't make any sense.

00:29:30 --> 00:29:33
So these are clearly people who aren't from the region,

00:29:32 --> 00:29:34
which usually means loggers

00:29:33 --> 00:29:36
'cause they've come from somewhere else.

00:29:35 --> 00:29:38
They're doing a job here and they're just cleaning

00:29:37 --> 00:29:40
their pots in the river at night

00:29:39 --> 00:29:41
and they see eyes come near them

00:29:40 --> 00:29:43
because the caiman probably smells fish.

00:29:42 --> 00:29:45
And then they just whack 'cause they want to see it.

00:29:44 --> 00:29:47
And they're just curious monkeys on a beach.

00:29:47 --> 00:29:52
And again, me friend of caiman, I protect from my type.

00:29:51 --> 00:29:56
- That said, you know, you protect your friends

00:29:55 --> 00:29:59
and you analyze and study your friends.

00:29:58 --> 00:30:03
But sometimes friends can have a bit of a misunderstanding.

00:30:02 --> 00:30:04
And if you have a bit of a misunderstanding

00:30:03 --> 00:30:06
with a black caiman, I feel like

00:30:05 --> 00:30:09
just a bit of a misunderstanding could lead

00:30:08 --> 00:30:10
to a

00:30:10 --> 00:30:13
bone crushing situation.

00:30:12 --> 00:30:16
- But not for a little, five-foot caiman.

00:30:15 --> 00:30:18
And I think that's incredibly speciesist of you.

00:30:19 --> 00:30:21
(Paul laughs)

00:30:20 --> 00:30:22
- Of all humans or all caiman?

00:30:21 --> 00:30:24
- No, like all my friends do the same thing.

00:30:23 --> 00:30:26
They go, "You swim in the Amazon rainforest?"

00:30:25 --> 00:30:27
You know, "You swim in that river?"

00:30:26 --> 00:30:29
And I go, "Yes, every day."

00:30:28 --> 00:30:31
We, you know, back flips into the river.

00:30:30 --> 00:30:33
We've been swimming in the river how many times

00:30:32 --> 00:30:35
with the piranha and the stingray

00:30:34 --> 00:30:37
and the candiru and the caiman and the anacondas,

00:30:36 --> 00:30:39
all of it in the river with us.

00:30:40 --> 00:30:42
And we just do it.

00:30:41 --> 00:30:43
And what's that for you?

00:30:42 --> 00:30:44
So what allows you to doing that,

00:30:43 --> 00:30:48
knowing and having researched all the different things

00:30:47 --> 00:30:49
that can kill you?

00:30:48 --> 00:30:51
Which I feel like most of them are in the river.

00:30:50 --> 00:30:54
What allows you to just get in there with us?

00:30:53 --> 00:30:58
- Well, I think it's something about you,

00:30:57 --> 00:31:00
where you become like this portal through which

00:30:59 --> 00:31:01
it's possible to see nature

00:31:00 --> 00:31:03
as not threatening but beautiful.

00:31:02 --> 00:31:05
And so in that you kinda naturally,

00:31:04 --> 00:31:07
by hanging out with you,

00:31:06 --> 00:31:09
I get to see the beauty of it.

00:31:09 --> 00:31:11
There is danger out there,

00:31:10 --> 00:31:13
but the dangerous part of it,

00:31:12 --> 00:31:15
just like the, there's a lot of danger in the city.

00:31:14 --> 00:31:16
There's danger in life,

00:31:15 --> 00:31:19
there's a lot of ways to get hurt emotionally, physically.

00:31:18 --> 00:31:21
There's a lot of ways to die in the stupidest of ways.

00:31:20 --> 00:31:24
We went on an expedition through the forest,

00:31:23 --> 00:31:26
just twisting your ankle, breaking your foot,

00:31:26 --> 00:31:30
getting a bite from a thing that gets infected.

00:31:29 --> 00:31:32
It's, there's a lot of ways to die and get hurt

00:31:31 --> 00:31:33
in the stupidest of ways

00:31:32 --> 00:31:38
in a nondramatic, caiman eating you alive kind of way.

00:31:37 --> 00:31:41
- Yeah, it strikes me as unfair because humans,

00:31:40 --> 00:31:46
we're still in our minds so programmed

00:31:45 --> 00:31:48
to worry about that predator.

00:31:47 --> 00:31:49
That predator, that predator.

00:31:48 --> 00:31:50
What predator?

00:31:49 --> 00:31:51
We've killed everything.

00:31:50 --> 00:31:53
Black caimans are coming off the endangered species list.

00:31:52 --> 00:31:55
We exterminated wolves from North America.

00:31:54 --> 00:31:58
I actually heard a suburban lady one time tell her son,

00:31:57 --> 00:32:01
"Watch out, foxes will get you."

00:32:00 --> 00:32:02
Foxes. - [Lex] Yeah.

00:32:02 --> 00:32:06
- They eat baby rabbits and mice.

00:32:05 --> 00:32:08
- Well, in the case of apex predators,

00:32:07 --> 00:32:10
I think when people say dangerous animals,

00:32:10 --> 00:32:15
they really are talking about just the power of the animal.

00:32:14 --> 00:32:17
And the black caiman have a lot of power.

00:32:16 --> 00:32:19
- A lot of power.

00:32:18 --> 00:32:21
- [Lex] And it's almost just a way to celebrate

00:32:20 --> 00:32:22
the power of the animal.

00:32:21 --> 00:32:23
- Sure, and if it's in celebration,

00:32:22 --> 00:32:26
then I'm all for it because my God is that power,

00:32:25 --> 00:32:29
like the waves of fury that you saw.

00:32:28 --> 00:32:31
Like when that tail, I mean you saw, you saw the tail,

00:32:30 --> 00:32:33
the spectacled, that perfect, amazing thing

00:32:32 --> 00:32:35
with all those interlocking scales that works.

00:32:34 --> 00:32:36
- [Lex] Yeah. - So it's like a perfect

00:32:35 --> 00:32:38
creation of engineering.

00:32:37 --> 00:32:41
And then when you have one that's this thick

00:32:40 --> 00:32:43
and all of a sudden that thing is moving

00:32:42 --> 00:32:46
with all the acceleration of that power,

00:32:45 --> 00:32:48
the volume of water,

00:32:47 --> 00:32:50
the sound that comes outta their throat.

00:32:49 --> 00:32:52
They're such, they're dragons.

00:32:51 --> 00:32:54
- We talked about the scales of the snake with like,

00:32:53 --> 00:32:56
the caiman, just the way it felt.

00:32:55 --> 00:32:59
- [Paul] Yeah. - Was incredible.

00:32:58 --> 00:33:02
Just the armor, the texture of it was so cool.

00:33:01 --> 00:33:03
- [Paul] Yeah. - I don't know,

00:33:02 --> 00:33:07
like the bottom one came and has a certain kind of texture

00:33:06 --> 00:33:08
and it just all feels like power

00:33:07 --> 00:33:11
but also all feels like designed really well.

00:33:10 --> 00:33:15
It's like exploring through touch,

00:33:14 --> 00:33:18
like a World War II tank or something like that, just.

00:33:17 --> 00:33:19
- [Paul] Yeah. - It's the engineering

00:33:18 --> 00:33:20
that went into this thing.

00:33:19 --> 00:33:22
- [Paul] Yeah. - That like

00:33:21 --> 00:33:25
the mechanism of evolution that created a thing

00:33:24 --> 00:33:28
that could survive for such a long time.

00:33:27 --> 00:33:29
It's just, like, incredible.

00:33:28 --> 00:33:31
This is a work of art.

00:33:31 --> 00:33:35
The, you know, the defense mechanisms, the power of it,

00:33:34 --> 00:33:36
the damage it can do,

00:33:36 --> 00:33:39
how effective it is as a hunter.

00:33:38 --> 00:33:42
All of that, all you could feel that in just by touching it.

00:33:42 --> 00:33:46
- Do you ever see the mashup where they put side by side

00:33:45 --> 00:33:49
the image of, I think it's a falcon in flight

00:33:48 --> 00:33:51
next to a stealth bomber

00:33:50 --> 00:33:53
and they're almost the exact same design.

00:33:52 --> 00:33:55
It's incredible, like that.

00:33:54 --> 00:33:57
- What's the equivalent for a croc?

00:33:56 --> 00:33:58
(Lex and Paul laugh)

00:33:57 --> 00:33:59
- Like you said, maybe a tank.

00:33:58 --> 00:34:01
- [Lex] Maybe a tank. - More like armadillo, turtle.

00:34:00 --> 00:34:03
- [Lex] Yeah, like hippos. - I dunno.

00:34:02 --> 00:34:05
Yeah, there may not be a machine,

00:34:04 --> 00:34:08
a war machine equivalent of a crocodile.

00:34:07 --> 00:34:09
It would have to have like a

00:34:09 --> 00:34:11
big jaw element to it.

00:34:10 --> 00:34:15
- In the water, I mean we talked also about hippos.

00:34:14 --> 00:34:16
Those are interesting creatures

00:34:15 --> 00:34:19
from all the way across the world, just monsters.

00:34:18 --> 00:34:21
- [Paul] Yeah. - Hippos and rhinos.

00:34:20 --> 00:34:24
Hippos are bigger usually, or rhinos are bigger?

00:34:23 --> 00:34:25
- Rhinos.

00:34:24 --> 00:34:28
Rhinos is the, after elephants is the largest.

00:34:27 --> 00:34:29
White rhinos.

00:34:28 --> 00:34:30
- They can be terrifying too.

00:34:29 --> 00:34:32
Again, when you step into the defense.

00:34:31 --> 00:34:34
- Absolutely, but I have to tell you,

00:34:33 --> 00:34:36
after being around so many rhinos.

00:34:35 --> 00:34:36
- You have friends, you have friend rhinos?

00:34:36 --> 00:34:38
- I have rhino friends.

00:34:37 --> 00:34:40
- [Lex] Yeah. - Black and white rhinos.

00:34:39 --> 00:34:43
- [Lex] Yeah. - And they're all sweethearts.

00:34:42 --> 00:34:44
And I mean sweethearts.

00:34:43 --> 00:34:47
And I mean when you look at a rhino,

00:34:46 --> 00:34:48
it's like a living dinosaur.

00:34:47 --> 00:34:50
I know it's a mammal, but somehow it's screams dinosaur

00:34:49 --> 00:34:52
'cause it seems like Pleistocenic

00:34:51 --> 00:34:55
and from another age with the giant horn.

00:34:54 --> 00:34:56
And they're so much bigger than you think.

00:34:55 --> 00:34:58
Like, they're minivan sized animals.

00:34:57 --> 00:35:02
Like you're, we're not taller than they are at the shoulder.

00:35:01 --> 00:35:03
And they have this strange shaped head

00:35:02 --> 00:35:08
and the huge horn and they sit there eating grass all day.

00:35:07 --> 00:35:10
So if a rhino is dangerous to a human,

00:35:09 --> 00:35:13
it's because the rhino is going don't hurt me.

00:35:12 --> 00:35:15
- [Lex] Yeah. - Don't hurt me.

00:35:14 --> 00:35:15
Don't hurt my baby.

00:35:15 --> 00:35:16
And then they're like, you know what?

00:35:15 --> 00:35:17
I'll just kill you, it'll be easier,

00:35:16 --> 00:35:18
'cause you're scaring me right now.

00:35:17 --> 00:35:19
- [Lex] Yeah. - You're too close

00:35:18 --> 00:35:20
to that rhino. - [Lex] Yeah.

00:35:20 --> 00:35:23
- And so like there again, I just think it's funny

00:35:22 --> 00:35:25
'cause humans were so quickly to go,

00:35:24 --> 00:35:27
"Which snakes are aggressive?"

00:35:26 --> 00:35:28
There are no aggressive snakes.

00:35:27 --> 00:35:32
You know, rhinos can be dangerous if provoked.

00:35:31 --> 00:35:36
Otherwise, they're peaceful, fat, grass unicorns, you know?

00:35:35 --> 00:35:39
Like, they're really pretty calm.

00:35:38 --> 00:35:40
- Yeah. - We had these incredible

00:35:39 --> 00:35:43
giant animals and the largest animals on our planet,

00:35:42 --> 00:35:45
the black caiman, the rhinos, the elephants,

00:35:44 --> 00:35:49
all the big, beautiful stuff is becoming less and less.

00:35:48 --> 00:35:50
- [Lex] Yeah. - And it almost reminds me

00:35:49 --> 00:35:52
like in "Game of Thrones", they're like, yeah, they're,

00:35:51 --> 00:35:53
in the beginning they're like, yeah,

00:35:52 --> 00:35:55
there used to be dragons.

00:35:54 --> 00:35:57
And it was like this memory and it's like, yeah,

00:35:56 --> 00:35:59
we used to have mammoths

00:35:58 --> 00:36:02
and we used to have Steller's sea cows

00:36:01 --> 00:36:04
that were 16-feet long, manatees.

00:36:03 --> 00:36:06
And it's, there were things we used to have,

00:36:05 --> 00:36:10
the Caspian tiger that only went extinct in the '90s,

00:36:09 --> 00:36:12
our lifetimes.

00:36:11 --> 00:36:14
And it's, that's mind-blowing to me.

00:36:13 --> 00:36:16
That has haunted me since I'm a child.

00:36:15 --> 00:36:18
I remember learning about extinction and I went

00:36:17 --> 00:36:20
wait, you're telling me that,

00:36:19 --> 00:36:21
I remember being a kid and going

00:36:20 --> 00:36:23
by the time I grew up you're saying that I,

00:36:22 --> 00:36:27
gorillas could be gone, elephants could be gone,

00:36:26 --> 00:36:28
and because we're doing it?

00:36:27 --> 00:36:31
And then I just that, I remember,

00:36:30 --> 00:36:33
I remember looking at the nightlight being blurry

00:36:32 --> 00:36:35
'cause I was crying.

00:36:34 --> 00:36:37
I was so upset and oh, and it was Lonesome George,

00:36:36 --> 00:36:39
that turtle, the Galapagos tortoise

00:36:38 --> 00:36:40
where there was one left and they said.

00:36:39 --> 00:36:41
- [Lex] Yeah. - If we just,

00:36:40 --> 00:36:43
if we just had a female, he could live.

00:36:42 --> 00:36:47
And I as a six, seven, eight year old, that destroyed me.

00:36:46 --> 00:36:48
- We're all just trying to get laid,

00:36:47 --> 00:36:49
including that turtle.

00:36:48 --> 00:36:52
- Including that turtle for a few hundred years.

00:36:51 --> 00:36:53
Dude.

00:36:52 --> 00:36:54
(Lex laughs)

00:36:53 --> 00:36:55
- So for young people out there,

00:36:54 --> 00:36:57
you think you are having trouble.

00:36:56 --> 00:36:57
- [Paul] Yeah. - Think about that turtle.

00:36:56 --> 00:36:59
- Think about that turtle, yeah.

00:36:58 --> 00:37:01
You know, there's a turtle that Darwin

00:37:00 --> 00:37:03
and Steve Irwin both owned.

00:37:02 --> 00:37:04
- Yeah, yeah, I heard about that turtle.

00:37:03 --> 00:37:06
Man, they live a long time.

00:37:05 --> 00:37:08
- [Paul] Yeah. - They've seen things.

00:37:07 --> 00:37:09
- They've seen things that,

00:37:08 --> 00:37:12
there's a great like internet joke

00:37:11 --> 00:37:14
where they're like, they're like accusing him of like

00:37:13 --> 00:37:16
being incongruous with modern times.

00:37:15 --> 00:37:18
They're like, he did nothing to stop slavery.

00:37:17 --> 00:37:19
He didn't fight in World War II.

00:37:18 --> 00:37:21
(Lex and Paul laugh)

00:37:20 --> 00:37:22
- Cancel the turtle.

00:37:21 --> 00:37:23
- Yeah, cancel the turtle.

00:37:22 --> 00:37:26
- Oh shit, what a world we live in.

00:37:25 --> 00:37:30
So it's interesting you mentioned black caiman and anacondas

00:37:29 --> 00:37:32
are both apex predators.

00:37:32 --> 00:37:36
So it seems like the reason they can exist

00:37:35 --> 00:37:38
in similar environments is 'cause they feed

00:37:37 --> 00:37:40
on slightly different things.

00:37:40 --> 00:37:44
How is it possible for them to coexist?

00:37:43 --> 00:37:48
I read that anacondas can eat caiman, but not black caiman.

00:37:47 --> 00:37:50
How often do they come in conflict?

00:37:49 --> 00:37:54
- So anacondas and caiman occupy the exact same niche

00:37:53 --> 00:37:57
and they're born at almost the exact same size.

00:37:56 --> 00:38:00
And unlike most species,

00:37:59 --> 00:38:01
they don't have sort of a size range

00:38:00 --> 00:38:03
that they're confined to.

00:38:02 --> 00:38:04
They start at this big.

00:38:03 --> 00:38:06
Baby caiman are this big.

00:38:05 --> 00:38:07
Baby anacondas are a little longer,

00:38:06 --> 00:38:09
but they're thinner and they don't have legs.

00:38:08 --> 00:38:12
So, it's the same thing in terms of mass.

00:38:11 --> 00:38:16
And they're all in the streams or at the edges of lakes

00:38:15 --> 00:38:17
or swamps.

00:38:16 --> 00:38:19
And so the baby anacondas eat the baby caiman.

00:38:18 --> 00:38:22
Baby caiman can't really take down an anaconda.

00:38:21 --> 00:38:24
They're going for little insects and fish.

00:38:23 --> 00:38:26
They have a quite a small mouth.

00:38:25 --> 00:38:28
So they, again, it's in their interest to hide

00:38:27 --> 00:38:30
from everything.

00:38:29 --> 00:38:35
A bird, a heron can eat a baby caiman, pop it back.

00:38:34 --> 00:38:36
And so they have to survive

00:38:35 --> 00:38:40
but the anaconda and the caiman kind of joust as they grow.

00:38:39 --> 00:38:41
- Can you actually explain how the anaconda

00:38:40 --> 00:38:43
would take down a caiman?

00:38:42 --> 00:38:47
Like, would it first use constriction and then eat it?

00:38:46 --> 00:38:49
Or what's the methodology?

00:38:48 --> 00:38:52
- Yeah, so anacondas have a kind of a, I don't know,

00:38:51 --> 00:38:54
like a three-point constriction system

00:38:53 --> 00:38:57
where their first thing is anchor.

00:38:56 --> 00:38:58
It's like jiu-jitsu.

00:38:57 --> 00:39:01
So the first thing is latch onto you.

00:39:00 --> 00:39:02
- I like how I'm writing this down, like, all right.

00:39:01 --> 00:39:03
- [Paul] Yes. - This is jiu-jitsu

00:39:02 --> 00:39:05
like masterclass here.

00:39:05 --> 00:39:07
- This is for when you're wrestling an anaconda,

00:39:06 --> 00:39:09
just in case.

00:39:08 --> 00:39:10
(Paul and Lex laugh)

00:39:09 --> 00:39:12
- And you'll be like the coach on the sidelines screaming.

00:39:11 --> 00:39:13
- You got it, Lex.

00:39:12 --> 00:39:16
(Lex laughs)

00:39:15 --> 00:39:17
Don't let him take the back.

00:39:16 --> 00:39:19
- Yeah. - All right, so one time

00:39:18 --> 00:39:20
and JJ were following a herd of collared peccary

00:39:19 --> 00:39:23
and JJ is teaching me tracking.

00:39:22 --> 00:39:25
So we're following, you know, the hoof prints

00:39:24 --> 00:39:26
through the mud and we're doing this

00:39:25 --> 00:39:28
and I'm talking about no backpacks,

00:39:27 --> 00:39:31
just machetes, bare feet running through the jungle.

00:39:30 --> 00:39:33
And we come to this stream and JJ is like,

00:39:32 --> 00:39:35
"I think we missed them.

00:39:34 --> 00:39:36
You know, I think they went."

00:39:35 --> 00:39:37
And I'm like, "No, no, no, they went here, look."

00:39:36 --> 00:39:38
And not 'cause I'm a great tracker

00:39:37 --> 00:39:41
'cause I can see, you know, a few dozen footprints,

00:39:40 --> 00:39:43
hundreds of individual footprints right there.

00:39:42 --> 00:39:45
And I'm going, "No, no, they just crossed here."

00:39:44 --> 00:39:47
And JJ was like, "You know what?

00:39:46 --> 00:39:49
We're not gonna get eyes on 'em today."

00:39:48 --> 00:39:50
He was like, "It's okay."

00:39:49 --> 00:39:50
He's like, "We did good.

00:39:49 --> 00:39:51
We followed them for a long time."

00:39:50 --> 00:39:52
And I was like, "Cool."

00:39:51 --> 00:39:53
And then I was trying to gauge like,

00:39:52 --> 00:39:55
can I drink this stream?

00:39:54 --> 00:39:58
And I see a culpa and a culpa is a salt deposit

00:39:57 --> 00:40:00
where animals come to feed

00:39:59 --> 00:40:02
'cause sodium is a deficiency

00:40:01 --> 00:40:05
that most herbivores have here.

00:40:04 --> 00:40:07
And all of a sudden I just hear

00:40:06 --> 00:40:09
like the sound of a wet stick snapping,

00:40:08 --> 00:40:11
just that bone crunch.

00:40:11 --> 00:40:15
And I look down and there's about a 16-foot anaconda

00:40:14 --> 00:40:20
wrapped around a freshly killed peccary, wild boar.

00:40:19 --> 00:40:23
And what this anaconda had done was as the,

00:40:22 --> 00:40:26
all the pigs were going across the stream,

00:40:25 --> 00:40:29
the anaconda had grabbed it by the jaw,

00:40:28 --> 00:40:32
swiped the legs, wrapped around it,

00:40:31 --> 00:40:36
bent it in half and then crushed its ribs.

00:40:35 --> 00:40:38
And that's what the anaconda do

00:40:37 --> 00:40:39
whether it's to mammals, to caiman,

00:40:38 --> 00:40:41
it's all the same thing.

00:40:40 --> 00:40:45
It's grab on, they have six rows of backwards-facing teeth.

00:40:44 --> 00:40:48
So once they hit you, they're never gonna come off.

00:40:47 --> 00:40:50
You actually have to go deeper in and then open

00:40:49 --> 00:40:52
before you can come out.

00:40:51 --> 00:40:53
All those backward-facing teeth,

00:40:52 --> 00:40:55
so they have an incredible anchor system

00:40:54 --> 00:40:58
and then they use their weight to pull you down to hell,

00:40:57 --> 00:40:59
to pull you down into that water,

00:40:58 --> 00:41:03
wrap around you and then start breaking you.

00:41:02 --> 00:41:04
And every breath you take, you go.

00:41:03 --> 00:41:05
(Paul gasps)

00:41:04 --> 00:41:07
And you, you're up against a barrier.

00:41:06 --> 00:41:10
And then when you, when you exhale, they go a little tighter

00:41:09 --> 00:41:12
and you're never gonna get that space back.

00:41:11 --> 00:41:15
Your lungs are never going to expand again.

00:41:14 --> 00:41:17
And I know this because I've been in that crush

00:41:16 --> 00:41:18
before JJ pulled me out of it.

00:41:17 --> 00:41:21
And so this pig, the anaconda had gotten it

00:41:20 --> 00:41:22
and as the pig was thrashing

00:41:21 --> 00:41:23
and the anaconda was wrapping around,

00:41:22 --> 00:41:24
it had bent it in half.

00:41:24 --> 00:41:27
And I just heard those vertebrae going.

00:41:26 --> 00:41:28
- [Lex] Yeah. - And so for a caiman,

00:41:27 --> 00:41:29
it's the same thing.

00:41:28 --> 00:41:30
They just grab 'em, they wrap around it

00:41:29 --> 00:41:32
and then they have to crush it until there's no response.

00:41:31 --> 00:41:35
They'll wait an hour, they'll wait a long time

00:41:34 --> 00:41:36
until there's no response from the animal.

00:41:35 --> 00:41:40
They'll overpower it, then they'll reposition,

00:41:39 --> 00:41:42
probably yawn a little bit,

00:41:41 --> 00:41:46
open their jaw and then start forcing that entire.

00:41:45 --> 00:41:48
Now here's the crazy thing,

00:41:47 --> 00:41:53
is that an anaconda has stomach acid capable of digesting

00:41:52 --> 00:41:57
an entire crocodile where nothing comes out the other side.

00:41:56 --> 00:41:58
And when you see how thick

00:41:57 --> 00:42:01
the bony plate of a crocodile skull is,

00:42:00 --> 00:42:03
that that can go in the mouth

00:42:02 --> 00:42:05
and nothing comes out the other side, that's insane.

00:42:04 --> 00:42:08
And so it always made me wonder on a chemistry level

00:42:07 --> 00:42:10
how you can have such incredible acid in the stomach

00:42:09 --> 00:42:12
that doesn't harm the anaconda itself.

00:42:13 --> 00:42:15
And someone said mucus, but it's.

00:42:14 --> 00:42:17
- But it's able to digest, oh, it's some kind of mucus.

00:42:16 --> 00:42:18
Oh, the mucus. - [Paul] The mucus.

00:42:17 --> 00:42:20
- There's oh, interesting.

00:42:19 --> 00:42:22
There's levels of protection from the anaconda itself.

00:42:21 --> 00:42:25
But it seems like the anaconda is such a simple system

00:42:24 --> 00:42:26
as an organism.

00:42:25 --> 00:42:28
- [Paul] I know. - Like that simplicity,

00:42:27 --> 00:42:30
taken at scale, it could just do the,

00:42:29 --> 00:42:34
can swallow a caiman, digest it slowly.

00:42:33 --> 00:42:37
- I know, but my question was how on Earth is

00:42:36 --> 00:42:40
it physically possible to have this hellish bile

00:42:39 --> 00:42:42
that can digest anything,

00:42:41 --> 00:42:46
even something as horrendous as a caiman scales

00:42:45 --> 00:42:49
and bones and all the hardest shit in nature

00:42:48 --> 00:42:51
and then not hurt the snake itself?

00:42:50 --> 00:42:53
And I had a chemist explain to me

00:42:52 --> 00:42:55
that it's probably some sort of mucus system

00:42:54 --> 00:42:58
that lines the stomach and neutralizes the acid

00:42:57 --> 00:42:59
and keeps it floating in there.

00:42:58 --> 00:43:01
But my god, that must be powerful stuff.

00:43:01 --> 00:43:04
- So what does it feel like,

00:43:03 --> 00:43:08
being crushed, choked by an anaconda?

00:43:10 --> 00:43:15
- You, when an anaconda is wrapped around you

00:43:14 --> 00:43:20
and you find yourself in the shocking realization

00:43:19 --> 00:43:22
that these could be your last moments breathing,

00:43:21 --> 00:43:24
you are confronted with

00:43:24 --> 00:43:27
e vast disparity in power,

00:43:26 --> 00:43:30
that there is so much power in these animals,

00:43:29 --> 00:43:32
so much crushing, deliberate,

00:43:31 --> 00:43:36
reptilian, ancient power that doesn't care.

00:43:35 --> 00:43:39
They're just trying to get you to stop.

00:43:38 --> 00:43:40
They just want you to stop ticking

00:43:39 --> 00:43:42
and there's nothing you can do.

00:43:41 --> 00:43:44
And there's, I find it very awe-inspiring

00:43:43 --> 00:43:45
when I encounter that kind of power.

00:43:44 --> 00:43:47
When you, even if it's that you see, you know,

00:43:46 --> 00:43:49
you see a dog run, you know?

00:43:48 --> 00:43:50
You ever try to outrun a dog

00:43:49 --> 00:43:54
and they just zip by you and you go, wow.

00:43:53 --> 00:43:56
You know, or you see a horse kick and you go, oh my god,

00:43:55 --> 00:43:59
if that hoof hit anyone's head,

00:43:58 --> 00:44:00
it'd knock 'em three states over.

00:43:59 --> 00:44:03
And it's like there is muscular power

00:44:02 --> 00:44:04
that is so far that, like you said,

00:44:03 --> 00:44:07
that explosive, that we dream of doing it.

00:44:06 --> 00:44:10
Like imagine if like a Muay Thai kickboxer could

00:44:09 --> 00:44:13
harness that sort of caiman power, that smash.

00:44:14 --> 00:44:16
And so it's just awe-inspiring.

00:44:15 --> 00:44:19
I think it's really, really impressive what animals can do.

00:44:18 --> 00:44:20
And we're all, you know,

00:44:19 --> 00:44:23
we're all the same sort of makeup for the most part.

00:44:22 --> 00:44:24
All the mammals, you know?

00:44:23 --> 00:44:26
We all have our skeletons look so similar.

00:44:25 --> 00:44:27
We all have like, you know,

00:44:26 --> 00:44:30
if you look at like a kangaroo's biceps and chest,

00:44:29 --> 00:44:32
it looks so much like a man's.

00:44:31 --> 00:44:34
And if same thing goes for a bear,

00:44:33 --> 00:44:35
or you ever see a naked chimp?

00:44:34 --> 00:44:37
- Have I? - There's like chimps

00:44:36 --> 00:44:39
with alopecia. - [Lex] Oh shit.

00:44:38 --> 00:44:41
- And so. - [Lex] Oh they're shredded.

00:44:40 --> 00:44:42
- It looks like a bodybuilder.

00:44:41 --> 00:44:43
- [Lex] Yeah. - Like it's got cuts

00:44:42 --> 00:44:45
and huge, huge everything.

00:44:44 --> 00:44:49
Like it's got pecs and they got that face that's just like,

00:44:49 --> 00:44:51
just let me in.

00:44:50 --> 00:44:53
- What, no. - Yeah, where's your wallet?

00:44:52 --> 00:44:55
- Yeah, do something.

00:44:54 --> 00:44:56
But yeah, but there's a,

00:44:55 --> 00:45:01
the specialization of a lifetime of doing damage

00:45:00 --> 00:45:03
to the world and using those muscles,

00:45:02 --> 00:45:06
it just makes you just that much more powerful

00:45:05 --> 00:45:11
than most humans 'cause humans I guess have more brain,

00:45:10 --> 00:45:13
so they get lazy.

00:45:12 --> 00:45:16
They start puzzle solving versus, you know,

00:45:15 --> 00:45:17
using the biceps directly.

00:45:16 --> 00:45:19
- Well, yes and no and I have this question, okay.

00:45:18 --> 00:45:22
So I, you know, that whole you are what you eat thing.

00:45:21 --> 00:45:25
Now we one time here had two chickens.

00:45:24 --> 00:45:27
Now, one of them was a wild chicken,

00:45:26 --> 00:45:29
like from the farm had walked around its whole life

00:45:28 --> 00:45:30
finding insects,

00:45:29 --> 00:45:34
and the other chicken was like factory raised.

00:45:33 --> 00:45:36
And so we cut the heads off of both of them,

00:45:35 --> 00:45:38
started getting ready to cook 'em.

00:45:37 --> 00:45:41
Now the factory-raised chicken was like

00:45:40 --> 00:45:43
a much higher percentage of fat,

00:45:42 --> 00:45:44
had less muscle on its body,

00:45:43 --> 00:45:48
was softer tissue, a lighter color.

00:45:47 --> 00:45:52
The farm-raised chicken had darker, more sinewy muscles,

00:45:51 --> 00:45:55
less fat, was clearly a better-made machine.

00:45:54 --> 00:45:57
And so my question is

00:45:57 --> 00:46:00
that what's happening with us?

00:45:59 --> 00:46:01
You know, like if you go see a Sherpa

00:46:00 --> 00:46:03
who's been walking his whole life and pulling, you know,
