- So the lead authors of that paper,

00:49:16 --> 00:49:21
Pat Champagne and Carter Payne, friends of mine,

00:49:20 --> 00:49:25
and what we started noticing for me began

00:49:24 --> 00:49:26
at that story I told you where we were

00:49:25 --> 00:49:29
coming across the stream and we saw the anaconda

00:49:28 --> 00:49:31
had

00:49:30 --> 00:49:35
been positioned just below a culpa.

00:49:34 --> 00:49:37
And then other people began noticing

00:49:36 --> 00:49:40
that anaconda seem to always be beneath these culpas

00:49:39 --> 00:49:42
where mammals were gonna be coming.

00:49:41 --> 00:49:46
And that contrasted with what we knew about anacondas.

00:49:45 --> 00:49:47
'Cause what we understood about anacondas,

00:49:46 --> 00:49:48
that they're purely ambush predators

00:49:47 --> 00:49:51
and they don't pursue their prey.

00:49:50 --> 00:49:54
But what we began finding out here,

00:49:53 --> 00:49:58
and Pat led the process of amazing scientists.

00:49:57 --> 00:50:00
He worked with the Acadia University for a long time,

00:49:59 --> 00:50:02
worked with us for a long time.

00:50:01 --> 00:50:07
And he was one of the first to put a transmitter

00:50:06 --> 00:50:09
in an anaconda right around here.

00:50:08 --> 00:50:11
And we were able to see their movements.

00:50:10 --> 00:50:12
And that's what these papers are showing,

00:50:11 --> 00:50:15
is that they actually do pursue their prey.

00:50:14 --> 00:50:17
They do move up and down using the streams

00:50:16 --> 00:50:19
as corridors through the forest.

00:50:18 --> 00:50:20
They actually do pursue their prey.

00:50:19 --> 00:50:22
They actually do seek out food.

00:50:21 --> 00:50:25
So I mean, think about it, it's a giant anaconda.

00:50:24 --> 00:50:27
Obviously it's not, it can't just sit in one spot.

00:50:26 --> 00:50:28
It has to put some work into it.

00:50:27 --> 00:50:32
And so they're using scent and they're using communication

00:50:31 --> 00:50:33
to use the streams.

00:50:32 --> 00:50:35
So you could be walking in the forest

00:50:34 --> 00:50:37

00:50:36 --> 00:50:39
a sizable anaconda looking for a meal.

00:50:41 --> 00:50:45

00:50:46 --> 00:50:48
to burrow a little bit?

00:50:48 --> 00:50:53

00:50:53 --> 00:50:56
subterranean more than we think.

00:51:36 --> 00:51:39

one of the largest female anacondas on record.

And since that time,

these guys have been continuing to study the species,

continuing to just, again, just add

of the species.

And studying green anacondas in lowland tropical rainforest.

You've seen how hard it is to move,

to operate, to navigate in this environment.

And so when you think of the fact that in order to learn

'cause even if you get lucky enough

to be able to observe it over time,

You still run away.

00:58:19 --> 00:58:22
There's no reason for a street fight.

00:58:21 --> 00:58:25
And I think the animals really understand that.

00:58:24 --> 00:58:26
There's no reason for this.

00:58:25 --> 00:58:29
- So like a giant anaconda and a giant black caiman,

00:58:28 --> 00:58:32
they could probably even coexist in the same environment

00:58:31 --> 00:58:36
just knowing, using the wisdom to avoid the fight.

00:58:35 --> 00:58:37
Like, why? - Yeah,

00:58:36 --> 00:58:38
or they would have a big showdown

00:58:37 --> 00:58:41
and one of them would either die or have to leave.

00:58:40 --> 00:58:42
They would have a territorial dispute.

00:58:41 --> 00:58:43
- Yeah. - [Paul] Yeah.

00:58:42 --> 00:58:46
- Without killing either of them.

00:58:45 --> 00:58:50
- Yeah, on it, dude, nature, anything could happen.

00:58:49 --> 00:58:53
One of the things that me and Pat wrote up was that I saw

00:58:52 --> 00:58:55
a yellowtailed crebo,

00:58:54 --> 00:58:57
which is like a six-foot rat snake eating

00:58:56 --> 00:58:58
an Oxyrhopus melanogenys,

00:58:57 --> 00:59:02
which is the red snake that we found last night.

00:59:01 --> 00:59:04
And just no one had ever,

00:59:03 --> 00:59:05
in scientific literature,

00:59:04 --> 00:59:08
we'd never seen a cribo eating an Oxyrhopus before.

00:59:07 --> 00:59:11
And so I had the observation in the field,

00:59:10 --> 00:59:12
I sent it to Pat Champagne,

00:59:11 --> 00:59:14
Pat writes it up, paper.

00:59:13 --> 00:59:16
And so it's like, it's this really cool,

00:59:15 --> 00:59:17
that's a really cool system

00:59:16 --> 00:59:19
'cause we're just out here all the time.

00:59:18 --> 00:59:20
You end up seeing things.

00:59:19 --> 00:59:23
JJ's dad saw an anaconda eating a tapir.

00:59:22 --> 00:59:25
Tapir is the size of a cow.

00:59:24 --> 00:59:26
- [Lex] Damn. - And it's,

00:59:25 --> 00:59:27
that guy didn't lie, you know?

00:59:26 --> 00:59:28
- [Lex] Yeah. - Some people,

00:59:27 --> 00:59:29
you trust your sources on that.

00:59:28 --> 00:59:32
He saw enough stuff, he didn't need to make up stories.

00:59:31 --> 00:59:33
And you know how you,

00:59:32 --> 00:59:35
you know what I love now is when you go to,

00:59:34 --> 00:59:36
when you ask people,

00:59:35 --> 00:59:37
when we were going up the mountain with Jimmy.

00:59:36 --> 00:59:40
- [Lex] Yeah. - JJ said to him,

00:59:39 --> 00:59:43
he goes, "Have you ever seen a puma

00:59:42 --> 00:59:43
up here in the mountains?"

00:59:42 --> 00:59:45
And Jimmy goes, "They're up here."

00:59:44 --> 00:59:47
And JJ went, "No, no, no, have you seen it?"

00:59:46 --> 00:59:50
And Jimmy went, "No, never seen one."

00:59:49 --> 00:59:52
And you know how most people will go,

00:59:51 --> 00:59:54
"Yeah, yeah, yeah, I've seen."

00:59:53 --> 00:59:56
That makes me trust the person when they admit,

00:59:55 --> 00:59:57
"Nah, I haven't seen it."

00:59:58 --> 01:00:01
- They're up here, I haven't seen it.

01:00:00 --> 01:00:04
And Jimmy's been living there his whole life.

01:00:03 --> 01:00:05
- [Paul] His whole life.

01:00:05 --> 01:00:07
- There's pumas in the mountains?

01:00:06 --> 01:00:08
- [Paul] You know, mountain lions, pumas.

01:00:07 --> 01:00:09
- Damn. - Whatever the, you know,

01:00:08 --> 01:00:11
there's all different names for them.

01:00:10 --> 01:00:13
They're distributed from,

01:00:12 --> 01:00:15
I think from Alaska down through Argentina.

01:00:14 --> 01:00:16
That's, they're everywhere.

01:00:15 --> 01:00:18
It's extremely successful species,

01:00:17 --> 01:00:22
from deserts to high mountains, everything.

01:00:21 --> 01:00:25
- I think you're saying pumas have a curiosity,

01:00:24 --> 01:00:29
have a way about them where they like explore,

01:00:28 --> 01:00:32
like follow people. - [Paul] Yeah.

01:00:31 --> 01:00:34
- Like, just to kind of figure out like

01:00:34 --> 01:00:37
just that curiosity versus like,

01:00:36 --> 01:00:39
as opposed to causing harm or hunting

01:00:38 --> 01:00:39
and that kind of stuff.

01:00:38 --> 01:00:41
Like, what is this about?

01:00:40 --> 01:00:44
- I think it's based in predatory instincts,

01:00:43 --> 01:00:46
but I also think there is a playfulness

01:00:45 --> 01:00:48
to higher intelligence animals that you don't see

01:00:47 --> 01:00:49
in lower intelligence animals.

01:00:48 --> 01:00:53
And so something like a rabbit, for instance,

01:00:52 --> 01:00:56
you're never gonna see a rabbit come in to check you out.

01:00:55 --> 01:00:57
- [Lex] Yeah. - You just,

01:00:56 --> 01:00:59
you can't even think of it like that.

01:00:58 --> 01:01:01
Like, a rabbit's just gonna either eat or run away.

01:01:00 --> 01:01:03
There's really two settings.

01:01:02 --> 01:01:06
When you think of something like a giant river otter

01:01:06 --> 01:01:10
or a tayra, which is a, they call it manco here.

01:01:09 --> 01:01:12
It's a huge arboreal weasel.

01:01:12 --> 01:01:14
And they'll come check you out.

01:01:13 --> 01:01:17
I woke up at my house the other day

01:01:16 --> 01:01:19
and there was a tayra climbing up the side of the house

01:01:18 --> 01:01:22
and he was looking down at me sleeping.

01:01:21 --> 01:01:25
And it's like, he came to check me out.

01:01:24 --> 01:01:26
Like, it's like they're smart enough

01:01:25 --> 01:01:27
and they're brave enough.

01:01:26 --> 01:01:28
Here's the important thing.

01:01:27 --> 01:01:30
They know that they can fend for themselves.

01:01:29 --> 01:01:32
They can fight, they can climb, they can run.

01:01:31 --> 01:01:35
And so they're like, let me, I'm curious.

01:01:34 --> 01:01:36
I got time, lemme check this out.

01:01:35 --> 01:01:38
- Yeah, they're gathering information.

01:01:37 --> 01:01:42
I wonder how complex and sophisticated their world model is.

01:01:41 --> 01:01:43
Like, how they're integrating

01:01:42 --> 01:01:45
all the information about the environment,

01:01:44 --> 01:01:47
like where all the different trees are,

01:01:46 --> 01:01:49
where all the different nests of the different insects are,

01:01:48 --> 01:01:52
what the different creatures are that by size,

01:01:51 --> 01:01:53
all that kind of stuff.

01:01:52 --> 01:01:57
I'm sure they don't have enough, you know, storage up there

01:01:56 --> 01:01:58
to like keep all that.

01:01:57 --> 01:02:01
But they probably keep the important stuff,

01:02:00 --> 01:02:03
basic, you know, so sort of integrate the experiences

01:02:02 --> 01:02:07
they have into like, what is dangerous, what is tasty,

01:02:06 --> 01:02:08
all that kind of stuff.

01:02:07 --> 01:02:13
- I think it's more complex than we realize.

01:02:12 --> 01:02:15
You go back to that Frans de Waal book,

01:02:14 --> 01:02:17
"Are We Smart Enough to Know How Smart Animals Are?"

01:02:16 --> 01:02:21
There's so many incredible examples of controlled studies

01:02:20 --> 01:02:24
where the researchers weren't understanding

01:02:23 --> 01:02:28
how to shed being so insurmountably human

01:02:27 --> 01:02:29
d understand that there are

01:02:28 --> 01:02:32
other types of intelligence,

01:02:31 --> 01:02:34
and whether that's elephants or cats.

01:02:33 --> 01:02:36
So big cats, for instance,

01:02:35 --> 01:02:40
we just saw a camera trap video from last night.

01:02:39 --> 01:02:41
- [Lex] Yeah. - Where you see

01:02:40 --> 01:02:43
one of our workers walk down the trail

01:02:42 --> 01:02:47
and then five minutes later, a cat behind him.

01:02:46 --> 01:02:49
- By the way, we were walking just exactly the same area,

01:02:48 --> 01:02:51
also exact same time, yeah.

01:02:50 --> 01:02:54
- Yeah, so we're out there and there's deer and there's cats

01:02:53 --> 01:02:55
and there's a jaguar and there's a puma,

01:02:54 --> 01:02:57
and there's all these animals out there.

01:02:56 --> 01:02:59
And we're out in the night,

01:02:58 --> 01:03:00
in the inky, black night,

01:02:59 --> 01:03:02
in this ocean of darkness beneath the trees.

01:03:01 --> 01:03:04
And we're just exploring and getting to see everything

01:03:03 --> 01:03:05
and there's all these little eyes and heartbeats.

01:03:04 --> 01:03:07
I love the jungle at night, man.

01:03:06 --> 01:03:08
It's the most exciting thing.

01:03:07 --> 01:03:09
- You know, one of the things you do

01:03:08 --> 01:03:10
when you turn off the headlamp.

01:03:09 --> 01:03:12
- [Paul] Yeah. - Complete darkness.

01:03:11 --> 01:03:13
- [Paul] Yeah. - All around you.

01:03:13 --> 01:03:15
And just the sounds.

01:03:14 --> 01:03:19
- Everything you hear, the cicadas, the birds,

01:03:18 --> 01:03:21
they're all screaming about sex.

01:03:20 --> 01:03:21
- [Lex] Yeah. - All the time.

01:03:20 --> 01:03:23
So they're just trying to get laid.

01:03:22 --> 01:03:24
- [Lex] Yeah. - So all of them are

01:03:23 --> 01:03:25
making mating calls.

01:03:24 --> 01:03:26
Now the trick is to make your mating call

01:03:25 --> 01:03:28
without attracting a predator.

01:03:27 --> 01:03:29
- [Lex] Yeah.

01:03:28 --> 01:03:30
- But at night,

01:03:29 --> 01:03:35
what amazes me is that for us, it's so,

01:03:35 --> 01:03:40
from the caveman logic of it's hard to make fire here,

01:03:39 --> 01:03:42
it's hard to even light a fire here,

01:03:42 --> 01:03:48
to having this incredible beam of it, you know?

01:03:47 --> 01:03:50
All of a sudden we can look at the jungle

01:03:51 --> 01:03:53
and walk through that darkness.

01:03:52 --> 01:03:56
Then we're seeing the frogs on those leaves

01:03:55 --> 01:03:58
and the snakes moving through the undergrowth

01:03:57 --> 01:04:00
and the deer sneaking through the shadows.

01:03:59 --> 01:04:05
It's like, it's almost as supernatural as skydiving.

01:04:04 --> 01:04:07
It's a strange thing to be able to do,

01:04:06 --> 01:04:08
that technology allows us to do.

01:04:07 --> 01:04:10
We're doing something really complex

01:04:09 --> 01:04:12
and we're walking on trails that have been cleared for us,

01:04:11 --> 01:04:13
that we've planned out.

01:04:12 --> 01:04:15
And so walking through the jungle at night,

01:04:14 --> 01:04:19
you just get this freak show of biodiversity.

01:04:18 --> 01:04:21
And I'm addicted to it, I truly love it.

01:04:20 --> 01:04:24
- Except for the times over the last few days

01:04:23 --> 01:04:28
when we walked on, through jungle without a trail.

01:04:27 --> 01:04:29
- [Paul] Yeah. - And that's just

01:04:28 --> 01:04:29
a different experience.

01:04:29 --> 01:04:32
- Well, how would you categorize if somebody said,

01:04:31 --> 01:04:35
Lex, I think I'm gonna go for a hike through the jungle,

01:04:34 --> 01:04:37
not on the trail? - Yeah.

01:04:36 --> 01:04:38
- [Paul] What would you tell 'em?

01:04:37 --> 01:04:42
- Every step is really hard work.

01:04:41 --> 01:04:43
Every step is a puzzle.

01:04:42 --> 01:04:48
Every step is a full of possibility of hurting yourself

01:04:47 --> 01:04:50
in a multitude of ways.

01:04:49 --> 01:04:54
You just, a wasp nest under a leaf,

01:04:53 --> 01:04:57
a hole under a leaf on the ground

01:04:56 --> 01:04:59
where if you step in it, you're gonna break

01:04:58 --> 01:05:03
a knee, ankle, leg and go into,

01:05:02 --> 01:05:05
not be able to move for a long time.

01:05:04 --> 01:05:09
There's all kinds of ants that can hurt you a little

01:05:08 --> 01:05:11
or can hurt you a lot.

01:05:11 --> 01:05:16
Bullet ants, there's snakes and spiders and,

01:05:17 --> 01:05:21
oh, my favorite

01:05:20 --> 01:05:25
that I've gotten to know intimately is

01:05:24 --> 01:05:29
different plants with different defensive mechanisms.

01:05:28 --> 01:05:32
One of which is just spikes so sharp.

01:05:31 --> 01:05:33
You have, I don't know if you brought it,

01:05:32 --> 01:05:34
but there's.

01:05:34 --> 01:05:35
(Lex laughs)

01:05:34 --> 01:05:36
- I didn't bring it, I didn't bring it.

01:05:35 --> 01:05:37
Where's my club?

01:05:36 --> 01:05:39
- There's an epic club with spikes,

01:05:38 --> 01:05:42
but there's so many trees that have spikes on them.

01:05:41 --> 01:05:43
Sometimes they're obvious spikes,

01:05:42 --> 01:05:44
sometimes less than obvious spikes.

01:05:43 --> 01:05:47
And you know, it could be just an innocent,

01:05:46 --> 01:05:50
as you take a step through a dense jungle,

01:05:49 --> 01:05:54
it could be an innocent placing of a hand on that tree

01:05:53 --> 01:05:58
that could just completely transform your experience,

01:05:57 --> 01:06:03
your life, by penetrating your hand with like

01:06:02 --> 01:06:05
20, 30, 40, 50 spikes.

01:06:04 --> 01:06:06
- [Paul] Yeah. - And just

01:06:05 --> 01:06:08
changing everything.

01:06:07 --> 01:06:10
That's just a completely different experience

01:06:09 --> 01:06:12
than going on a trail where you,

01:06:11 --> 01:06:13
where you're a observer of the jungle

01:06:12 --> 01:06:15
versus the participant of it.

01:06:14 --> 01:06:18
- [Paul] Yeah. - And it truly is

01:06:17 --> 01:06:21
extreme hard work to take every single step.

01:06:20 --> 01:06:22
- Now just think about this.

01:06:21 --> 01:06:24
I think scientifically, 'cause people like to summarize,

01:06:23 --> 01:06:27
people like to get really, really sort of cavalier

01:06:26 --> 01:06:29
with our scientific progress.

01:06:28 --> 01:06:30
And they go, "You know, we've already explored the Amazon."

01:06:29 --> 01:06:31
It's like, well have we?

01:06:30 --> 01:06:35
Because in between each tributary is, you know,

01:06:34 --> 01:06:36
let's say just between some of them,

01:06:35 --> 01:06:39
let's just say 100 miles of unbroken forest.

01:06:39 --> 01:06:41
Who's explored that?

01:06:40 --> 01:06:42
- [Lex] Yeah. - Maybe some of the tribes

01:06:41 --> 01:06:44
have been there, maybe.

01:06:43 --> 01:06:46
Some areas, they haven't been.

01:06:45 --> 01:06:49
Now when you're talking about scientists,

01:06:48 --> 01:06:50
whether they're Indigenous scientists,

01:06:49 --> 01:06:52
Western scientists, whatever,

01:06:51 --> 01:06:55
so many of the areas in this jungle

01:06:54 --> 01:06:58
that is the size of the continental US

01:06:57 --> 01:06:59
still have not been accessed.

01:06:58 --> 01:07:02
And the places where people are doing research,

01:07:01 --> 01:07:03
see, I've been down here long enough.

01:07:02 --> 01:07:05
I see all the PhDs come down here

01:07:04 --> 01:07:07
and they all go to the same few research stations.

01:07:06 --> 01:07:09
They're safe, they have a bed.

01:07:08 --> 01:07:12
If you get heli-dropped into the middle of the jungle

01:07:11 --> 01:07:14
in the deepest, most remote parts,

01:07:13 --> 01:07:16
you're gonna find micro ecosystems.

01:07:15 --> 01:07:18
You're gonna see little species variations.

01:07:17 --> 01:07:20
You're gonna see a type of flower

01:07:19 --> 01:07:21
that JJ's never seen before,

01:07:20 --> 01:07:23
like what happened the other day.

01:07:22 --> 01:07:25
As you start walking through new patches of forest,

01:07:24 --> 01:07:28
you start finding new species and everything here changes.

01:07:27 --> 01:07:29
You just go a little bit upriver

01:07:28 --> 01:07:31
and the animals you see differ.

01:07:30 --> 01:07:33
You go on this side of the river

01:07:32 --> 01:07:34
sus on the north side of the river,

01:07:33 --> 01:07:36
there's two other species of primates there

01:07:35 --> 01:07:37
that don't exist here.

01:07:36 --> 01:07:39
And that's in the mammal paper that we did

01:07:38 --> 01:07:42
with the emperor tamarins and the pygmy marmosets

01:07:41 --> 01:07:43
that the rangers found.

01:07:42 --> 01:07:47
- Yeah, the mammal paper is looking at the diversity

01:07:46 --> 01:07:52
of life in this one region of the Amazon.

01:07:51 --> 01:07:55
What kind of, can you talk more about that paper,

01:07:54 --> 01:07:58
"Mammal Diversity along the Las Piedras River"?

01:07:57 --> 01:07:59
- Once again, the mammal paper.

01:07:58 --> 01:08:01
Pat Champagne, the prodigy,

01:08:01 --> 01:08:04
he was sort of leading on this

01:08:03 --> 01:08:05
with a bunch of other scientists

01:08:04 --> 01:08:07
who have worked in the region,

01:08:06 --> 01:08:10
including Holly O'Donnell out of Oxford, myself.

01:08:09 --> 01:08:12
I really just made a few observations.

01:08:11 --> 01:08:14
The Junglekeepers rangers got featured

01:08:13 --> 01:08:16
because they're the ones that spotted a pygmy marmoset

01:08:15 --> 01:08:20
hat had previously been unrecorded on the river.

01:08:19 --> 01:08:22
I got to contribute because I had

01:08:21 --> 01:08:26
the only photograph that I believe anyone has

01:08:25 --> 01:08:28
of an emperor tamarin on this river.

01:08:27 --> 01:08:32
It's the first proof of emperor tamarin on this river.

01:08:31 --> 01:08:33
And that's exciting.

01:08:32 --> 01:08:36
It's exciting because, you know,

01:08:35 --> 01:08:38
you'll, you can post a picture

01:08:37 --> 01:08:41
or share a scientific observation or write about something.

01:08:40 --> 01:08:43
And then what happens is you get these

01:08:42 --> 01:08:46
like couch experts, these armchair experts

01:08:45 --> 01:08:47
who will come and say, you know,

01:08:46 --> 01:08:50
"No, no, you don't get blue and yellow macaws there.

01:08:49 --> 01:08:51
I can tell from my bird book,

01:08:50 --> 01:08:52
it says they're not there."

01:08:51 --> 01:08:54
And they'll tell you you're wrong, you know?

01:08:53 --> 01:08:56
No, you don't get wooly monkeys there or emperor tamarins.

01:08:55 --> 01:09:00
It's like, but we have proof.

01:08:59 --> 01:09:01
And so we're coming together to try

01:09:00 --> 01:09:02
and add to that knowledge.

01:09:01 --> 01:09:05
- My general sort of amateur experience of the species

01:09:04 --> 01:09:07
I've encountered here is like,

01:09:06 --> 01:09:08
this should not exist.

01:09:07 --> 01:09:11
Whatever this is, this is not real.

01:09:11 --> 01:09:14
This is CGI, like what?

01:09:13 --> 01:09:16
Just the colors, the weirdness.

01:09:15 --> 01:09:17
I mean, there's,

01:09:16 --> 01:09:21
I think I called it the Paris Hilton caterpillar

01:09:20 --> 01:09:22
because it's like furry.

01:09:21 --> 01:09:23
It looks like a one of those.

01:09:22 --> 01:09:24
- [Paul] It looks like Paris Hilton's dog.

01:09:23 --> 01:09:25
- Like yeah, yeah.

01:09:24 --> 01:09:28
It's like really furry and it's transparent

01:09:27 --> 01:09:30
and sort of it's transparent.

01:09:29 --> 01:09:32
All you see is this white, beautiful fur.

01:09:31 --> 01:09:34
It's just like this caterpillar.

01:09:33 --> 01:09:35
It doesn't look real. - Yeah.

01:09:34 --> 01:09:37
- Do you think there are species,

01:09:36 --> 01:09:39
like how many species have we not discovered?

01:09:38 --> 01:09:42
And is there a species that are like extremely badass

01:09:41 --> 01:09:44
that we haven't discovered yet?

01:09:43 --> 01:09:49
- If you look up how many trees are

01:09:48 --> 01:09:50
in the Amazon rainforest,

01:09:49 --> 01:09:55
it's something in the order of 400 billion trees.

01:09:54 --> 01:10:00
There's something like 70 to 80,000 species of plants,

01:09:59 --> 01:10:02
individual types of plants here.

01:10:01 --> 01:10:05
1,500 species of trees.

01:10:04 --> 01:10:09
It's so vast that it's comparable,

01:10:08 --> 01:10:14
like the scale is like only comparable to the universe

01:10:14 --> 01:10:17
in terms of stars and galaxies

01:10:16 --> 01:10:20
and for the sheer immensity of it.

01:10:19 --> 01:10:25
And so we're describing new species every year

01:10:24 --> 01:10:27
and just walking on the trail at night,

01:10:26 --> 01:10:29
you and I have seen, you know,

01:10:28 --> 01:10:31
you see a tiny, little spider hidden in a crevice.

01:10:30 --> 01:10:35
And has the scientific eye ever seen that spider before?

01:10:34 --> 01:10:36
Has it been documented?

01:10:35 --> 01:10:38
Do we know anything about its lifecycle?

01:10:37 --> 01:10:41
There's still so much that's here

01:10:40 --> 01:10:42
that is completely unknown.

01:10:41 --> 01:10:44
You know, we have pictures of all these butterflies.

01:10:43 --> 01:10:45
Somebody went out with a butterfly net

01:10:44 --> 01:10:48
and caught these butterflies, took a picture of it,

01:10:47 --> 01:10:50
gave it a name, put it in a butterfly book.

01:10:49 --> 01:10:51
What do we know?

01:10:50 --> 01:10:53
What host plant do they use for their caterpillars?

01:10:52 --> 01:10:55
What's their geographical range?

01:10:54 --> 01:10:57
What do we actually know?

01:10:56 --> 01:10:57
Not that much.

01:10:56 --> 01:11:00
So are there creatures out here that haven't been described?

01:10:59 --> 01:11:01
Absolutely.

01:11:00 --> 01:11:03
- And some of them could be

01:11:02 --> 01:11:07
xtremely effective predators in a niche environment.

01:11:06 --> 01:11:08
- Yeah, absolutely.

01:11:07 --> 01:11:11
I mean, certainly in the canopy,

01:11:10 --> 01:11:14
50% of the life in a rainforest is in the canopy.

01:11:13 --> 01:11:17
And we've had very limited access to the canopy

01:11:16 --> 01:11:18
for all of history.

01:11:18 --> 01:11:19
You know, if you wanted to get up

01:11:18 --> 01:11:20
into the rainforest canopy,

01:11:19 --> 01:11:22
you basically have to climb a vine.

01:11:21 --> 01:11:24
Or with scientists, when I was a kid,

01:11:23 --> 01:11:26
I always used to see them with like the slingshots

01:11:25 --> 01:11:27
or the bow and arrows.

01:11:26 --> 01:11:30
They would shoot a piece of paracord over a branch,

01:11:29 --> 01:11:31
pull the rope up and then, you know,

01:11:30 --> 01:11:32
do the ascension thing.

01:11:31 --> 01:11:34
And then you're up in this tree

01:11:33 --> 01:11:38
getting swarmed by sweat bees, getting stung by wasps.

01:11:37 --> 01:11:40
You're trying to do science up there in that environment.

01:11:39 --> 01:11:41
It's incredibly hostile.

01:11:40 --> 01:11:44
And so having canopy platforms,

01:11:43 --> 01:11:47
I actually met a guy at a French film festival

01:11:46 --> 01:11:50
who had used hot air balloons

01:11:49 --> 01:11:52
to float over the canopy of the Amazon

01:11:51 --> 01:11:54
and then lay these big nets

01:11:53 --> 01:11:57
over the broccoli of the trees.

01:11:56 --> 01:11:58
And the nets were dense enough

01:11:57 --> 01:11:59
that humans could walk on the nets

01:11:58 --> 01:12:02
and then reach through and pull cactuses and lizards

01:12:01 --> 01:12:03
and snakes, whatever,

01:12:02 --> 01:12:04
just take specimens from the canopy.

01:12:03 --> 01:12:06
That's how difficult it is,

01:12:05 --> 01:12:09
that scientists have resorted to using hot air balloons.

01:12:10 --> 01:12:12
And so having a tree house,

01:12:11 --> 01:12:14
having canopy platforms,

01:12:13 --> 01:12:15
having, it's starting to get,

01:12:14 --> 01:12:17
there's starting to be more and more access

01:12:16 --> 01:12:18
to the rainforest canopy.

01:12:17 --> 01:12:22
And so we're beginning to log more data.

01:12:21 --> 01:12:25
You know, we've even observed in our tree house,

01:12:24 --> 01:12:26
which is supposed to be the tallest in the world,

01:12:25 --> 01:12:29
we're seeing lizards that we don't see on the ground,

01:12:28 --> 01:12:32
lizards that have never been documented on this river.

01:12:31 --> 01:12:34
Like, we're seeing snakes where they're saying,

01:12:33 --> 01:12:36
we saw this snake inside a crevice on that tree

01:12:35 --> 01:12:39
in the strangler fig and we don't know what it is.

01:12:39 --> 01:12:42
It's just people haven't been up there.

01:12:41 --> 01:12:45
- And that's where a lot of the monkeys are.

01:12:44 --> 01:12:48
That's where there's just a lot of dynamic life up there.

01:12:47 --> 01:12:51
- Yeah, I mean when you wake up in the canopy

01:12:50 --> 01:12:54
in the morning, in the Amazon rainforest,

01:12:53 --> 01:12:56
as soon as that, the darkness lifts,

01:12:55 --> 01:12:59
as soon as that purple comes in the East in the morning,

01:12:58 --> 01:13:00
the howler monkeys start up.

01:12:59 --> 01:13:02
- [Lex] Yeah. - And then the parrots

01:13:01 --> 01:13:04
tart up, and then the tinamous start going,

01:13:03 --> 01:13:05
and then the macaws start going.

01:13:04 --> 01:13:05
And pretty soon everybody's going,

01:13:04 --> 01:13:08
and the spider monkey groups are all calling to each other.

01:13:07 --> 01:13:10
And it's just the whole dawn chorus starts

01:13:09 --> 01:13:11
and it's so exciting.

01:13:10 --> 01:13:12
- So you're saying when they're screaming,

01:13:11 --> 01:13:14
it's usually about sex.

01:13:13 --> 01:13:16
- Sex or territory, usually.

01:13:15 --> 01:13:18
- Mm, sex and violence.

01:13:17 --> 01:13:20
Or implied violence or the threat of violence.

01:13:19 --> 01:13:22
- Yeah, I mean howler monkeys in the morning,

01:13:21 --> 01:13:23
they're letting other groups know

01:13:22 --> 01:13:24
this is where we're at.

01:13:23 --> 01:13:25
- [Lex] Yeah. - We're gonna be foraging

01:13:24 --> 01:13:27
over here, you better stay away.

01:13:26 --> 01:13:29
And so it's a little bit respectful as well.

01:13:28 --> 01:13:31
There is order in the chaos.

01:13:30 --> 01:13:33
- So just speaking of screaming,

01:13:32 --> 01:13:36
macaws are like these beautiful creatures.

01:13:35 --> 01:13:39
They're lifelong partners.

01:13:38 --> 01:13:40
They stick together.

01:13:39 --> 01:13:42
So there's you often see, they're monogamous,

01:13:41 --> 01:13:43
you see two of them together.

01:13:42 --> 01:13:45
But when they communicate,

01:13:44 --> 01:13:48
their love language seems to be very loud screaming.

01:13:47 --> 01:13:51
- [Paul] Yeah. - What do you learn

01:13:50 --> 01:13:53
about relationships from macaws?

01:13:52 --> 01:13:55
- That it can be loud and rough and still be loving.

01:13:54 --> 01:13:57
- And still be loving.

01:13:56 --> 01:13:58
But is that interesting to you,

01:13:57 --> 01:14:00
that there's like monogamy in some species,

01:13:59 --> 01:14:02
that they, they're lifelong partners

01:14:01 --> 01:14:04
and then there's like total lack of monogamy

01:14:03 --> 01:14:05
in other species?

01:14:04 --> 01:14:06
- It's all interesting.

01:14:05 --> 01:14:09
I mean, there's the anti-monogamy crew who's like

01:14:08 --> 01:14:11
you know, we were never meant to be monogamous.

01:14:10 --> 01:14:12
We're supposed to just be animals.

01:14:11 --> 01:14:15
And then there's the other side of the crew that's like,

01:14:14 --> 01:14:16
we were meant to be monogamous.

01:14:15 --> 01:14:18
We are monogamous creatures.

01:14:17 --> 01:14:20
That's what God wanted between a man and a woman.

01:14:19 --> 01:14:22
And then other people are like, yeah,

01:14:21 --> 01:14:24
but I know about these two gay penguins.

01:14:23 --> 01:14:25
And so that's natural too.

01:14:24 --> 01:14:26
- [Lex] Yeah. - And so then everyone tries

01:14:25 --> 01:14:29
to draw their identity.

01:14:28 --> 01:14:31
They're trying to justify their identity off of the

01:14:30 --> 01:14:32
not the laws of nature.

01:14:31 --> 01:14:34
So the fact that macaws are monogamous

01:14:33 --> 01:14:36
really doesn't have anything to do with anybody

01:14:35 --> 01:14:39
except for that it's beneficial for them to work together

01:14:38 --> 01:14:40
to raise chicks.

01:14:39 --> 01:14:41
It's difficult.

01:14:40 --> 01:14:45
They rely on ironwood trees or aguaje palms

01:14:44 --> 01:14:48
and it's difficult to find the right hole in a tree.

01:14:47 --> 01:14:49
There's only so much macaw real estate.

01:14:48 --> 01:14:51
And so they need to use those holes.

01:14:51 --> 01:14:55
And each one of those ancient trees,

01:14:54 --> 01:14:56
it's usually 500 years or more,

01:14:55 --> 01:15:00
is a valuable macaw generating site in the forest.

01:14:59 --> 01:15:02
And so if those trees go down,

01:15:01 --> 01:15:05
you lose exponential amounts of macaws

01:15:04 --> 01:15:06
and that's how you get endangered species.

01:15:05 --> 01:15:08
And so that's why we're trying to protect

01:15:07 --> 01:15:09
he ironwood trees.

01:15:09 --> 01:15:11
- Another ridiculous question.

01:15:10 --> 01:15:13
- Tell me. - [Lex] If every

01:15:12 --> 01:15:15
jungle creature was the same size.

01:15:14 --> 01:15:16
- Oh boy.

01:15:15 --> 01:15:17
- Who would be the new apex predator,

01:15:16 --> 01:15:20
the new alpha at the top of the food chain?

01:15:19 --> 01:15:22
- Dude, that's like Super Smash Brothers of the jungle.

01:15:21 --> 01:15:24
That's incredible. - [Lex] Oh yeah, yeah.

01:15:23 --> 01:15:25
- Like, bullet ants.

01:15:24 --> 01:15:27
If you had a bullet ant that was the size.

01:15:26 --> 01:15:28
- Yeah.

01:15:28 --> 01:15:31
Can it be like a tournament?

01:15:30 --> 01:15:33
- So everyone is pound for pound ratioed.

01:15:32 --> 01:15:34
- [Lex] Yeah. - For efficiency.

01:15:33 --> 01:15:37
So you have basically like a six-foot bullet ant

01:15:36 --> 01:15:40
versus a huge black caiman versus an anaconda

01:15:39 --> 01:15:43
versus ocelots who are the size of jaguars versus.

01:15:42 --> 01:15:47
- Yeah, well let's go bullet ant versus black caiman,

01:15:46 --> 01:15:48
same size. - But they're comparable size.

01:15:47 --> 01:15:49
- [Lex] Same size.

01:15:49 --> 01:15:51
- I don't know, man.

01:15:50 --> 01:15:52
I never thought about it.

01:15:51 --> 01:15:54
I mean, bullet and has these giant.

01:15:53 --> 01:15:56
- [Lex] Yeah. - Giant, giant mandibles

01:15:55 --> 01:15:57
that could probably grab the black caiman.

01:15:56 --> 01:15:59
And then at that amount of venom,

01:15:58 --> 01:16:00
you're talking about a bucket of venom

01:16:00 --> 01:16:02
going into that black caiman.

01:16:01 --> 01:16:04
Black caiman's gonna get paralyzed immediately.

01:16:03 --> 01:16:07
- Well, insects have just a tremendous amount

01:16:06 --> 01:16:08
of like, strength.

01:16:07 --> 01:16:09
I don't know how they generate,

01:16:08 --> 01:16:10
what the geometry of that is.

01:16:09 --> 01:16:12
The natural world can't create that same kind of power

01:16:11 --> 01:16:14
in the bigger thing, it seems like.

01:16:13 --> 01:16:16
- [Paul] It seems like, yeah. - It seems like ants

01:16:15 --> 01:16:18
and like just these tiny creatures are

01:16:17 --> 01:16:20
the ones that are able to have that much strength.

01:16:19 --> 01:16:21
I don't know how that works,

01:16:20 --> 01:16:23
with the physics of that. - Yeah, so like an ant,

01:16:22 --> 01:16:25
a leafcutter ant lifting that leaf.

01:16:24 --> 01:16:26
That doesn't make any sense.

01:16:25 --> 01:16:28
- Yeah, it doesn't make any sense.

01:16:27 --> 01:16:29
- It doesn't make any sense.

01:16:28 --> 01:16:30
- I don't know if that's a limit of physics.

01:16:29 --> 01:16:33
I think it's just a limit of evolution of how that works.

01:16:32 --> 01:16:35
- One of the most interesting limits that I heard

01:16:34 --> 01:16:37
somebody talking about recently was

01:16:36 --> 01:16:40
the reason that dinosaurs didn't get bigger, even bigger.

01:16:39 --> 01:16:40
Because

01:16:41 --> 01:16:44
that's the conditions on Earth were favorable towards it

01:16:43 --> 01:16:47
was that at some point their eggs reached,

01:16:46 --> 01:16:49
there's physical limits that their eggs reached the size.

01:16:48 --> 01:16:50
- [Lex] Yeah. - The eggs were so big

01:16:49 --> 01:16:53
that eggs need to breathe for the embryo to survive.

01:16:52 --> 01:16:55
And their eggs reached a limit where

01:16:54 --> 01:16:57
in order to have a shell that could hold

01:16:56 --> 01:17:00
the mass of the liquid and the young dinosaur,

01:16:59 --> 01:17:03
if they got bigger, it wouldn't be permeable anymore.

01:17:03 --> 01:17:05
And I thought that was so interesting

01:17:04 --> 01:17:07
'cause the entire size of physical creatures was

01:17:06 --> 01:17:09
determined by how thick shell can be

01:17:08 --> 01:17:13
fore it breaks or before it can't pass air through it.

01:17:12 --> 01:17:17
- Yeah, there might be a lot of that like biophysics limits.

01:17:16 --> 01:17:19
- That's fascinating stuff.

01:17:18 --> 01:17:22
- Just like the interplay between biology,

01:17:21 --> 01:17:25
chemistry and physics of like a life form,

01:17:24 --> 01:17:26
'cause like this thing,

01:17:25 --> 01:17:29
there's a lot involved in creating a single living organism

01:17:28 --> 01:17:31
that could survive in this world.

01:17:30 --> 01:17:34
And bigger, you know, being big is not always good,

01:17:33 --> 01:17:37
but being a big creature, it's for many reasons,

01:17:36 --> 01:17:38
like you were saying,

01:17:37 --> 01:17:40
the big creatures seem to be going extinct.

01:17:39 --> 01:17:41
- [Paul] Yeah. - For many reasons,

01:17:40 --> 01:17:45
but in the human world is because they're seen to be

01:17:44 --> 01:17:46
of higher value.

01:17:46 --> 01:17:49
- Given the current size of the jungle,

01:17:48 --> 01:17:54
I think that the MVP, the pound for pound GOAT is ocelots.

01:17:53 --> 01:17:56
You know, you're talking about like a mid-size,

01:17:55 --> 01:18:00
40, 50 pound cat that can climb.

01:18:00 --> 01:18:02
That does, unlike a jaguar,

01:18:01 --> 01:18:05
every time it hunts, it's going after a deer,

01:18:04 --> 01:18:06
it catches a deer,

01:18:05 --> 01:18:08
the deer could hit it with its antlers,

01:18:07 --> 01:18:10
it could tear it with its hooves.

01:18:09 --> 01:18:12
It's risking its life for that meal.

01:18:11 --> 01:18:15
An ocelot, ocelots walk around at night

01:18:14 --> 01:18:19
and they climb a tree, eat a whole bunch of eggs,

01:18:18 --> 01:18:22
eat the mother bird too, kill a snake,

01:18:21 --> 01:18:24
maybe mess around and eat a baby caiman.

01:18:23 --> 01:18:26
They can have whatever they like

01:18:25 --> 01:18:29
and they're sleek enough and smart enough

01:18:28 --> 01:18:31
to get away from predators.

01:18:30 --> 01:18:33
They don't really have predators.

01:18:33 --> 01:18:35
And so they're sort of,

01:18:34 --> 01:18:36
they sort of occupy this perfect niche

01:18:35 --> 01:18:39
where they can hunt small prey in high quantity

01:18:38 --> 01:18:41
without taking on big risks.

01:18:40 --> 01:18:43
And so if you had to choose an animal to be,

01:18:42 --> 01:18:45
it'd probably be like an ocelot

01:18:44 --> 01:18:46
or I would say giant river otters,

01:18:45 --> 01:18:50
which are so damn cool because they're,

01:18:49 --> 01:18:52
the locals call 'em (speaks foreign language),

01:18:51 --> 01:18:54
river wolves because they're so tough

01:18:53 --> 01:18:56
and they're so social and they're so like us

01:18:55 --> 01:18:59
because they're intensely familial groups.

01:18:58 --> 01:19:01
They live in holes by the sides of lakes

01:19:00 --> 01:19:02
and they swim through the water

01:19:01 --> 01:19:03
and they catch fish all day long.

01:19:02 --> 01:19:06
Piranhas, they eat 'em, just like the scales go flying

01:19:05 --> 01:19:08
as they eat these piranhas.

01:19:07 --> 01:19:10
And they're so joyous in the way they swim

01:19:09 --> 01:19:12
and they have friends and they have family.

01:19:11 --> 01:19:14
And they, I think it would be,

01:19:13 --> 01:19:16
I think we could relate to being a river otter really

01:19:15 --> 01:19:18
'cause I can't picture being a cat

01:19:18 --> 01:19:21
and being so solitary

01:19:20 --> 01:19:24
and just marching along a 15 mile route

01:19:23 --> 01:19:26
and making sure there's no other cats

01:19:25 --> 01:19:29
coming in on your territory and marking that territory.

01:19:28 --> 01:19:33
It seems very solo and very cat-like.

01:19:32 --> 01:19:35
- It's a lonely existence.

01:19:34 --> 01:19:36
- [Paul] Lonely existence. - And we humans are

01:19:35 --> 01:19:38
social beings. - We're so social.

01:19:37 --> 01:19:40
And so to me, river otter is just like having

01:19:39 --> 01:19:41
a big, Italian family.

01:19:40 --> 01:19:43
You're like constantly eating, you're freaking out.

01:19:42 --> 01:19:45
You know, just like causing problems with the black caiman.

01:19:44 --> 01:19:47
- Take down a black caiman.

01:19:46 --> 01:19:48
- Yeah, start a street fight.

01:19:47 --> 01:19:50
- Yeah, yeah, yeah, it's a family thing.

01:19:49 --> 01:19:51
You mentioned piranhas. - [Paul] Yeah.

01:19:50 --> 01:19:52
- What do you think, you know,

01:19:51 --> 01:19:54
they're a source of a lot of fear for people.

01:19:53 --> 01:19:56
What do you find beautiful and fascinating

01:19:55 --> 01:19:57
about these creatures?

01:19:56 --> 01:19:58
They're also kind of social,

01:19:57 --> 01:20:01
or at least they hunt and operate in groups.

01:20:00 --> 01:20:03
- Yeah, not in the mammalian way though.

01:20:02 --> 01:20:06
Piranhas are in large schools,

01:20:05 --> 01:20:07
but fish are so different.

01:20:06 --> 01:20:09
Like if you, I can talk to you all day

01:20:08 --> 01:20:12
about how much I'd love to be an otter also.

01:20:11 --> 01:20:13
- [Lex] Yeah. - Going back

01:20:12 --> 01:20:14
to the fighting thing,

01:20:13 --> 01:20:16
otters and weasels, Mustelidae,

01:20:15 --> 01:20:18
tend to be very loose in their skin.

01:20:17 --> 01:20:19
So if you grab an otter,

01:20:18 --> 01:20:20
it can still rotate around to bite you.

01:20:19 --> 01:20:21
- [Lex] Yeah. - So it's like

01:20:20 --> 01:20:23
if I grab you by the back, you're stuck.

01:20:22 --> 01:20:24
You know, like we can't,

01:20:23 --> 01:20:26
you grab them by the skin.

01:20:25 --> 01:20:28
- [Lex] Yeah. - They can rotate around

01:20:27 --> 01:20:29
and just shred you apart.

01:20:28 --> 01:20:31
So they're really cool fighters.

01:20:31 --> 01:20:35
Piranha fish, fish, I don't, you know,

01:20:34 --> 01:20:38
I don't identify with fish in terms like that.

01:20:37 --> 01:20:41
I think living out here has made me think of fish as

01:20:41 --> 01:20:46
kind of rapid food that can or can't be gotten.

01:20:45 --> 01:20:48
Like, you know, to me a piranha is just,

01:20:47 --> 01:20:49
is when I see a piranha, I think about how I want,

01:20:48 --> 01:20:51
how I want it to taste.

01:20:50 --> 01:20:54
- Yeah, so like fish is a food source

01:20:53 --> 01:20:55
of so many creatures in the jungle.

01:20:54 --> 01:20:57
- [Paul] Yeah. - So they're primarily

01:20:56 --> 01:20:58
a food source.

01:20:57 --> 01:20:59
But piranhas are, I mean.

01:20:58 --> 01:21:01
- [Paul] Predator as well. - They're predators.

01:21:00 --> 01:21:02
They're serious predators.

01:21:01 --> 01:21:03
- They are serious predators.

01:21:02 --> 01:21:05
I found a baby black caiman not that long ago,

01:21:04 --> 01:21:07
and he was missing all of his toes

01:21:06 --> 01:21:09
'cause the piranhas had eaten them off.

01:21:08 --> 01:21:10
It was really sad.

01:21:09 --> 01:21:10
He just had these stumps

01:21:09 --> 01:21:12
and he was swimming around the water and I was like,

01:21:11 --> 01:21:14
you are not gonna make it.

01:21:13 --> 01:21:15
He was like eight inches.

01:21:14 --> 01:21:16
And he was such a cute, little puppy.

01:21:15 --> 01:21:18
He had those big eyes.

01:21:17 --> 01:21:20
And I was just like, "Man, you already are

01:21:19 --> 01:21:20
missing all your toes."

01:21:19 --> 01:21:23
I was like, just a matter of time.

01:21:22 --> 01:21:26
No, he can't get away, so some big agami heron's

01:21:25 --> 01:21:29
gonna come and just nail him, pop him down his throat.

01:21:28 --> 01:21:30
And that's the end of that for the caiman.

01:21:29 --> 01:21:32
- I mean, nature is metal.

01:21:31 --> 01:21:34
- Nature sure as shit is metal.

01:21:33 --> 01:21:36
- Bite off a little bit and then makes you vulnerable.

01:21:35 --> 01:21:38
And then that vulnerability is exploited

01:21:37 --> 01:21:41
by some other species and then that's it, that's the end.

01:21:40 --> 01:21:42
- Yeah, but humans are brutal too.

01:21:41 --> 01:21:45
Like that story we heard about that guy

01:21:44 --> 01:21:49
the other day who caught a stingray on a fishing hook,

01:21:48 --> 01:21:52
chopped its tail off to make it safe for humans,

01:21:51 --> 01:21:54
cut a piece of the stingray off

01:21:53 --> 01:21:55
so he could use it for bait

01:21:54 --> 01:21:57
and then threw the live fish back in the river.

01:21:56 --> 01:22:01
Like, to me that is incomprehensible amounts of cruelty

01:22:01 --> 01:22:04
with flawed logic in every direction.

01:22:03 --> 01:22:07
Like, if you're gonna use the thing as bait, use it as bait.

01:22:06 --> 01:22:08
If you're gonna remove its tail,

01:22:07 --> 01:22:10
well then just kill it all together.

01:22:09 --> 01:22:13
- [Lex] Yeah. - Or if you wanna save

01:22:12 --> 01:22:14
the animal and not kill it,

01:22:13 --> 01:22:16
then don't maim it before you return it to its,

01:22:15 --> 01:22:18
it was such a weird.

01:22:18 --> 01:22:20
- So if you kill an animal,

01:22:19 --> 01:22:22
you want to use it to as fullest by using it

01:22:21 --> 01:22:24
as a food source. - [Paul] Yeah.

01:22:23 --> 01:22:26
- By cooking it, by, you know, eating every part of it,

01:22:25 --> 01:22:27
all that kind of stuff.

01:22:26 --> 01:22:29
- Yeah, so we have, we've been eating paco.

01:22:28 --> 01:22:31
- [Lex] Yeah. - [Paul] In your time here.

01:22:30 --> 01:22:32
- [Lex] Fried paco is great.

01:22:31 --> 01:22:33
- [Paul] Amazing, it's delicious.

01:22:33 --> 01:22:35
Full of, you could tell it makes you healthy.

01:22:34 --> 01:22:36
- Yeah. - I feel like

01:22:35 --> 01:22:38
we'd better work out so we can go harder in the jungle.

01:22:37 --> 01:22:40
- Yeah. - And so

01:22:40 --> 01:22:44
a few months ago in August when the river was down,

01:22:43 --> 01:22:46
it was, there was a day that the river was clear.

01:22:45 --> 01:22:48
And a friend of mine, Victor,

01:22:47 --> 01:22:51
who's married to a native girl, he said,

01:22:50 --> 01:22:53
"It's time to go paco fishing."

01:22:52 --> 01:22:54
And at the time we were stuck out here

01:22:53 --> 01:22:56
and we had no resupply.

01:22:56 --> 01:22:58
Everybody was busy.

01:22:57 --> 01:23:00
And so everyone was demoralized.

01:22:59 --> 01:23:02
The staff was hungry, we were hungry.

01:23:01 --> 01:23:04
And it really became this thing of like,

01:23:03 --> 01:23:06
"Hey, go catch us some paco."

01:23:05 --> 01:23:07
They were working on the trails.

01:23:06 --> 01:23:08
They were installing the solar.

01:23:07 --> 01:23:10
We were working hard and we didn't have food.

01:23:09 --> 01:23:12
And so we went out to the river

01:23:11 --> 01:23:14
and what we did was we went upriver,

01:23:13 --> 01:23:16
we camped on the beach.

01:23:16 --> 01:23:20
And in the morning, Victor's wife was

01:23:19 --> 01:23:23
canoeing with the paddle, dead quiet.

01:23:22 --> 01:23:25
Don't let the paddle touch the wooden boat.

01:23:25 --> 01:23:28
Nikita was balanced in the middle of the thing.

01:23:27 --> 01:23:30
Victor's on the front with this huge fishing rod.

01:23:30 --> 01:23:32
And I'm sitting there and he goes,

01:23:31 --> 01:23:33
"I'll catch the first one, you catch the second one."

01:23:32 --> 01:23:35
And he's got this huge fishing rod

01:23:34 --> 01:23:38
and a piece of half rotten meat from the day before.

01:23:37 --> 01:23:40
And he's smacking it against the.

01:23:39 --> 01:23:42
6:00 AM, he's just letting it smack against the water.

01:23:41 --> 01:23:46
And I'm going, and we're floating down the river

01:23:45 --> 01:23:48
and I'm going, this is not gonna work.

01:23:47 --> 01:23:49
And we're floating and we're floating.

01:23:48 --> 01:23:51
And a half hour passes and I'm going, it's dawn.

01:23:50 --> 01:23:53
I wanna go back to sleep.

01:23:52 --> 01:23:55
I'm such an, I'm just not a morning person.

01:23:54 --> 01:23:57
And all of a sudden a fish hits that line,

01:23:56 --> 01:24:00
almost pulls this man off of his feet

01:23:59 --> 01:24:02
and he swings the thing in, the fish comes on the boat.

01:24:01 --> 01:24:05
And then I realized he's got a big metal mallet on the boat

01:24:04 --> 01:24:07
so that you could try to shut that fish off.

01:24:06 --> 01:24:12
And it's this huge, oar-shaped, thick, muscular paco.

01:24:12 --> 01:24:16
And as soon as I saw that fish, I just thought

01:24:15 --> 01:24:20
wow, the strongest of this species

01:24:19 --> 01:24:24
for millions of years have been swimming in this river.

01:24:23 --> 01:24:25
And suddenly we've,

01:24:24 --> 01:24:28
through this incredible combination of the boat

01:24:27 --> 01:24:30
and the cord and the hook,

01:24:29 --> 01:24:31
none of which we made,

01:24:30 --> 01:24:34
and the skill that he had from knowing how to fish a paco,

01:24:33 --> 01:24:37
'cause otherwise no chance that you're getting that fish.

01:24:36 --> 01:24:39
They hide, they're very, very suspicious

01:24:38 --> 01:24:40
of what you're doing.

01:24:39 --> 01:24:43
We had gotten this fish onto the boat and plume,

01:24:42 --> 01:24:46
you hammer it like a caveman, boom, it doesn't die.

01:24:45 --> 01:24:48
Boom, you have to crush its skull.

01:24:47 --> 01:24:49
And now you have this fish

01:24:48 --> 01:24:52
and you're holding this genetic material,

01:24:51 --> 01:24:54
this sustenance for your life

01:24:53 --> 01:24:57
that has been developing since the dinosaur times.

01:24:56 --> 01:25:00
It's so beautiful.

01:24:59 --> 01:25:03
The act, the sacred act of eating that,

01:25:02 --> 01:25:06
of the fish, of the competition with the fish.

01:25:05 --> 01:25:08
And we spent the morning fishing.

01:25:07 --> 01:25:09
We got three pacos,

01:25:08 --> 01:25:12
three huge, giant vegetarian piranha.

01:25:11 --> 01:25:15
And I just remember touching them with so much reverence,

01:25:14 --> 01:25:17
thinking about the incredible history

01:25:16 --> 01:25:20
and how that before these rivers existed,

01:25:19 --> 01:25:23
those pacos were swimming through the water

01:25:22 --> 01:25:28
and trying to survive through history,

01:25:27 --> 01:25:30
through history until this,

01:25:29 --> 01:25:32
until we took just a few.

01:25:31 --> 01:25:33
And we did it respectfully

01:25:32 --> 01:25:36
and we did it when we needed it most,

01:25:35 --> 01:25:37
not at a time when it was just for fun.

01:25:36 --> 01:25:39
And it was really, really special.

01:25:38 --> 01:25:42
- Well, humans using them for sustenance,

01:25:41 --> 01:25:43
there's a collaboration there.

01:25:42 --> 01:25:46
That's something also that I've seen in the jungle.

01:25:45 --> 01:25:48
That there's creatures using each other

01:25:47 --> 01:25:52
and it's like a dance of either of mutually using

01:25:51 --> 01:25:56
each other or it's parasitic or symbiotic.

01:25:55 --> 01:25:59
It's interesting, like there's a

01:25:58 --> 01:26:03
medicinal plant you grabbed that was full of ants.

01:26:02 --> 01:26:05
- Yeah. - That were like trying

01:26:04 --> 01:26:07
to murder you by biting,

01:26:07 --> 01:26:10
but they were defending the plant that they were using

01:26:09 --> 01:26:11
for whatever purpose.

01:26:10 --> 01:26:13
But there's a clear dance there

01:26:12 --> 01:26:15
of the ants using the plant and the plant existing,

01:26:14 --> 01:26:18
therefore other applications and other use for humans.

01:26:18 --> 01:26:21
And there's that kind of circle of life happening.

01:26:20 --> 01:26:23
But the ants were a defense.

01:26:22 --> 01:26:26
So the plant didn't have its own defense mechanism.

01:26:25 --> 01:26:30
The ants, the army of ants was there to protect the plant.

01:26:29 --> 01:26:32
- Mm-hmm, and did you actually,

01:26:31 --> 01:26:34
when you, remember, we put our backpacks down

01:26:33 --> 01:26:36
at that one spot and it was like

01:26:35 --> 01:26:37
the ants got on your backpack.

01:26:36 --> 01:26:39
And I said, "Oh shit, this is that tree."

01:26:38 --> 01:26:40
Did you actually get bitten by one of those?

01:26:39 --> 01:26:42
'Cause they're incredibly painful.

01:26:41 --> 01:26:43
- [Lex] Yeah. - The Tangarana one,

01:26:42 --> 01:26:45
they're like. - Yeah, yeah,

01:26:44 --> 01:26:47
surprisingly painful 'cause they're small.

01:26:46 --> 01:26:49
They, there's nothing like,

01:26:48 --> 01:26:51
luckily have not been bitten by a bullet ant yet.

01:26:50 --> 01:26:53
- Yeah, but it's just, it's amazing

01:26:52 --> 01:26:55
because they live inside the tree.

01:26:54 --> 01:26:57
The tree comes standard with holes in it

01:26:56 --> 01:27:01
hat allow the ants to move and to exist safe.

01:27:01 --> 01:27:05
And it protects their eggs and they protect the tree.

01:27:04 --> 01:27:06
And they, so we saw that spot where there was

01:27:05 --> 01:27:08
a perfect circle around the trees

01:27:07 --> 01:27:13
'cause the ants had excavated the other vegetation

01:27:12 --> 01:27:15
so that those trees could have no competition to grow.

01:27:15 --> 01:27:21
The incredible calculation of how ants know to guard,

01:27:21 --> 01:27:24
come programmed to garden that tree

01:27:23 --> 01:27:29
and the tree somehow has been genetically informed to have

01:27:28 --> 01:27:31
ant habitat within itself.

01:27:30 --> 01:27:32
It's mind-blowing.

01:27:31 --> 01:27:34
And it actually is the foundation

01:27:33 --> 01:27:37
of a lot of existential confusion for me,

01:27:36 --> 01:27:39
because how the hell is this possible?

01:27:38 --> 01:27:42
- Yeah, well one of the things you mentioned

01:27:41 --> 01:27:45
that's also a source of a lot of existential confusion

01:27:44 --> 01:27:47
for me is ants. - [Paul] Yeah.

01:27:46 --> 01:27:50
- And the intelligence of different creatures in the forest.

01:27:49 --> 01:27:52
There's these giant colonies.

01:27:51 --> 01:27:54
There's just giant systems.

01:27:53 --> 01:27:57
But even just looking at a single colony of ants,

01:27:56 --> 01:28:00
them collaborating, leafcutter ants,

01:27:59 --> 01:28:01
is an incredible system.

01:28:00 --> 01:28:04
So individually the ants seem kind of dumb

01:28:03 --> 01:28:05
and simplistic. - [Paul] Mm.

01:28:04 --> 01:28:10
- But taken together, there is a vast intelligence operating

01:28:10 --> 01:28:12
that's able to be robust and resilient,

01:28:12 --> 01:28:15
in any kind of conditions is able to figure out

01:28:14 --> 01:28:16
a new environment,

01:28:15 --> 01:28:19
is able to be resilient to any kinds of attacks

01:28:18 --> 01:28:20
and all that kind of stuff.

01:28:19 --> 01:28:22
What do you find beautiful about them?

01:28:21 --> 01:28:23
- [Paul] Like as you said,

01:28:22 --> 01:28:24
just leafcutter ants in this jungle.

01:28:23 --> 01:28:26
- [Lex] Yeah. - That's forgetting

01:28:25 --> 01:28:28
all the other hundreds of species of ants

01:28:27 --> 01:28:29
that are in this jungle.

01:28:28 --> 01:28:32
But just the leafcutters apparently digest

01:28:33 --> 01:28:38
roughly 17% of the total biomass of the forest.

01:28:37 --> 01:28:41
Everything, all these giant trees, all that leaf litter,

01:28:40 --> 01:28:44
17% of that, almost a fifth of this forest

01:28:43 --> 01:28:46
cycles through leafcutter ant colonies.

01:28:45 --> 01:28:49
So they're constantly regenerating the forest.

01:28:48 --> 01:28:53
They're a huge source of the driver of this ecosystem.

01:28:52 --> 01:28:55
And so to me, when you see them working,

01:28:54 --> 01:28:57
it's, again, like I said, you see your friends

01:28:56 --> 01:28:58
as you go through the jungle,

01:28:57 --> 01:29:00
you see all the kapok tree, you see kania tree,

01:28:59 --> 01:29:02
oh, there's leafcutter ants doing what it's supposed to do.

01:29:01 --> 01:29:04
And it's just so beautiful.

01:29:03 --> 01:29:05
I find them very beautiful.

01:29:04 --> 01:29:07
Army ants, they're so tough.

01:29:06 --> 01:29:09
They're so ready to fight.

01:29:08 --> 01:29:10
They have this huge mandibles.

01:29:09 --> 01:29:11
They're just ready to, they're just,

01:29:10 --> 01:29:13
they're transporting their eggs.

01:29:12 --> 01:29:14
They're moving from here to there.

01:29:13 --> 01:29:15
Anything that's in the way is getting eaten.

01:29:14 --> 01:29:17
They're just savage

01:29:16 --> 01:29:18
and they're kind of cute for that.

01:29:17 --> 01:29:19
Unless you're tied to a tree.

01:29:18 --> 01:29:21
- The savagery is cute, yes.

01:29:20 --> 01:29:23
- I find it's kind of reassuring, you know?

01:29:22 --> 01:29:25
You want certain things to be tough.

01:29:24 --> 01:29:27
That's their part. - Oh, that everybody

01:29:26 --> 01:29:32
plays a part in the entirety of the nature mechanism.

01:29:31 --> 01:29:35
- In the powerful play, but.

01:29:34 --> 01:29:37
(Lex laughs)

01:29:36 --> 01:29:38
- Yeah, yeah.

01:29:37 --> 01:29:40
- But the army ants are so savage.

01:29:39 --> 01:29:43
You know, like if you step on army ants,

01:29:42 --> 01:29:46
they will all kamikaze and just attack onto your feet

01:29:45 --> 01:29:48
and they'll just sacrifice their own life

01:29:47 --> 01:29:49
for the good of the thing.

01:29:48 --> 01:29:51
And they'll be trying to kill your shoes.

01:29:50 --> 01:29:54
And there's something funny about that to me.

01:29:53 --> 01:29:56
There's something like kind of reassuring again,

01:29:55 --> 01:29:58
unless, unless,

01:29:57 --> 01:30:00
imagine if you're going through the jungle

01:29:59 --> 01:30:03
and you slip and you fall and you twist your knee.

01:30:02 --> 01:30:04
- [Lex] Yeah. - And you fall

01:30:03 --> 01:30:06
in just the right way but you can't get up.

01:30:05 --> 01:30:07
- [Lex] Yeah. - You can't,

01:30:06 --> 01:30:09
you're stuck there. - [Lex] Yeah.

01:30:08 --> 01:30:10
- And then army ants find you.

01:30:09 --> 01:30:12
- [Lex] Yeah. - They will take you apart.

01:30:11 --> 01:30:16
There are records of horses that have been tied up

01:30:15 --> 01:30:20
and army ants come and they'll take out the whole horse.

01:30:19 --> 01:30:21
- Imagine the pain of that.

01:30:22 --> 01:30:26
- It might be raining on us very hard very soon.

01:30:25 --> 01:30:27
- You wanna pause?

01:30:26 --> 01:30:30
- No, I think we'll stay here until the ship goes down.

01:30:29 --> 01:30:33
- We should mention that there's this one source of light

01:30:32 --> 01:30:34
and we're shrouded in darkness.

01:30:33 --> 01:30:37
- [Paul] And now the night shift is gonna take over soon.

01:30:36 --> 01:30:39
And we are in the Amazon rainforest.

01:30:38 --> 01:30:41
- What does the rainforest represent to you?

01:30:40 --> 01:30:45
When you zoom out, look at the entirety of it.

01:30:45 --> 01:30:50
- Carl Sagan's pale blue dot resonated

01:30:49 --> 01:30:51
with a lot of people,

01:30:50 --> 01:30:54
that everything you've ever heard of,

01:30:54 --> 01:30:56
all the heroes, all the villains,

01:30:55 --> 01:30:59
all of your ancestors, every achievement,

01:30:58 --> 01:31:04
tragedy, triumph, everything has happened on that one spot.

01:31:03 --> 01:31:07
This one tiny, tiny little rock that has life on it.

01:31:06 --> 01:31:10
And to me, the rainforests represent

01:31:09 --> 01:31:12
he crown jewel of that.

01:31:11 --> 01:31:15
As far as we know and to the best of our knowledge

01:31:14 --> 01:31:17
and with our shrewd, scientific brains

01:31:16 --> 01:31:19
at their fullest capacity,

01:31:19 --> 01:31:24
this is still the only place that we know that has life.

01:31:24 --> 01:31:26
And given that,

01:31:25 --> 01:31:31
the fact that there are still these tropical,

01:31:30 --> 01:31:36
towering, complex ecosystems that we are barely understand,

01:31:35 --> 01:31:40
crawling and full of the most incredible life,

01:31:39 --> 01:31:44
it's just, to me it's so wonderful.

01:31:43 --> 01:31:45
It's so incredible.

01:31:44 --> 01:31:47
Those, the waterfalls and the birds and the macaws

01:31:46 --> 01:31:49
and the jaguars, it's barely believable.

01:31:48 --> 01:31:52
Like, if you were to theoretically tell

01:31:51 --> 01:31:54
a hypothetical alien, "I live on this planet,

01:31:53 --> 01:31:55
and there's just these places

01:31:54 --> 01:31:57
where everything is interconnected.

01:31:56 --> 01:31:59
Everything means something to something else

01:31:58 --> 01:32:02
and the whole is this system that keeps us alive.

01:32:01 --> 01:32:04
And each tree is pumping air into the river,

01:32:03 --> 01:32:07
and there's an invisible river above the actual river.

01:32:06 --> 01:32:09
And the whole thing goes into stabilizing

01:32:08 --> 01:32:09
our global climate.

01:32:08 --> 01:32:14
And each little, tiny leafcutter ant somehow contributes

01:32:13 --> 01:32:16
to this giant biotic orchestra

01:32:15 --> 01:32:19
that keeps us alive and makes our environment possible."

01:32:18 --> 01:32:21
That is beautiful.

01:32:20 --> 01:32:22
I love that.

01:32:21 --> 01:32:24
And so the rainforests to me are

01:32:23 --> 01:32:25
the greatest celebration of life

01:32:24 --> 01:32:29
and probably the greatest challenge for us

01:32:28 --> 01:32:30
as a global society,

01:32:29 --> 01:32:32
because if we can't protect the crown jewel,

01:32:31 --> 01:32:36
the best thing, you know, the most beautiful part,

01:32:35 --> 01:32:39
then we're really, really missing the point.

01:32:38 --> 01:32:43
- Yeah, the diversity of organisms here is

01:32:42 --> 01:32:46
the biggest celebration of life

01:32:46 --> 01:32:49
that is at the core of what makes Earth

01:32:48 --> 01:32:50
a really special thing.

01:32:49 --> 01:32:53
That said, you and I have been arguing about aliens

01:32:52 --> 01:32:55
for pretty much the day I showed up.

01:32:54 --> 01:32:57
(Lex laughs)

01:32:56 --> 01:32:59
All right you brought a machete to this fight.

01:33:00 --> 01:33:03
Luckily, the table is long enough to.

01:33:02 --> 01:33:04
- I can't reach you.

01:33:03 --> 01:33:05
- You can't reach me.

01:33:04 --> 01:33:08
So to you, Earth is truly special.

01:33:07 --> 01:33:09
- [Paul] Yeah. - You don't think

01:33:08 --> 01:33:11
there's other Earths out there,

01:33:10 --> 01:33:13
millions of other Earths in our galaxy?

01:33:12 --> 01:33:14
When you look up, you know,

01:33:13 --> 01:33:16
we were sitting in the Amazon River.

01:33:15 --> 01:33:17
- [Paul] Okay. - The dark,

01:33:16 --> 01:33:19
the storm rolled over. - [Paul] Yeah.

01:33:18 --> 01:33:20
- And you started counting the stars.

01:33:19 --> 01:33:22
- [Paul] Yeah. - One, two,

01:33:21 --> 01:33:24
and that was once you can count the stars,

01:33:23 --> 01:33:26
that was a sign that the storm will actually pass,

01:33:25 --> 01:33:27
eventually it will pass.

01:33:26 --> 01:33:29
And that's what you're doing, three, four, five.

01:33:28 --> 01:33:30
And it's going to pass.

01:33:29 --> 01:33:32
You're not gonna have to sit in that river

01:33:31 --> 01:33:34
for like all night.

01:33:33 --> 01:33:37
So just a couple hours to keep yourself warm, okay.

01:33:36 --> 01:33:40
Each of those stars, there's Earth-like planets around them.

01:33:39 --> 01:33:43
- [Paul] Okay. - Why do you think

01:33:42 --> 01:33:45
there's not alien civilizations there?

01:33:46 --> 01:33:50
- You can write down a calculation on a napkin.

01:33:49 --> 01:33:53
You can cite different Hollywood movies.

01:33:52 --> 01:33:55
You can point up to the pieces of light in the stars.

01:33:54 --> 01:33:59
But if you, if I talk about show me a single cell

01:33:58 --> 01:34:02
that's not from this planet, it's still not possible.

01:34:01 --> 01:34:04
And so I agree with you that the likelihood is there,

01:34:03 --> 01:34:06
all indications point to it.

01:34:05 --> 01:34:08
It would be fascinating,

01:34:07 --> 01:34:09
especially if it was done in,

01:34:08 --> 01:34:11
especially, you know, imagine finding a planet

01:34:10 --> 01:34:13
of alternative life forms.

01:34:12 --> 01:34:14
Not necessarily even intelligent.

01:34:13 --> 01:34:16
Imagine just a planet of butterflies,

01:34:15 --> 01:34:19
whatever, you know, something else,

01:34:18 --> 01:34:20
that would be amazing.

01:34:19 --> 01:34:24
But I'm concerned with the reality that we have

01:34:23 --> 01:34:26
in front of us is that this is the spaceship,

01:34:25 --> 01:34:27
this is life. - [Lex] Yeah.

01:34:26 --> 01:34:29
- And so right now, given that reality,

01:34:28 --> 01:34:31
maybe that's the case.

01:34:30 --> 01:34:33
Maybe there are other planets

01:34:33 --> 01:34:35
or

01:34:37 --> 01:34:39
maybe we are the first.

01:34:38 --> 01:34:41
Maybe life originated here.

01:34:40 --> 01:34:46
Maybe God, the universe, whatever, maybe this is it.

01:34:46 --> 01:34:51
This is the testing ground for something bigger

01:34:50 --> 01:34:55
and this complexity and this diversity of life

01:34:54 --> 01:34:58
and this life that we have is that important.

01:34:57 --> 01:35:00
And I think that part of what we do when we go,

01:34:59 --> 01:35:02
oh yeah, but there's other planets,

01:35:01 --> 01:35:06
where first of all we're taking an assumption

01:35:05 --> 01:35:09
into reality without, I mean, you know,

01:35:08 --> 01:35:11
aliens are right now are about as real as Santa Claus.

01:35:10 --> 01:35:13
We think they're out there, but we're not sure.

01:35:12 --> 01:35:14
Maybe a little more real because, you know,

01:35:13 --> 01:35:16
it could make sense.

01:35:15 --> 01:35:17
We, no one has an alien.

01:35:16 --> 01:35:18
No one's seen an alien,

01:35:17 --> 01:35:20
no one's even seen cellular life.

01:35:19 --> 01:35:24
And so I'm not, again, if they showed up tomorrow, great,

01:35:23 --> 01:35:24
let's study them.

01:35:23 --> 01:35:29
But right now we have this very simple threat going on

01:35:28 --> 01:35:32
where we can't stop killing each other

01:35:31 --> 01:35:33
and our living environment.

01:35:33 --> 01:35:36
And so while some people can specialize

01:35:35 --> 01:35:38
in looking to the stars and to other planets

01:35:37 --> 01:35:41
and talk about being an interplanetary species,

01:35:40 --> 01:35:42
I'm very much concerned with the fact

01:35:41 --> 01:35:45
hat here in our home turf,

01:35:44 --> 01:35:47
our living environment where the air is good

01:35:46 --> 01:35:50
and the rivers are clean and the trees are big

01:35:49 --> 01:35:52
and there's macaws flying through the sky

01:35:51 --> 01:35:54
and salmon in the rivers,

01:35:54 --> 01:35:57
not only do we have a responsibility

01:35:56 --> 01:35:59
to each other and to our children

01:35:58 --> 01:36:03
to protect this incredible gift that is our entire reality,

01:36:02 --> 01:36:05
it seems kind of weird to,

01:36:04 --> 01:36:08
at some point, it, conservation seems kind of ridiculous.

01:36:07 --> 01:36:10
Like you're begging people to not pollute

01:36:09 --> 01:36:12
the things that keep them alive.

01:36:11 --> 01:36:16
It's almost kind of silly at a point.

01:36:16 --> 01:36:19
But we have this incredible thing

01:36:18 --> 01:36:21
where there are fish in the ocean and in the rivers

01:36:20 --> 01:36:22
that come standard with life on Earth.

01:36:21 --> 01:36:26
And we're harming the ability of Earth's ecosystems

01:36:25 --> 01:36:28
to provide for that life.

01:36:27 --> 01:36:31
And we are the generation that's gonna decide

01:36:30 --> 01:36:34
if those systems continue to provide life

01:36:33 --> 01:36:36
to all the people on Earth and all the generations.

01:36:35 --> 01:36:39
And by the way, all the other animals that exist

01:36:38 --> 01:36:41
for their own reasons,

01:36:40 --> 01:36:43
other consciousnesses that we're just beginning

01:36:42 --> 01:36:44
to understand,

01:36:43 --> 01:36:47
elephants, humpback whales, whatever,

01:36:46 --> 01:36:48
families of giant river otters.

01:36:49 --> 01:36:53
You, not everything can be seen from a human perspective.

01:36:52 --> 01:36:55
These are other species that have their own stories.

01:36:54 --> 01:36:59
And so I'm more biocentric than anthropocentric

01:36:58 --> 01:37:02
in that I think that nature is important.

01:37:01 --> 01:37:04
But I also believe that we are

01:37:07 --> 01:37:09
special.

01:37:08 --> 01:37:11
We are the most intelligent animal.

01:37:10 --> 01:37:13
- So one, I agree with you.

01:37:12 --> 01:37:16
There's some degree to which when you imagine aliens,

01:37:16 --> 01:37:22
you forget if by for a moment how special

01:37:21 --> 01:37:25
and important life is here on Earth, yes.

01:37:25 --> 01:37:27
But it's also a way to

01:37:29 --> 01:37:34
reach out through curiosity in trying to understand

01:37:33 --> 01:37:36
what is intelligence, what is consciousness?

01:37:35 --> 01:37:39
What is exactly the thing that makes life on Earth special?

01:37:39 --> 01:37:42
Another way of doing that,

01:37:41 --> 01:37:44
and I see the jungle in that same way,

01:37:43 --> 01:37:47
is basically treating the animals all around us,

01:37:46 --> 01:37:49
the life forms all around us as kinds of aliens,

01:37:50 --> 01:37:53
as, that's a humbling way.

01:37:52 --> 01:37:56
That's an intellectual humility with which to approach

01:37:55 --> 01:37:59
the study of like what the hell is going on here?

01:37:58 --> 01:38:02
- [Paul] Yeah. - This is truly incredible.

01:38:01 --> 01:38:06
Like, are the animals we've met

01:38:05 --> 01:38:09
over the last few days conscious?

01:38:08 --> 01:38:11
What is the nature of their intelligence?

01:38:10 --> 01:38:13
What is the nature of their consciousness?

01:38:12 --> 01:38:14
What motivates them?

01:38:13 --> 01:38:15
Are they individual creatures

01:38:14 --> 01:38:18
or are they actually part of the large system?

01:38:17 --> 01:38:19
And how large is the system?

01:38:18 --> 01:38:21
Is Earth one big system

01:38:20 --> 01:38:24
and humans are just little fingertips of that system?

01:38:23 --> 01:38:29
Or are each of the individual animals

01:38:28 --> 01:38:30
really the key actors

01:38:29 --> 01:38:33
and everything else is in the emerging complexity

01:38:32 --> 01:38:34
of the system?

01:38:33 --> 01:38:38
So I think thinking about aliens is a necessary,

01:38:39 --> 01:38:43
I like my town with a little drop of poison from Tom Waits

01:38:42 --> 01:38:45
is a necessary perturbation of the system

01:38:44 --> 01:38:47
of our thinking to sort of say,

01:38:46 --> 01:38:49
"Hey, we don't know what the fuck's going on around here."

01:38:48 --> 01:38:53
Sure, and aliens is a nice way to say

01:38:52 --> 01:38:58
okay, the mystery all around us is immense.

01:38:58 --> 01:39:04
'Cause to me, likely, aliens are living among us.

01:39:03 --> 01:39:07
Not in a trivial sense, little green men.

01:39:07 --> 01:39:11
But the force that created life

01:39:11 --> 01:39:16
I think permeates the entirety of the universe.

01:39:15 --> 01:39:20
That there is a force that's creative.

01:39:19 --> 01:39:24
- Now, the force that created life is a big one.

01:39:23 --> 01:39:26
And then the other thing is what do you mean by that?

01:39:25 --> 01:39:30
There's aliens living among us.

01:39:29 --> 01:39:32
You mean extraterrestrials?

01:39:31 --> 01:39:34
- Yes. - Living among us.

01:39:33 --> 01:39:36
- Yes. - You believe that?

01:39:37 --> 01:39:41
- Not like 100% but there's a good percentage.

01:39:40 --> 01:39:44
I don't understand how it's possible

01:39:43 --> 01:39:46
for there not to be

01:39:45 --> 01:39:49
a very large number of alien civilization

01:39:48 --> 01:39:52
throughout just our galaxy.

01:39:51 --> 01:39:54
- But that's different than saying

01:39:53 --> 01:39:54
that they're living among us.

01:39:53 --> 01:39:56
If you tell me that there's aliens living

01:39:55 --> 01:39:57
five galaxies over

01:39:56 --> 01:39:59
and that they're just out there somewhere,

01:39:58 --> 01:40:04
I'm kind of more on your side than that they're here.

01:40:03 --> 01:40:05
- [Lex] Yeah. - Because just like Bigfoot,

01:40:04 --> 01:40:06
like we have camera traps.

01:40:05 --> 01:40:09
We have DNA sequencing through water now.

01:40:08 --> 01:40:13
Like we can, you're telling me no one found

01:40:12 --> 01:40:16
one wing nut of a ship in all,

01:40:15 --> 01:40:19
like the Egyptians up until right now,

01:40:18 --> 01:40:21
no one in Russia saw like a crashed ship,

01:40:20 --> 01:40:24
took a picture, tweeted that ship real quick and you know?

01:40:23 --> 01:40:25
- I think there's no Bigfoot,

01:40:24 --> 01:40:29
there's no trivial manifestations of aliens.

01:40:28 --> 01:40:30
I think if they're here,

01:40:29 --> 01:40:34
they're here in ways that are not comprehendible by humans

01:40:33 --> 01:40:36
because they're far more advanced than humans.

01:40:35 --> 01:40:39
They're far more advanced than any life forms on Earth.

01:40:38 --> 01:40:42
So they're, even if it's just their probes,

01:40:41 --> 01:40:44
which cannot just even comprehend it,

01:40:44 --> 01:40:49
I think it's possible that they operate

01:40:48 --> 01:40:51
in the space of ideas, for example,

01:40:50 --> 01:40:53
that ideas could be aliens,

01:40:52 --> 01:40:54
feelings could be aliens.

01:40:53 --> 01:40:56
Consciousness itself could be aliens.

01:40:55 --> 01:40:59
So we can't restrict our understanding

01:40:58 --> 01:41:02
of what is a life form to a thing

01:41:01 --> 01:41:06
that is a biological creature that operates

01:41:05 --> 01:41:08
via natural selection on this particular planet.

01:41:07 --> 01:41:11
It could be much, much, much more sophisticated.

01:41:10 --> 01:41:13
It could be in a space of computation, for example.

01:41:12 --> 01:41:16
As we in the 21st century are developing

01:41:15 --> 01:41:19
increasingly sophisticated computational systems

01:41:18 --> 01:41:20
with artificial intelligence,

01:41:19 --> 01:41:22
it could be operating on some other level

01:41:21 --> 01:41:24
that we can't even imagine.

01:41:23 --> 01:41:26
It could be operating on a level of physics

01:41:25 --> 01:41:28
that we have not even begun to understand.

01:41:27 --> 01:41:32
We barely understand quantum mechanics.

01:41:31 --> 01:41:33
We use it.

01:41:32 --> 01:41:36
Quantum mechanics is a way we used to make

01:41:35 --> 01:41:37
very accurate predictions.

01:41:36 --> 01:41:42
But to understand why it's operating that way, we don't.

01:41:41 --> 01:41:46
And there's so many gigantic, powerful cosmic entities

01:41:45 --> 01:41:49
out there that we detect,

01:41:49 --> 01:41:52
sometimes can't detect dark matter, dark energy,

01:41:51 --> 01:41:54
but it's out there.

01:41:53 --> 01:41:58
We know it exists, but we can't explain why

01:41:57 --> 01:42:00
and what the fuck it is.

01:41:59 --> 01:42:04
We give it names, black holes and dark energy

01:42:03 --> 01:42:05
and dark matter,

01:42:04 --> 01:42:08
but those are all names for things

01:42:07 --> 01:42:10
that mathematically equations predict,

01:42:09 --> 01:42:11
but we don't understand.

01:42:10 --> 01:42:16
And so all of that is just to say that aliens could be here

01:42:16 --> 01:42:21
in ways that are for now and maybe for a long time

01:42:20 --> 01:42:23
going to be impossible for humans to understand.

01:42:22 --> 01:42:26
- So aliens in the strict biological sense,

01:42:25 --> 01:42:29
like horseshoe crabs,

01:42:29 --> 01:42:32
we agree that they're not,

01:42:31 --> 01:42:35
we haven't found physical aliens.

01:42:34 --> 01:42:39
- The only way I can imagine finding physical aliens is

01:42:38 --> 01:42:42
if alien species are trying to communicate with us humans

01:42:42 --> 01:42:45
or with other life forms

01:42:44 --> 01:42:48
and are trying to figure out a way to communicate with us

01:42:47 --> 01:42:52
uch that we dumb humans would understand.

01:42:51 --> 01:42:53
Like, let's create a thing.

01:42:54 --> 01:42:58
- Yo, there's a moth the size

01:42:59 --> 01:43:01
of a small eagle.

01:43:00 --> 01:43:02
(Paul and Lex laugh)

01:43:01 --> 01:43:04
- It's trying to get its 15 minutes of attention.

01:43:03 --> 01:43:05
- It just might, it just might.

01:43:04 --> 01:43:07
- Big fan of the podcast.

01:43:06 --> 01:43:09
- Okay, Lex, I love you.

01:43:08 --> 01:43:10
(Lex laughs)

01:43:09 --> 01:43:11
All right, so what you're,

01:43:10 --> 01:43:12
wouldn't it be interesting,

01:43:11 --> 01:43:15
it'd be really fascinating to me if we found out

01:43:14 --> 01:43:16
hat there were aliens living among us.

01:43:15 --> 01:43:18
- [Lex] Yeah. - And we couldn't see them.

01:43:17 --> 01:43:22
And what some of the people were calling aliens,

01:43:21 --> 01:43:25
the scientists, the religious people were calling angels.

01:43:24 --> 01:43:27
And then everybody had this realization

01:43:26 --> 01:43:29
that whether you call them aliens or angels,

01:43:28 --> 01:43:30
there are these other,

01:43:30 --> 01:43:33
there is more, way more to the universe

01:43:32 --> 01:43:34
than we're realizing. - Yeah.

01:43:33 --> 01:43:36
- I just for me,

01:43:38 --> 01:43:41
the fact that there's.

01:43:40 --> 01:43:43
- [Lex] There's a skull on the table.

01:43:42 --> 01:43:44
- Yeah, there's a skull on table.

01:43:43 --> 01:43:46
- [Lex] There's a skull in your hand.

01:43:45 --> 01:43:47
- There's now a skull in my hand

01:43:46 --> 01:43:50
of a monkey with a bullet in its head

01:43:49 --> 01:43:52
that I found on the floor of an Indigenous community

01:43:51 --> 01:43:53
where they eat monkeys.

01:43:52 --> 01:43:57
I didn't kill the monkey, so save your comments.

01:43:56 --> 01:44:02
But you know, in terms of the animals,

01:44:01 --> 01:44:05
I think that when I see space, it, my feeling,

01:44:04 --> 01:44:07
and I'm not requiring anybody else to have this feeling,

01:44:06 --> 01:44:09
but because we know,

01:44:08 --> 01:44:12
'cause this is the only place that we know that there's life

01:44:11 --> 01:44:13
and we have no idea how it started,

01:44:15 --> 01:44:19
I just think it's so important to protect it.

01:44:18 --> 01:44:22
And for me it's just as much about our children

01:44:21 --> 01:44:24
as it is about the little spider monkeys

01:44:23 --> 01:44:26
and the little baby caiman that are in the river right now,

01:44:25 --> 01:44:29
because life is so beautiful.

01:44:28 --> 01:44:30
- [Lex] Yeah. - And I think

01:44:29 --> 01:44:34
that there's a huge amount of intellectual responsibility

01:44:33 --> 01:44:35
that we can

01:44:36 --> 01:44:40
transfer off of ourselves if we go

01:44:39 --> 01:44:42
yeah, the rivers are filled with trash

01:44:41 --> 01:44:44
and yeah, extinction is happening,

01:44:43 --> 01:44:46
but we have to be an interplanetary species anyway

01:44:45 --> 01:44:49
'cause at any moment this could all end from an asteroid

01:44:48 --> 01:44:51
and like, everything's going to shit anyway.

01:44:50 --> 01:44:52
And so it's like, we're fucking up this planet

01:44:51 --> 01:44:54
and it's like, that's,

01:44:53 --> 01:44:57
we're just being angry teenagers who are, you know,

01:44:56 --> 01:44:58
going goth for a while.

01:44:57 --> 01:45:00
And it's like, what if you just rolled up your sleeves

01:44:59 --> 01:45:02
and said, holy shit, wait a second.

01:45:01 --> 01:45:05
You know, we can pretty much do whatever we want.

01:45:04 --> 01:45:06
We can fly all over the world.

01:45:05 --> 01:45:08
We have, we can do heart transplants,

01:45:07 --> 01:45:11
we can watch Netflix in the Amazon if we wanted to.

01:45:10 --> 01:45:13
Like, we could do all this amazing stuff.

01:45:12 --> 01:45:15
We can capture on video our adventures

01:45:14 --> 01:45:18
and go back and watch them again and again and again.

01:45:17 --> 01:45:21
There's so much incredible opportunity

01:45:20 --> 01:45:22
that technology has allowed us to do.

01:45:21 --> 01:45:24
And we're the richest in history.

01:45:23 --> 01:45:26
I mean, we could do everything.

01:45:25 --> 01:45:27
We could cross the whole planet in a second.

01:45:27 --> 01:45:30
And it's like, that's an amazing time to be alive.

01:45:29 --> 01:45:32
And if we just don't fuck up the ecosystems

01:45:31 --> 01:45:36
and kill all the other animals, we got it made.

01:45:35 --> 01:45:39
- Yeah, so it is true that we can destroy ourselves

01:45:38 --> 01:45:39
and nuclear weapons,

01:45:38 --> 01:45:44
but it also is true that that snake that I got to handle

01:45:43 --> 01:45:46
yesterday is like one of the most beautiful things

01:45:46 --> 01:45:49
Earth has ever created.

01:45:48 --> 01:45:51
In that little organism is encapsulated

01:45:50 --> 01:45:54
the entire history of Earth and it's beautiful.

01:45:53 --> 01:45:56
So we, both things are true.

01:45:55 --> 01:45:58
- [Paul] Yeah. - We should worry

01:45:57 --> 01:46:01
about the existential destruction of human civilization

01:46:00 --> 01:46:03
through the weapons we create

01:46:02 --> 01:46:07
