and we should become multiplanetary species as a backup

01:46:06 --> 01:46:08
for that purpose.

01:46:07 --> 01:46:11
But also remember that this place is

01:46:10 --> 01:46:15
really, really special and probably, if not difficult,

01:46:14 --> 01:46:17
probably impossible to recreate elsewhere.

01:46:18 --> 01:46:22
And by the way, there's something incredibly powerful

01:46:21 --> 01:46:23
about a skull.

01:46:22 --> 01:46:27
- Yeah, you ever hold a human skull, it'll give you,

01:46:27 --> 01:46:30
it'll weigh on you for a sec

01:46:29 --> 01:46:31
'cause you look into this,

01:46:30 --> 01:46:33
the hollow eyes of this face,

01:46:32 --> 01:46:36
and suddenly you go, you feel your own teeth,

01:46:35 --> 01:46:37
you go, you feel your own skull,

01:46:36 --> 01:46:40
and you go, holy shit.

01:46:39 --> 01:46:41
You go, what is going on?

01:46:40 --> 01:46:43
It's like taking acid, you just go, oh boy,

01:46:42 --> 01:46:47
I forgot that I'm a ghost inhabiting a meat vehicle

01:46:46 --> 01:46:48
on a floating rock.

01:46:47 --> 01:46:52
- But even a monkey. - [Paul] Yeah.

01:46:51 --> 01:46:54
- It's like looking at a

01:46:55 --> 01:46:59
ncestor, you know?

01:46:58 --> 01:47:00
Not a direct ancestor,

01:46:59 --> 01:47:03
but there's a, it's like a, you know,

01:47:02 --> 01:47:05
like you looking at a puddle at a reflection,

01:47:04 --> 01:47:07
- [Paul] Mm-hmm, a little blurry, but it's still there.

01:47:06 --> 01:47:08
- It's a little blurry, but it's still there.

01:47:07 --> 01:47:10
- [Paul] Yeah. - It's still there.

01:47:09 --> 01:47:14
And like the roots of who we are is still there.

01:47:13 --> 01:47:16
And it's all kind of incredible.

01:47:15 --> 01:47:18
Do you ever think of the tree of life

01:47:17 --> 01:47:20
just kind of like where we came from?

01:47:19 --> 01:47:23
- Yeah. - [Lex] The jungle is

01:47:22 --> 01:47:25
ephemeral, it just keeps,

01:47:24 --> 01:47:27
it's a system that just keeps forgetting

01:47:26 --> 01:47:28
because it's just churning and churning

01:47:27 --> 01:47:29
and churning,

01:47:28 --> 01:47:32
has in some ways no history.

01:47:31 --> 01:47:34
But to create the jungle, to create life on Earth,

01:47:33 --> 01:47:39
there's a deep history of lots of death, sex and death.

01:47:39 --> 01:47:43
- A festival of sex and death, life on Earth.

01:47:44 --> 01:47:46
- That's what I see in the skull.

01:47:45 --> 01:47:47
- Yeah.

01:47:47 --> 01:47:50
There's something, it's,

01:47:49 --> 01:47:53
there's something kind of terrifying about that image to me.

01:47:52 --> 01:47:55
Like when I hold that skull every now and then at night,

01:47:54 --> 01:47:57
you hold that skull and you,

01:47:56 --> 01:47:59
it just reminds you that you're temporary.

01:47:58 --> 01:48:03
- Yeah, both you and I will one day have one of those, yeah.

01:48:04 --> 01:48:06
Hmm,

01:48:06 --> 01:48:09
mine will be bigger.

01:48:08 --> 01:48:11
(Lex and Paul laugh)

01:48:10 --> 01:48:13
- My God, the male competition continues.

01:48:12 --> 01:48:17
The silverback slaps the lesser male once again.

01:48:17 --> 01:48:19
- [Lex] Do you have a lighter?

01:48:18 --> 01:48:22
- Yeah, bro, you wanna light this blunt?

01:48:21 --> 01:48:25
(Paul and Lex laugh)

01:48:24 --> 01:48:27
- What are your favorite animals to interact with?

01:48:27 --> 01:48:31
- I mean, my favorite, absolute favorite animal

01:48:30 --> 01:48:33
to interact with is 100% elephants,

01:48:32 --> 01:48:34
which there's no elephants here,

01:48:33 --> 01:48:37
but I've been incredibly privileged to spend some time

01:48:36 --> 01:48:39
with elephants both in India and in Africa.

01:48:38 --> 01:48:40
And

01:48:42 --> 01:48:46
I think that they're so smart and so complex

01:48:45 --> 01:48:49
that we do a really bad job of understanding

01:48:48 --> 01:48:52
what an elephant really is.

01:48:51 --> 01:48:55
I think that most children probably think of elephants

01:48:54 --> 01:48:57
as like something kind of cuddly.

01:48:56 --> 01:48:59
Most adults probably think of,

01:48:58 --> 01:49:02
have a similar misconception of them.

01:49:01 --> 01:49:04
When you see an elephant,

01:49:03 --> 01:49:07
when you see a 12-foot tall bull elephant

01:49:06 --> 01:49:09
with bone coming out of its face,

01:49:08 --> 01:49:12
with huge tusks and those giant,

01:49:11 --> 01:49:16
it's an octopus-faced, butterfly-eared behemoth

01:49:15 --> 01:49:18
at's a survival machine.

01:49:17 --> 01:49:19
And it'll look at you and just go,

01:49:20 --> 01:49:24
do I have to kill you to keep safe?

01:49:23 --> 01:49:25
And it's just, they're so tough.

01:49:24 --> 01:49:27
And they have dirt on their back

01:49:26 --> 01:49:29
and they have flower petals in the little hair,

01:49:28 --> 01:49:31
you realize they have hair all over their body

01:49:30 --> 01:49:35
and they, the power to throw a car over, to flip it,

01:49:34 --> 01:49:37
just one of the most impressive animals on Earth.

01:49:36 --> 01:49:39
And I think that I've gotten really good

01:49:38 --> 01:49:41
at interacting with wild elephants

01:49:40 --> 01:49:44
in a way that's respectful to them.

01:49:43 --> 01:49:47
And I think that when an elephant allows you

01:49:46 --> 01:49:48
to be in its space,

01:49:47 --> 01:49:51
it's because you're showing submissiveness

01:49:50 --> 01:49:54
and respect for the elephant space,

01:49:53 --> 01:49:55
and they're so intelligent

01:49:54 --> 01:49:59
hat they're communicating with seismic vibrations

01:49:58 --> 01:49:59
through the Earth,

01:49:58 --> 01:50:02
that they have high, you know, a matriarchal society,

01:50:01 --> 01:50:06
that they can remember the maps of their ancestors,

01:50:05 --> 01:50:07
that they know how to found, find water,

01:50:06 --> 01:50:08
that they can solve problems.

01:50:07 --> 01:50:11
They're such beautiful animals and they're so,

01:50:10 --> 01:50:12
talk about aliens.

01:50:11 --> 01:50:14
They're so alien looking.

01:50:13 --> 01:50:16
- [Lex] So weird. - These big, weird heads

01:50:15 --> 01:50:18
and the trunks with all those muscles.

01:50:17 --> 01:50:20
And they're so different than us.

01:50:19 --> 01:50:25
But yet I actually think that we grew up together, you know?

01:50:24 --> 01:50:27
They kind of raised us, sibling species,

01:50:26 --> 01:50:32
that we've been, we've inhabited the same epoch in history

01:50:31 --> 01:50:35
and we've relied on the ecosystems that they've created.

01:50:34 --> 01:50:37
And I think that they have a deep understanding

01:50:36 --> 01:50:39
of humans, elephants,

01:50:38 --> 01:50:43
and I think I see them more like aliens,

01:50:42 --> 01:50:47
more like non-human beings that we share the Earth with.

01:50:46 --> 01:50:49
So I don't see it as we're humans and they're animals.

01:50:48 --> 01:50:53
I actually see elephants as sort of a separate society

01:50:52 --> 01:50:55
along with humans as one of the dominant species

01:50:54 --> 01:50:56
on the planet.

01:50:55 --> 01:50:57
- So almost every species,

01:50:56 --> 01:51:00
especially the intelligent ones, especially the big ones,

01:50:59 --> 01:51:02
are their own societies that overlap

01:51:01 --> 01:51:05
and sometimes co-develop.

01:51:04 --> 01:51:08
- Yeah, I think whales, I think elephants,

01:51:07 --> 01:51:10
I think that there's those higher, you know,

01:51:09 --> 01:51:12
no one's suggesting that sardines are, you know,

01:51:11 --> 01:51:14
somehow need human rights or something.

01:51:13 --> 01:51:17
But I think the elephants need representation in governments

01:51:16 --> 01:51:21
because they influence their landscape.

01:51:20 --> 01:51:23
They engineer their environment.

01:51:22 --> 01:51:25
They have emotions, they have families,

01:51:24 --> 01:51:26
they have burial rituals.

01:51:25 --> 01:51:28
They're so like us.

01:51:27 --> 01:51:31
And yet we treat them like they're just oversized cows

01:51:30 --> 01:51:32
that we have to be scared of.

01:51:31 --> 01:51:36
It's, they're not the same as domesticated livestock.

01:51:35 --> 01:51:38
They're one of the treasures of Earth.

01:51:37 --> 01:51:41
I mean, look, let's just say little green men showed up

01:51:40 --> 01:51:42
and you said, they said, "Well, what's Earth?"

01:51:41 --> 01:51:46
It's like, well, there's mountains, there's rivers.

01:51:45 --> 01:51:47
It's like, well, how do I do this?

01:51:46 --> 01:51:50
You know, there's mountains, rivers, there's elephants.

01:51:49 --> 01:51:51
Like, it's like one of the first things

01:51:50 --> 01:51:53
a baby learns is elephant.

01:51:52 --> 01:51:55
Even if he's never seen one.

01:51:54 --> 01:51:57
It's just so iconic on Earth.

01:51:57 --> 01:52:01
Like you said. - Darren Aronofsky.

01:52:00 --> 01:52:04
- Darren Aronofsky, the elephant walking over the camera.

01:52:03 --> 01:52:05
I haven't seen it.

01:52:04 --> 01:52:06
You said it's incredible. - [Lex] Yeah, yeah.

01:52:05 --> 01:52:09
Saw it at The Sphere, "The Postcard From Earth".

01:52:08 --> 01:52:11
I mean, it's a celebration of Earth.

01:52:10 --> 01:52:12
- [Paul] Yeah.

01:52:11 --> 01:52:13
- [Lex] In all forms,

01:52:12 --> 01:52:18
and one of the critical big creatures in that film is

01:52:17 --> 01:52:19
an elephant.

01:52:18 --> 01:52:22
And it steps over the audience and the whole, like,

01:52:22 --> 01:52:26
the (indistinct) reverberates that power.

01:52:26 --> 01:52:29
I mean, some of it is size.

01:52:28 --> 01:52:30
- [Paul] Yeah. - Some of it is like,

01:52:29 --> 01:52:33
how did Earth create this?

01:52:32 --> 01:52:35
It is a weird looking creature,

01:52:34 --> 01:52:38
but we take it for granted 'cause we've accepted

01:52:37 --> 01:52:39
that this Earth can create this kind of thing.

01:52:39 --> 01:52:44
But it is weird, beautifully weird.

01:52:43 --> 01:52:44
- Oh, it's beautifully weird.

01:52:43 --> 01:52:47
I mean, elephants, there's something really impressive

01:52:46 --> 01:52:49
and wise about them.

01:52:48 --> 01:52:51
- [Lex] Yeah. - There's also beautiful weird

01:52:50 --> 01:52:54
that isn't so, that doesn't come with so much grandeur.

01:52:53 --> 01:52:57
Like to me a giraffe is beautifully weird.

01:52:56 --> 01:52:58
- [Lex] Yeah. - But they're just, you know,

01:52:57 --> 01:53:02
they're 18 foot tall camel deer things with, you know,

01:53:01 --> 01:53:03
giant necks.

01:53:02 --> 01:53:05
And they're strange

01:53:04 --> 01:53:07
and they're absolutely, serenely beautiful

01:53:06 --> 01:53:11
but they don't have that deep intelligence

01:53:10 --> 01:53:12
that elephants have.

01:53:11 --> 01:53:14
There's something that elephants have,

01:53:13 --> 01:53:15
- Do you see it in their eyes?

01:53:14 --> 01:53:18
Where is, how does the intelligence manifest itself?

01:53:18 --> 01:53:21
- Well, this is the thing.

01:53:20 --> 01:53:22
A lot of people, a lot of the,

01:53:21 --> 01:53:24
when I was reading Frans de Waal's book,

01:53:23 --> 01:53:27
a lot of what he was saying was that, you know,

01:53:26 --> 01:53:31
people give elephants human problems to solve

01:53:30 --> 01:53:33
in controlled environments and call it, you know,

01:53:32 --> 01:53:35
a study on elephant intelligence.

01:53:34 --> 01:53:38
Whereas if you're watching wild elephants

01:53:37 --> 01:53:40
and you're in the wild,

01:53:39 --> 01:53:44
you're gonna be watching them in a way that they're looking,

01:53:43 --> 01:53:45
you've pulled up in a safari vehicle

01:53:44 --> 01:53:47
or you've pulled over to the side of the road

01:53:46 --> 01:53:49
and the elephants are wary of you.

01:53:48 --> 01:53:50
So they're not acting natural.

01:53:49 --> 01:53:53
But as soon as you start watching wild elephants,

01:53:52 --> 01:53:56
truly in the wild, comfortable with your presence,

01:53:55 --> 01:53:59
you see how they start caring for their babies

01:53:58 --> 01:54:01
or how they can get annoyed.

01:54:00 --> 01:54:03
I once watched elephants around a waterhole,

01:54:02 --> 01:54:03
and there's this warthog,

01:54:02 --> 01:54:05
and I don't know why, but this warthog decided

01:54:04 --> 01:54:09
he needed to get in and there was this young, male elephant,

01:54:08 --> 01:54:10
and he kept turning around to this warthog

01:54:09 --> 01:54:12
and just being like, don't make me do it.

01:54:11 --> 01:54:15
Now, this elephant did not need to hurt the warthog.

01:54:14 --> 01:54:16
- [Lex] Yeah. - And the warthog was

01:54:15 --> 01:54:18
just like, I need a drink, I need a drink, I need a drink.

01:54:17 --> 01:54:19
Much simpler brain.

01:54:18 --> 01:54:21
The elephant was like, you could just tell,

01:54:20 --> 01:54:23
he was like, watch this.

01:54:22 --> 01:54:27
And he just went and crushed the warthog

01:54:26 --> 01:54:29
like it was a big beetle.

01:54:28 --> 01:54:31
- [Lex] Yeah. - And crushed his pelvis.

01:54:30 --> 01:54:33
And the warthog dragged itself away on its front legs

01:54:32 --> 01:54:34
and probably went off to die.

01:54:33 --> 01:54:37
But this young elephant put out his ears

01:54:36 --> 01:54:39
and he like paraded around with his tail up

01:54:38 --> 01:54:42
and he was like, look what I did, destruction.

01:54:41 --> 01:54:45
And it's like, that's a very relatable type of,

01:54:44 --> 01:54:46
he was annoyed with the warthog.

01:54:45 --> 01:54:49
- [Lex] Yeah. - And so you see them

01:54:48 --> 01:54:50
do these things.

01:54:49 --> 01:54:52
I mean, the most magical thing,

01:54:51 --> 01:54:54
and I've spoken about this many times,

01:54:53 --> 01:54:57
was that I was walking with a herd of semi-wild elephants

01:54:56 --> 01:54:59
that were crossing through a village in India

01:54:58 --> 01:55:02
because elephants have lost a lot of their territory

01:55:01 --> 01:55:06
because there's so much population in India.

01:55:05 --> 01:55:07
And so we were crossing through a village,

01:55:06 --> 01:55:09
which is very delicate because the matriarchs are

01:55:08 --> 01:55:12
leading the babies and there's villagers who have no idea

01:55:11 --> 01:55:13
what an elephant is.

01:55:12 --> 01:55:15
And they're watching the elephants cross.

01:55:14 --> 01:55:17
And the matriarchs backed this girl up against a wall,

01:55:16 --> 01:55:19
and she was terrified,

01:55:18 --> 01:55:20
standing there with her back against the wall.

01:55:19 --> 01:55:23
And the elephant just put her trunk out

01:55:22 --> 01:55:24
and touched the girl's stomach.

01:55:23 --> 01:55:25
And then the other elephants came

01:55:24 --> 01:55:27
and they all started touching her stomach.

01:55:26 --> 01:55:31
And the ranger there explained to me, just went,

01:55:30 --> 01:55:32
"She's pregnant.

01:55:31 --> 01:55:33
They know she's pregnant.

01:55:32 --> 01:55:36
They can smell, they can tell, and they're curious."

01:55:35 --> 01:55:39
And they, all the female elephants came

01:55:38 --> 01:55:41
to investigate the pregnant girl.

01:55:40 --> 01:55:42
And she had no idea what was going on.

01:55:41 --> 01:55:46
And so it's like that stuff, that stuff.

01:55:45 --> 01:55:47
(Paul growls)

01:55:46 --> 01:55:49
- And it's cool to hear that, you know,

01:55:48 --> 01:55:52
with the crushing and the pride of a young elephant

01:55:51 --> 01:55:53
hat there's a complexity of behavior.

01:55:52 --> 01:55:57
It's just like with humans, I mean, you know, humans.

01:55:56 --> 01:55:58
- [Paul] That's not always pretty.

01:55:57 --> 01:55:59
- That's the thing, man.

01:55:58 --> 01:56:01
Humans are capable of good and evil.

01:56:00 --> 01:56:03
And sometimes we attach these words.

01:56:02 --> 01:56:06
(animals chirp)

01:56:06 --> 01:56:09
I love that there's just,

01:56:08 --> 01:56:12
it's an orchestra of different sounds.

01:56:11 --> 01:56:13
- [Paul] Yeah. - And that's,

01:56:12 --> 01:56:14
that one is sex?

01:56:13 --> 01:56:16
- That's a bamboo rat calling out for a mate, yeah.

01:56:15 --> 01:56:18
- A mate, all right.

01:56:18 --> 01:56:20
Good luck to you buddy.

01:56:20 --> 01:56:22
- Good hunting.

01:56:22 --> 01:56:25
- You know, humans are capable

01:56:26 --> 01:56:28
of evil things and beautiful things.

01:56:27 --> 01:56:30
And I wonder if animals are the same.

01:56:29 --> 01:56:33
You think there's just different personalities

01:56:32 --> 01:56:36
and different life trajectories for animals?

01:56:35 --> 01:56:38
Like, as they develop in their understanding

01:56:37 --> 01:56:43
of social interaction, of survival,

01:56:42 --> 01:56:47
of maybe even primitive concepts of right and wrong

01:56:46 --> 01:56:51
within the social system,

01:56:50 --> 01:56:53
do you think there is a lot of diversity

01:56:52 --> 01:56:56
and personalities and behavior?

01:56:56 --> 01:57:02
Just like different people, is there different elephants?

01:57:02 --> 01:57:04
- Of course.

01:57:03 --> 01:57:06
And what I really like is that you said

01:57:05 --> 01:57:08
is there a perception of what's right and wrong?

01:57:07 --> 01:57:10
Because elephants have a code of ethics.

01:57:09 --> 01:57:12
- [Lex] Hmm. - And so as the,

01:57:11 --> 01:57:15
for the simplest example is that as young males

01:57:14 --> 01:57:18
begin to grow, they start developing these tusks.

01:57:17 --> 01:57:20
And those tusks are a tool and they use 'em.

01:57:19 --> 01:57:22
So for Indian elephants, the females don't have tusks

01:57:21 --> 01:57:24
and the males do.

01:57:23 --> 01:57:27
The females kick the males out of the herd.

01:57:26 --> 01:57:30
The females keep all the sisters and the aunts

01:57:29 --> 01:57:31
and the cousins together.

01:57:30 --> 01:57:33
But the males are their own thing.

01:57:32 --> 01:57:35
And so here's the thing.

01:57:34 --> 01:57:36
If you have, so what you get is

01:57:35 --> 01:57:38
these crews of male elephants

01:57:39 --> 01:57:42
and the older males will, you know,

01:57:41 --> 01:57:44
there's play fighting that goes on around, you know,

01:57:43 --> 01:57:45
two young males can play fight,

01:57:44 --> 01:57:48
but the older males, they'll kick some ass.

01:57:47 --> 01:57:50
They'll show them how to behave.

01:57:49 --> 01:57:52
They'll explain who gets to talk to the females,

01:57:51 --> 01:57:55
who gets to interact, who gets to mate,

01:57:54 --> 01:57:57
who gets the best vegetation to eat.

01:57:56 --> 01:57:59
And so there's an order established.

01:57:58 --> 01:58:03
And so young male elephants have to be taught how to act,

01:58:02 --> 01:58:06
just like a teenage human has to be taught.

01:58:05 --> 01:58:08
You know, you can't just haul off

01:58:07 --> 01:58:09
and break another kid's nose.

01:58:08 --> 01:58:12
You gotta, there's gonna be consequence.

01:58:11 --> 01:58:14
Maybe you'll get suspended,

01:58:13 --> 01:58:16
or maybe that kid will get his friends

01:58:15 --> 01:58:17
and beat the living shit out of you.

01:58:16 --> 01:58:20
Whatever it is, society regulates your behavior.

01:58:19 --> 01:58:24
And elephants have a very strict, very predictable,

01:58:23 --> 01:58:27
sort of like the males teach the males how to run things.

01:58:26 --> 01:58:30
And the females, which really have the final say,

01:58:29 --> 01:58:31
they're matriarchal.

01:58:31 --> 01:58:34
They're the ones leading the herd where to go.

01:58:33 --> 01:58:37
The males follow where the wise females

01:58:36 --> 01:58:38
tell them where to go.

01:58:37 --> 01:58:41
- So that regulation mechanisms from that emerges

01:58:40 --> 01:58:45
a kind of moral system under which they operate,

01:58:44 --> 01:58:47
what's right and wrong.

01:58:46 --> 01:58:48
- For an elephant, yeah. - [Lex] For an elephant.

01:58:47 --> 01:58:50
- Right and wrong for an elephant is not the same

01:58:49 --> 01:58:51
as what's right and wrong for a grizzly bear.

01:58:50 --> 01:58:53
Grizzly bear, if you're a male grizzly bear

01:58:52 --> 01:58:56
and you see a female with cubs,

01:58:55 --> 01:58:58
you just kill those cubs and then you can mate with that,

01:58:57 --> 01:58:59
you can mate with her and put your own cubs in there.

01:58:58 --> 01:59:03
And it's like, that's a whole different type of ethics.

01:59:02 --> 01:59:07
- Yeah, the value of child life is different.

01:59:06 --> 01:59:09
- [Paul] Yeah. - From species to species.

01:59:08 --> 01:59:10
- [Paul] Yeah. - Some of them hold

01:59:09 --> 01:59:11
it sacred, some of them not at all.

01:59:10 --> 01:59:14
- And that's why I think I resonate so much with elephants

01:59:13 --> 01:59:15
because they're, I think they're,

01:59:14 --> 01:59:18
I think that we are kind of matriarchal,

01:59:17 --> 01:59:20
at least I grew up matriarchal.

01:59:19 --> 01:59:24
Like, women were the force in my life, my family,

01:59:23 --> 01:59:25
and most of my friends' families,

01:59:24 --> 01:59:27
women kind of had the final say.

01:59:26 --> 01:59:31
And I feel like that's the way it is with elephants.

01:59:30 --> 01:59:33
Like, you might be bigger and stronger,

01:59:32 --> 01:59:34
but it doesn't really account for much

01:59:33 --> 01:59:37
if you're not smarter and more emotionally intelligent

01:59:36 --> 01:59:40
and you know how to take care of the group.

01:59:40 --> 01:59:45
- Just to zoom out into the ridiculous questions

01:59:44 --> 01:59:47
as we were talking about aliens.

01:59:46 --> 01:59:49
- [Paul] Hmm. - There's a lot of people

01:59:48 --> 01:59:52
trying to understand, trying to study the origin of life.

01:59:51 --> 01:59:54
- Oh, I love this.

01:59:53 --> 01:59:59
- First of all, what do you think is life versus non-life?

01:59:58 --> 02:00:02
Like when you look at like ants

02:00:01 --> 02:00:05
or even like the simplest, simplest of organisms,

02:00:04 --> 02:00:07
we saw a frog in a stream yesterday

02:00:06 --> 02:00:08
that was like a leaf frog.

02:00:07 --> 02:00:12
It was like as flat as a sheet of paper.

02:00:11 --> 02:00:14
And it does a lot of weird things.

02:00:13 --> 02:00:18
And it found a way to exist in this world.

02:00:17 --> 02:00:20
But that's a single living organisms

02:00:19 --> 02:00:22
with a bunch of components to it.

02:00:21 --> 02:00:26
But like, there's a life form that exists in this world.

02:00:25 --> 02:00:29
What is the difference between that and a rock?

02:00:28 --> 02:00:32
What is that, what is the essence of that life?

02:00:31 --> 02:00:35
And this might be an unanswerable question.

02:00:34 --> 02:00:36
There's probably a chemistry, physics,

02:00:35 --> 02:00:38
biology way of answering that.

02:00:37 --> 02:00:40
Like, what to you is that?

02:00:40 --> 02:00:45
- I think to me, life is something that grows

02:00:44 --> 02:00:48
and responds to stimuli like in basic biology 101.

02:00:47 --> 02:00:50
I think, and I'm fine with that.

02:00:49 --> 02:00:51
I don't need it to be more romantic than that.

02:00:50 --> 02:00:55
But I think it's actually comical how,

02:00:55 --> 02:01:01
how do you get from a rock to an orangutan, you know?

02:01:00 --> 02:01:06
And our answer for that is primordial soup.

02:01:05 --> 02:01:07
Maybe there was just stuff on Earth

02:01:06 --> 02:01:11
and then the stuff just got up and started walking.

02:01:10 --> 02:01:13
Maybe there just, there was nothing happening

02:01:12 --> 02:01:17
and then there was all of a sudden there was a cell.

02:01:16 --> 02:01:19
And the cell had function and then it complexified

02:01:18 --> 02:01:20
and then it started reproducing

02:01:19 --> 02:01:22
and found male and female parts.

02:01:21 --> 02:01:25
And what?

02:01:24 --> 02:01:29
Like it, we are so underequipped to understand

02:01:28 --> 02:01:33
how the hell we got here, let alone ants or even bacteria.

02:01:32 --> 02:01:34
- I see this so many,

02:01:34 --> 02:01:39
in very simple mathematical models,

02:01:38 --> 02:01:40
like something Game of Life,

02:01:39 --> 02:01:42
they're cellular automata.

02:01:41 --> 02:01:46
You could see from simple rules

02:01:45 --> 02:01:49
and simple objects when they're interacting together,

02:01:48 --> 02:01:53
as you grow that system, complex objects arise.

02:01:53 --> 02:01:58
Like that emergence of complexity is not understood

02:01:57 --> 02:02:00
by science, by mathematics at all.

02:01:59 --> 02:02:03
And it seems like from primordial soups,

02:02:02 --> 02:02:06
you can get a lot of cool shit.

02:02:05 --> 02:02:09
And the force of getting from soup

02:02:09 --> 02:02:13
to like two humans on microphones.

02:02:12 --> 02:02:14
- [Paul] Yeah.

02:02:14 --> 02:02:17
- Not understood and it seems to be a thing

02:02:16 --> 02:02:19
that happens on Earth.

02:02:18 --> 02:02:22
I tend to think that it's a thing that happens

02:02:21 --> 02:02:23
everywhere in the universe

02:02:22 --> 02:02:28
and there's some deep force that's pushing this along

02:02:28 --> 02:02:30
in some way.

02:02:29 --> 02:02:33
That there's something we, I don't want to sort of

02:02:35 --> 02:02:39
simplify it, but there is something that creates

02:02:38 --> 02:02:42
complexity out of simplicity that we don't quite understand.

02:02:42 --> 02:02:46
And that's the thing that created the first organism,

02:02:45 --> 02:02:48
living organism on Earth,

02:02:47 --> 02:02:52
that like leap from no life to life on Earth,

02:02:52 --> 02:02:53
that's a weird one.

02:02:52 --> 02:02:56
- That's a weird one 'cause you can imagine,

02:02:56 --> 02:02:58
I think that what?

02:02:57 --> 02:03:00
The Earth is 4.5 billion years old

02:02:59 --> 02:03:04
and you can imagine just this

02:03:03 --> 02:03:09
rock of a planet with like rain and storms and elements

02:03:08 --> 02:03:14
and iron and granite and like, just random stuff.

02:03:13 --> 02:03:17
It's pretty easy to imagine that.

02:03:16 --> 02:03:19
But then I remember that book that I think

02:03:18 --> 02:03:20
we all had the same book when we were kids

02:03:19 --> 02:03:22
and then like they show this like fish-like animal

02:03:21 --> 02:03:24
crawling out of a,

02:03:23 --> 02:03:25
out of the primordial soup.

02:03:24 --> 02:03:29
And it's like, bro, you just missed the most important part,

02:03:28 --> 02:03:31
author of that book, bro.

02:03:31 --> 02:03:37
And I think the first bacteria came in around 3,

02:03:36 --> 02:03:39
3.7 billion years ago,

02:03:38 --> 02:03:41
so there's like at least like, you know,

02:03:41 --> 02:03:44
a bunch of billion years where there's just nothing.

02:03:43 --> 02:03:45
It's just a planet. - [Lex] Yeah.

02:03:44 --> 02:03:48
- And then we start seeing fossils of the first bacteria.

02:03:47 --> 02:03:51
- And the bacteria stuck around for a long time.

02:03:50 --> 02:03:53
A billion, 2 billion years.

02:03:52 --> 02:03:54
It's just very, very long.

02:03:53 --> 02:03:56
- Just bacteria. - Just bacteria

02:03:55 --> 02:03:57
but a lot of them. - [Paul] A lot.

02:03:58 --> 02:04:01
- There's probably a lot of innovation,

02:04:00 --> 02:04:04
a lot of murder, a lot of interaction.

02:04:03 --> 02:04:05
- [Paul] Yeah. - Yeah.

02:04:04 --> 02:04:08
And then, I mean, there's a big, a few big leaps

02:04:07 --> 02:04:09
along the history of life on Earth.

02:04:08 --> 02:04:10
- [Paul] Yeah. - You know,

02:04:09 --> 02:04:12
the predator-prey dynamic.

02:04:11 --> 02:04:13
- [Paul] Yeah. - That was

02:04:12 --> 02:04:14
a really cool innovation.

02:04:13 --> 02:04:16
It's almost like innovation's like features on iPhone.

02:04:15 --> 02:04:19
It's like, it's nice, like predator-prey,

02:04:19 --> 02:04:24
eukaryotes, so complex, multicellular organisms,

02:04:25 --> 02:04:29
emerging from the water to land.

02:04:28 --> 02:04:30
That was weird.

02:04:29 --> 02:04:31
That was a. - [Paul] Yeah.

02:04:30 --> 02:04:33
- Interesting innovation.

02:04:32 --> 02:04:36
This how, whatever led to humans

02:04:36 --> 02:04:40
that, there's a lot of interesting stuff there.

02:04:39 --> 02:04:41
- See, I can't even get that far.

02:04:40 --> 02:04:46
I can't get from rock and sand to cells.

02:04:45 --> 02:04:48
- [Lex] Yeah. - That's a huge.

02:04:47 --> 02:04:50
- [Lex] Yeah. - I mean,

02:04:49 --> 02:04:54
to everything around us that has cells,

02:04:53 --> 02:04:56
it's just, it's wild.

02:04:55 --> 02:04:59
Even again, and I could imagine being on another planet

02:04:58 --> 02:05:03
and how incredibly valuable this thing would be.

02:05:02 --> 02:05:06
This, it's impossible to replicate.

02:05:05 --> 02:05:08
I'm looking at it through the candlelight right now,

02:05:07 --> 02:05:10
and I can see all of the structures in this leaf,

02:05:09 --> 02:05:12
the incredible structures in this leaf

02:05:11 --> 02:05:14
that look exactly like the veins in my arm,

02:05:13 --> 02:05:16
which look exactly like the rivers

02:05:15 --> 02:05:18
that are flowing across this landscape.

02:05:17 --> 02:05:21
And it's like, life has this overwhelming pattern

02:05:20 --> 02:05:23
that it uses and it's so beautiful.

02:05:23 --> 02:05:27
I just think it's, yeah.

02:05:27 --> 02:05:31
When you imagine the days of the lightning and the volcanoes

02:05:30 --> 02:05:33
and the primordial soup, it's,

02:05:32 --> 02:05:36
there's a big gap there.

02:05:35 --> 02:05:37
And it's fascinating to think about

02:05:36 --> 02:05:38
and it's fascinating to see

02:05:37 --> 02:05:40
how different people's belief systems

02:05:41 --> 02:05:44
lead them to different answers there.

02:05:43 --> 02:05:45
- Not to give any spoilers,

02:05:44 --> 02:05:49
but "Postcard from Earth", so Darren Aronofsky's film,

02:05:48 --> 02:05:54
the idea there is there's probes that are sent out

02:05:53 --> 02:05:55
from Earth. - [Paul] Oh, that's so cool.

02:05:54 --> 02:05:57
- To all these other planets.

02:05:56 --> 02:06:01
And each probe contains two humans, a man and a woman.

02:06:02 --> 02:06:05
And those two humans are in love.

02:06:04 --> 02:06:07
So think of a couple in love.

02:06:06 --> 02:06:09
They're sent there with all the information,

02:06:08 --> 02:06:13
basically a leaf that holds the information,

02:06:12 --> 02:06:17
what it takes to create life on other planet.

02:06:16 --> 02:06:19
- [Paul] Wow. - To recreate an Earth

02:06:18 --> 02:06:20
on other planets.

02:06:19 --> 02:06:24
And the two humans hold all the information

02:06:23 --> 02:06:26
for the things that make life on Earth special,

02:06:25 --> 02:06:28
especially in human civilization,

02:06:27 --> 02:06:32
is love, consciousness, the social connection.

02:06:31 --> 02:06:35
So all that information is sent in the probe

02:06:34 --> 02:06:39
and the postcard from Earth is those humans waking up,

02:06:38 --> 02:06:42
remembering all the information that is Earth, that.

02:06:41 --> 02:06:44
- [Paul] Whoa. - Like a celebration of

02:06:44 --> 02:06:48
all the things that make Earth magical

02:06:47 --> 02:06:49
throughout its history,

02:06:48 --> 02:06:51
all the diversity of organisms, all of that.

02:06:50 --> 02:06:53
You're loading all that in to create life

02:06:52 --> 02:06:54
on that new planet,

02:06:54 --> 02:06:57
which is something I think alien civilizations are doing.

02:06:56 --> 02:06:59
They're sending probes all throughout the galaxy

02:06:58 --> 02:07:00
and they just haven't arrived yet.

02:06:59 --> 02:07:02
But anyway, that's another.

02:07:01 --> 02:07:03
- That's so beautiful.

02:07:02 --> 02:07:05
And one of the things that I think,

02:07:04 --> 02:07:06
I wanna see that so much

02:07:05 --> 02:07:10
and one of the things that I love about Aronofsky's work

02:07:09 --> 02:07:12
is "The Fountain".

02:07:11 --> 02:07:14
And what I find so beautiful about that is

02:07:13 --> 02:07:15
that now here he's saying

02:07:14 --> 02:07:19
okay, we're sending probes out to other worlds,

02:07:18 --> 02:07:20
alien civilizations.

02:07:19 --> 02:07:22
And in "The Fountain", it was sort of what I thought he did

02:07:21 --> 02:07:25
so beautifully was braid together those three stories

02:07:24 --> 02:07:28
where in one, I don't remember if he's in a spaceship

02:07:27 --> 02:07:30
or if that's supposed to be like his soul.

02:07:29 --> 02:07:31
The other one, he's a scientist

02:07:30 --> 02:07:33
in sort of like comparable times to ours.

02:07:32 --> 02:07:35
And then he's the Spanish explorer.

02:07:34 --> 02:07:38
But either way, there's the tree of life

02:07:37 --> 02:07:42
and it sort of braids together all of the major religions.

02:07:41 --> 02:07:44
And it made me think of that quote that you hear

02:07:43 --> 02:07:46
where it says, you know, oh God, what was it?

02:07:46 --> 02:07:50
Christ wasn't a Christian and Buddha wasn't a Buddhist

02:07:49 --> 02:07:51
and Mohammad wasn't a Muslim.

02:07:50 --> 02:07:53
They were all just teachers who were teaching love.

02:07:52 --> 02:07:57
And it's like "The Fountain" sort of says

02:07:56 --> 02:08:00
nature is the, that driving force.

02:07:59 --> 02:08:03
And it's our job to understand that the game is love.

02:08:02 --> 02:08:05
And that's what the main character in "The Fountain"

02:08:04 --> 02:08:09
needs to learn, is that it's nature that's going to just,

02:08:08 --> 02:08:12
that's going to carry your soul through this thing.

02:08:11 --> 02:08:13
And that there's so much you don't understand.

02:08:12 --> 02:08:16
And the epiphany at the end, God, I love that movie.

02:08:15 --> 02:08:17
God, I love that.

02:08:16 --> 02:08:18
- Among many things, you're also an artist

02:08:17 --> 02:08:22
hat's trying to convert the thing that is nature

02:08:21 --> 02:08:25
into the thing that we humans can understand,

02:08:24 --> 02:08:26
the complexity, the beauty of it.

02:08:25 --> 02:08:28
That's what Darren Aronofsky tried to do

02:08:27 --> 02:08:30
with those couple of films.

02:08:29 --> 02:08:32
That's something that I hope you do

02:08:31 --> 02:08:34
actually in the medium of film too.

02:08:33 --> 02:08:35
That would be very interesting.

02:08:34 --> 02:08:37
And you do that in a medium of books, for only.

02:08:38 --> 02:08:40
How much do you think we understand

02:08:39 --> 02:08:42
about the history of life on Earth?

02:08:42 --> 02:08:45
- I think we got it all wrong.

02:08:44 --> 02:08:46
(Lex laughs)

02:08:45 --> 02:08:46
No, I don't know.

02:08:45 --> 02:08:48
It seems like they change it all the time.

02:08:47 --> 02:08:52
You know, they say that Easter Island, you know,

02:08:51 --> 02:08:53
when I was in college, they were big on telling you

02:08:52 --> 02:08:55
that Easter Island, they ruined their environment

02:08:54 --> 02:08:59
and they had environmental collapse

02:08:58 --> 02:09:01
and that's why there was nobody on Easter Island.

02:09:00 --> 02:09:03
It was a cautionary tale.

02:09:02 --> 02:09:04
We could ruin our environment.

02:09:03 --> 02:09:05
And now it seems like they've changed their mind on that.

02:09:04 --> 02:09:09
And then when humans entered North America

02:09:08 --> 02:09:11
seems to be hugely up to speculation.

02:09:10 --> 02:09:14
And, you know, the Africa spreading,

02:09:13 --> 02:09:15
that we all spread out of Africa

02:09:14 --> 02:09:18
nd then the Pleistocene overkill extinction theory,

02:09:17 --> 02:09:21
and it's like, it seems like every few years they update it

02:09:20 --> 02:09:22
and they change it and they say,

02:09:21 --> 02:09:24
oh, the guys, no, no, no, no,

02:09:23 --> 02:09:25
the guy from 10 years ago,

02:09:24 --> 02:09:27
actually, my new theory is the best theory.

02:09:26 --> 02:09:29
Let's write some books and get me on Letterman.

02:09:28 --> 02:09:31
And it seems like there's a new prevailing theory

02:09:30 --> 02:09:34
that's really always exciting and edgy

02:09:33 --> 02:09:37
about how we got here and where we came from

02:09:36 --> 02:09:38
and how we dispersed

02:09:37 --> 02:09:40
and maybe even has some political implications,

02:09:39 --> 02:09:43
like how we should use the Amazon moving forward.

02:09:42 --> 02:09:46
Like, the Amazon was engineered by people, so fuck it.

02:09:45 --> 02:09:48
Let's just cut it down.

02:09:47 --> 02:09:50
- Yeah, it's, I tend to believe

02:09:49 --> 02:09:51
that we mostly don't understand anything,

02:09:50 --> 02:09:55
but there is an optimism in continuously figuring out

02:09:54 --> 02:09:56
he puzzle of that. - [Paul] Sure.

02:09:55 --> 02:10:00
- We offline talked about the Graham Hancock

02:09:59 --> 02:10:03
Flint Dibble debate on Rogan.

02:10:02 --> 02:10:04
I like debates, personally. - [Paul] Yeah.

02:10:03 --> 02:10:07
- [Lex] So Flint Dibbel represents mainstream archeology.

02:10:06 --> 02:10:11
And I actually like the whole science,

02:10:10 --> 02:10:12
the whole field of archeology.

02:10:11 --> 02:10:14
You're trying to figure out history

02:10:13 --> 02:10:16
with so little information.

02:10:15 --> 02:10:19
You're trying to put together this puzzle

02:10:18 --> 02:10:20
when you have so little

02:10:19 --> 02:10:23
and you're desperately clinging onto little clues.

02:10:22 --> 02:10:24
And from those clues,

02:10:23 --> 02:10:27
using the simplest possible explanation to understand.

02:10:26 --> 02:10:29
And now with modern technology,

02:10:28 --> 02:10:32
as Flint was trying to express,

02:10:31 --> 02:10:35
that you can use large amounts of data

02:10:34 --> 02:10:36
that's like imperfect,

02:10:35 --> 02:10:39
but just the scale and using that

02:10:38 --> 02:10:41
o reconstruct civilizations.

02:10:40 --> 02:10:42
There are different practices

02:10:41 --> 02:10:45
from the little details of what kind of things they eat,

02:10:44 --> 02:10:46
how they interact with each other,

02:10:45 --> 02:10:49
what kind of art they create to when they existed,

02:10:48 --> 02:10:50
what are the timeframes, all that kind of stuff.

02:10:49 --> 02:10:52
- [Paul] Hmm. - And that starts to fill in

02:10:51 --> 02:10:54
the gaps of our understanding,

02:10:53 --> 02:10:58
but still the error bars are large

02:10:57 --> 02:11:02
in terms of what really happened.

02:11:01 --> 02:11:03
And that leaves room for things

02:11:02 --> 02:11:05
like Graham Hancock talks about,

02:11:04 --> 02:11:07
like lost civilizations,

02:11:06 --> 02:11:09
which I like also because it gives, you have

02:11:11 --> 02:11:15
a kind of humility about maybe there's giant things

02:11:14 --> 02:11:18
we don't know about or we got completely wrong.

02:11:17 --> 02:11:20
And that's always good to, like, remember.

02:11:20 --> 02:11:24
- It's confusing to me to imagine like what,

02:11:23 --> 02:11:25
I don't even know what,

02:11:24 --> 02:11:27
like what ended the why, where did the Egyptians go?

02:11:26 --> 02:11:28
Like what happened to.

02:11:27 --> 02:11:29
- [Lex] Yeah. - It seemed like

02:11:28 --> 02:11:30
they were doing so good.

02:11:29 --> 02:11:31
They had so much cool shit.

02:11:31 --> 02:11:34
But I mean, I was reading anthropological stuff

02:11:33 --> 02:11:37
in the Amazon about tribes that, you know,

02:11:36 --> 02:11:40
just through their societal structures

02:11:39 --> 02:11:42
and through their hunting practices

02:11:41 --> 02:11:47
that didn't really develop

02:11:46 --> 02:11:49
ractices that worked

02:11:48 --> 02:11:51
and kind of bands of people that went extinct

02:11:50 --> 02:11:54
before they could turn into larger societies.

02:11:53 --> 02:11:57
And there's a lot of people that got it wrong.

02:11:56 --> 02:12:01
You know, for every explorer that

02:12:00 --> 02:12:04
leaves Borneo and arrives in South America,

02:12:03 --> 02:12:07
there's probably hundreds more that just die at sea,

02:12:06 --> 02:12:10
get eaten by sharks, you know, avalanche.

02:12:09 --> 02:12:13
And it's just, it's so fascinating to me that we,

02:12:12 --> 02:12:15
all of us really past our grandparents

02:12:14 --> 02:12:17
don't really even know where we came from.

02:12:17 --> 02:12:19
Like, do you know who

02:12:18 --> 02:12:21
your great-great-great grandparents are?

02:12:20 --> 02:12:22
Like, no. - No.

02:12:21 --> 02:12:23
I mean, there's methods of trying to figure that out,

02:12:22 --> 02:12:25
but really again,

02:12:24 --> 02:12:26
the error bars are so large that it's almost like

02:12:25 --> 02:12:30
we're trying to create a narrative that make sense for us.

02:12:29 --> 02:12:33
You know, that I'm 10% Neanderthal,

02:12:32 --> 02:12:35
therefore I can bench press this much

02:12:34 --> 02:12:39
and therefore my aggressive tendencies have a explanation

02:12:38 --> 02:12:41
when in reality there's so much diversity of personalities

02:12:40 --> 02:12:43
that they

02:12:44 --> 02:12:49
far overshadow any possible histories we might have.

02:12:48 --> 02:12:51
- Your aggressive tendencies don't have

02:12:50 --> 02:12:52
any explanation, you're.

02:12:51 --> 02:12:55
- No, you need to, you listen to me right now.

02:12:54 --> 02:12:56
(Paul laughs)

02:12:55 --> 02:12:57
- I'm sorry, don't hit me again.

02:12:56 --> 02:12:59
Don't choke me out again.

02:12:58 --> 02:13:02
- Yeah, man, one of the things you and I talk a lot about is

02:13:01 --> 02:13:03
different explorers. - [Paul] Yeah.

02:13:04 --> 02:13:06
- Who do you think is,

02:13:07 --> 02:13:10
I'm just throwing a ridiculous question one after the other.

02:13:09 --> 02:13:12
Who do you think is the greatest explorer of all time?

02:13:11 --> 02:13:15
- Oh God, I love Shackleton but I hate the cold,

02:13:14 --> 02:13:17
so I can't, I don't really, I can't even read about it.

02:13:16 --> 02:13:18
I hate the cold so much.

02:13:17 --> 02:13:19
(Lex laughs)

02:13:18 --> 02:13:21
I can't even go there for fun.

02:13:21 --> 02:13:24
I think Percy Fawcett in the Amazon was

02:13:25 --> 02:13:29
the GOAT in terms of just sheer,

02:13:28 --> 02:13:31
the last of the Victorian era, you know,

02:13:30 --> 02:13:34
march forward, go deeper,

02:13:33 --> 02:13:38
just stop at nothing and then eventually take such big risks

02:13:37 --> 02:13:39
that you never come back.

02:13:38 --> 02:13:43
It's hard for me to relate

02:13:42 --> 02:13:45
to that kind of exploration because to me,

02:13:44 --> 02:13:46
I'm such a softie.

02:13:45 --> 02:13:48
I wouldn't wanna like leave my family behind.

02:13:47 --> 02:13:50
I wouldn't wanna, like,

02:13:49 --> 02:13:51
even if you told me that I could leave Earth

02:13:50 --> 02:13:53
and go exploring and I could go touch the Moon,

02:13:52 --> 02:13:55
I'd be like, "Nope, absolutely not."

02:13:54 --> 02:13:57
Like, the highway is dangerous enough.

02:13:56 --> 02:14:00
Like, I would never risk dying in space.

02:13:59 --> 02:14:03
This guy left his home,

02:14:02 --> 02:14:05
went out into the jungle out there

02:14:04 --> 02:14:08
with horrendous gear compared to the camping gear

02:14:07 --> 02:14:09
we have today,

02:14:08 --> 02:14:14
no headlamp, and just explored for years on end.

02:14:13 --> 02:14:15
- Well, let me actually push back,

02:14:14 --> 02:14:17
like you have that explorer,

02:14:16 --> 02:14:19
there is definitely a thing in you,

02:14:18 --> 02:14:23
just me having observed you behave in the jungle,

02:14:22 --> 02:14:24
in the world,

02:14:23 --> 02:14:28
you're pulled towards exploration, towards adventure,

02:14:27 --> 02:14:31
towards the possibility of discovering something beautiful,

02:14:30 --> 02:14:33
including like a small, little creature

02:14:32 --> 02:14:35
or like a whole new part of the rainforest,

02:14:34 --> 02:14:38
a part of the world that like is like, holy shit,

02:14:37 --> 02:14:38
this is beautiful.

02:14:37 --> 02:14:40
I think that's the same kind of imperative.

02:14:39 --> 02:14:42
So maybe not going out to the stars, but like.

02:14:41 --> 02:14:44
- [Paul] Yeah. - I could see you doing

02:14:43 --> 02:14:45
exactly the same thing.

02:14:44 --> 02:14:49
So he disappeared in 1925 during an expedition

02:14:48 --> 02:14:51
to find an ancient lost city,

02:14:50 --> 02:14:54
which he and other people believed existed

02:14:53 --> 02:14:55
in the Amazon rainforest.

02:14:54 --> 02:14:56
So there's that pull, like,

02:14:55 --> 02:14:59
I'm going to go into there

02:14:58 --> 02:15:01
with shitty equipment

02:15:00 --> 02:15:03
with the possibility of finding something.

02:15:02 --> 02:15:07
- And they said he ran into uncontacted tribes

02:15:06 --> 02:15:08
and started goofing off.

02:15:07 --> 02:15:10
I think he started,

02:15:09 --> 02:15:11
I think he started dancing and singing,

02:15:10 --> 02:15:13
like the tribes were ready to kill him.

02:15:12 --> 02:15:16
And he started goofing and like doing a song and a dance

02:15:15 --> 02:15:17
and just being ridiculous.

02:15:16 --> 02:15:19
And the tribes were like, what now?

02:15:18 --> 02:15:21
And they're like, "Wait, wait, wait, wait, wait.

02:15:20 --> 02:15:23
Don't shoot him yet, that's a funny one."

02:15:22 --> 02:15:25
- [Lex] Yeah. - And they actually,

02:15:24 --> 02:15:28
he kind of like on a human level used humor

02:15:27 --> 02:15:31
to save his own life on multiple occasions

02:15:30 --> 02:15:32
to the point where he deescalated the situation

02:15:31 --> 02:15:34
and was like, look, we're not here to fight.

02:15:33 --> 02:15:37
We're here to, we have a pile of maps, you know?

02:15:36 --> 02:15:40
All my guys have beriberi, dengue, malaria,

02:15:39 --> 02:15:41
like, we're dying out here.

02:15:40 --> 02:15:42
If you guys just go on your merry way,

02:15:41 --> 02:15:45
we'll go on our merry way and like, incredible.

02:15:44 --> 02:15:46
He was so tough.

02:15:45 --> 02:15:48
And then that guy from Shackleton's expedition

02:15:47 --> 02:15:51
ended up on one of Fawcett's expeditions

02:15:50 --> 02:15:53
and you go, oh yeah, he's a proven explorer.

02:15:52 --> 02:15:54
He's been through the Antarctic.

02:15:53 --> 02:15:56
And the guy was like, "Fuck the jungle."

02:15:55 --> 02:15:57
(Lex laughs)

02:15:56 --> 02:15:58
Absolutely fuck the jungle.

02:15:57 --> 02:16:01
He was like, and there's a great quote where he says,

02:16:00 --> 02:16:03
"Without a machete and," something, you know,

02:16:02 --> 02:16:04
I don't remember exactly the words he used,

02:16:03 --> 02:16:06
but he said, "Without a machete

02:16:05 --> 02:16:08
in this environment, you don't last."

02:16:07 --> 02:16:10
- [Lex] Yeah. - And you know that now,

02:16:09 --> 02:16:12
like you in that tangle

02:16:11 --> 02:16:15
to just take three steps that way would,

02:16:14 --> 02:16:16
I would immediately be taking on, I mean,

02:16:15 --> 02:16:18
I'm not wearing shoes right now.

02:16:17 --> 02:16:19
- [Lex] Yeah. - Bullet ants,

02:16:18 --> 02:16:21
venomous snakes, spikes through my feet,

02:16:20 --> 02:16:22
tripping over myself.

02:16:21 --> 02:16:24
I don't have a headlamp,

02:16:23 --> 02:16:26
unbelievable risk right there.

02:16:26 --> 02:16:29
We're sitting on the edge of tragedy.

02:16:29 --> 02:16:31
- Can you explain what the purpose

02:16:30 --> 02:16:33
of the machete in this situation is?

02:16:32 --> 02:16:34
Like, what is a machete?

02:16:33 --> 02:16:35
How does it work?

02:16:34 --> 02:16:37
How does it allow you to navigate

02:16:36 --> 02:16:40
in this exceptionally dense environment?

02:16:39 --> 02:16:44
- So this is the tool that I spend most of my life carrying.

02:16:43 --> 02:16:48
This is in my hand for 90% of my time.

02:16:47 --> 02:16:52
And in the jungle you really need a machete.

02:16:51 --> 02:16:54
There's so much plant life here

02:16:53 --> 02:16:56
that you have to cut your way through.

02:16:55 --> 02:16:59
And like a jaguar, an ocelot,

02:16:58 --> 02:17:01
a lot of these other animals that are

02:17:00 --> 02:17:03
more horizontally based and low to the ground,

02:17:02 --> 02:17:04
they can make it,

02:17:03 --> 02:17:05
like when we got stuck in those bamboo patches

02:17:04 --> 02:17:08
and we were just hacking through 'em and it's dangerous

02:17:07 --> 02:17:10
and there's, as you hit the bamboo,

02:17:09 --> 02:17:12
it ricochets and there's spikes

02:17:11 --> 02:17:14
and then one piece falls and it pulls a train,

02:17:13 --> 02:17:16
a vine that has spikes on it

02:17:15 --> 02:17:17
and that hits you in the neck.

02:17:16 --> 02:17:20
And it just, the jungle is savage to humans.

02:17:19 --> 02:17:23
But if you are an agouti,

02:17:22 --> 02:17:26
a little rodent or a jaguar or a deer,

02:17:25 --> 02:17:27
you can kind of slip through this stuff.

02:17:26 --> 02:17:29
And the deer have developed really small antlers.

02:17:28 --> 02:17:32
They can just kind of weave through low to the ground.

02:17:31 --> 02:17:36
And so for us being these vertical beings,

02:17:35 --> 02:17:38
walking through the jungle,

02:17:37 --> 02:17:40
it really helps to be able to move the sticks

02:17:39 --> 02:17:42
that are diagonally opposing your movement at all times.

02:17:41 --> 02:17:45
So a machete's just a very, very useful tool.

02:17:44 --> 02:17:47
It could help you pull thorns out of your body.

02:17:46 --> 02:17:50
As you saw last night, we can use it to find food.

02:17:49 --> 02:17:53
- Mm-hmm, you want machete fishing,

02:17:52 --> 02:17:56
you cut a fish head off with a machete

02:17:55 --> 02:17:59
by like it was swimming

02:17:58 --> 02:18:02
and then you basically, you know

02:18:02 --> 02:18:05
macheted the water.

02:18:04 --> 02:18:08
And the other fascinating thing about

02:18:07 --> 02:18:10
hat fish without its head, it kept moving, so.

02:18:09 --> 02:18:11
- [Paul] That was amazing.

02:18:10 --> 02:18:12
- It was just using I guess its nervous system

02:18:11 --> 02:18:15
to swim beautifully.

02:18:14 --> 02:18:16
I mean, there's so many questions there

02:18:15 --> 02:18:18
about how nature works.

02:18:17 --> 02:18:18
(Paul laughs)

02:18:17 --> 02:18:19
- Well, let's explain it 'cause you,

02:18:18 --> 02:18:21
the way the machete hit this fish,

02:18:20 --> 02:18:25
it kind of took his, just his eyes off of

02:18:24 --> 02:18:26
and his lower jaw was still there.

02:18:25 --> 02:18:27
- [Lex] Yeah. - So it was really just

02:18:26 --> 02:18:30
like the brain and the top jaw that came off.

02:18:29 --> 02:18:33
And this fish as the dust cleared in the stream,

02:18:32 --> 02:18:34
this fish was,

02:18:33 --> 02:18:37
I found it very haunting in a very like, interstellar way.

02:18:36 --> 02:18:39
Like it was just, the programming was still there,

02:18:38 --> 02:18:42
but the brain was gone and the fish was just still moving

02:18:41 --> 02:18:44
and it was gonna die, but it was still swimming.

02:18:43 --> 02:18:45
- [Lex] Yeah. - And it looked like a

02:18:44 --> 02:18:46
live fish.

02:18:45 --> 02:18:47
It was something.

02:18:46 --> 02:18:48
- And you're still trying to catch it,

02:18:47 --> 02:18:50
which is interesting watch, yeah.

02:18:49 --> 02:18:50
- And I had to work to catch it because every time

02:18:49 --> 02:18:52
I caught it, it would freak out.

02:18:51 --> 02:18:53
- [Lex] Yeah. - And then it would

02:18:52 --> 02:18:54
jump back in the water.

02:18:53 --> 02:18:55
And I'm programmed here from years and years

02:18:54 --> 02:18:57
of living in the Amazon that everything can hurt you.

02:18:56 --> 02:19:00
So you actually become quite, you know,

02:19:00 --> 02:19:02
if a moth lands on you, you flick it

02:19:01 --> 02:19:03
because it could be a bullet ant.

02:19:02 --> 02:19:04
And so even the fish here,

02:19:03 --> 02:19:06
a lot of the fish here have spikes coming out of them.

02:19:05 --> 02:19:08
And so even though I know that fish,

02:19:07 --> 02:19:11
I know its name, I've eaten them many times,

02:19:10 --> 02:19:12
as I was holding it,

02:19:11 --> 02:19:14
when it would twitch with that explosive power,

02:19:13 --> 02:19:15
just like the caiman,

02:19:14 --> 02:19:18
I would get that fear response and release it.

02:19:17 --> 02:19:20
And so that happened three or four times

02:19:19 --> 02:19:21
before I finally said, this is stupid.

02:19:20 --> 02:19:24
Even though he is slippery, he hasn't got a head.

02:19:24 --> 02:19:25
I can hold onto him.

02:19:24 --> 02:19:26
I put him in my pocket.

02:19:25 --> 02:19:28
- Yeah, put him in pocket.

02:19:27 --> 02:19:29
- [Paul] And then we fried him up and we.

02:19:28 --> 02:19:32
- And he was delicious.

02:19:31 --> 02:19:35
So, and I'm grateful for his existence and for his role

02:19:34 --> 02:19:36
and for my existence on this planet,

02:19:35 --> 02:19:39
this brief existence that I was able to enjoy

02:19:38 --> 02:19:41
that delicious, delicious fish.

02:19:40 --> 02:19:43
So the machete is used to cut through

02:19:42 --> 02:19:44
this extremely dense jungle.

02:19:43 --> 02:19:46
There's vines, by the way, there's rope-like things.

02:19:45 --> 02:19:49
- [Paul] Yeah. - That are extremely strong.

02:19:48 --> 02:19:51
And they go all kinds of directions, they go horizontal

02:19:50 --> 02:19:53
and all of this, I don't even,

02:19:53 --> 02:19:58
how tree, we have a tree right above us that makes no sense.

02:19:57 --> 02:19:59
(Paul laughs)

02:19:58 --> 02:20:02
There's like a tree that kind of failed

02:20:01 --> 02:20:05
and then a new tree was created on top of it.

02:20:04 --> 02:20:08
That makes, it just makes no sense.

02:20:07 --> 02:20:08
- [Paul] Yeah. - It feels like

02:20:07 --> 02:20:11
sometimes trees come from the sky.

02:20:10 --> 02:20:13
Sometimes they come from the ground.

02:20:12 --> 02:20:17
I don't really quite understand the, how that works

02:20:16 --> 02:20:20
'cause there's new trees that grow on old trees

02:20:19 --> 02:20:23
and the old trees rot away and the new trees come up.

02:20:22 --> 02:20:24
- Yeah. - [Lex] That whole mechanism.

02:20:23 --> 02:20:27
- Strangler figs, and so strangler figs,

02:20:26 --> 02:20:28
as you go across the world's ecosystems,

02:20:27 --> 02:20:30
that whole belt of, you know,

02:20:29 --> 02:20:32
whether you're in rainforest in the Amazon,

02:20:31 --> 02:20:35
the Congo, Indonesia,

02:20:34 --> 02:20:37
all across the tropics you have strangler figs.

02:20:36 --> 02:20:40
And the amazing thing that this species does,

02:20:39 --> 02:20:43
it's become a keystone species across the planet

02:20:43 --> 02:20:46
with a hyper-influence on its ecosystem wherever it is

02:20:45 --> 02:20:49
because they produce fruit in the dry season

02:20:48 --> 02:20:52
when the rest of the forest is making it hard

02:20:51 --> 02:20:54
for animals to find fruit, to find food.

02:20:53 --> 02:20:56
And so the bats, the birds, the monkeys,

02:20:55 --> 02:20:57
they all go to the strangler fig.

02:20:56 --> 02:20:59
They eat the fruit and the fruit of course is

02:20:58 --> 02:21:01
just tricking the animals.

02:21:00 --> 02:21:03
The plants are tricking the animals

02:21:02 --> 02:21:05
into carrying their seeds to another tree.

02:21:04 --> 02:21:08
And so they're getting free transportation.

02:21:07 --> 02:21:09
Monkey takes a poop on another tree

02:21:08 --> 02:21:10
after eating strangler figs

02:21:09 --> 02:21:14
and then that strangler fig sends out its vines,

02:21:13 --> 02:21:15
gets to the ground,

02:21:14 --> 02:21:18
and then as soon as it begins sucking up nutrients,

02:21:17 --> 02:21:20
out-competes that tree for light,

02:21:20 --> 02:21:26
grows hyper-drive around the trunk of that tree.

02:21:25 --> 02:21:27
And then eventually that tree will die

02:21:26 --> 02:21:30
and the strangler fig will win

02:21:29 --> 02:21:32
because it got a boost up to the top.

02:21:31 --> 02:21:33
Whereas these little trees down here,

02:21:32 --> 02:21:34
they're gonna have to wait their turn.

02:21:33 --> 02:21:36
They have to wait until a tree falls

02:21:35 --> 02:21:37
until there's a light gap.

02:21:36 --> 02:21:39
And then they have enough food to grow quick.

02:21:38 --> 02:21:42
And so this whole thing is an energy economy.

02:21:41 --> 02:21:43
Everything is just trying to get sunlight.

02:21:42 --> 02:21:46
And so strangler figs, yeah,

02:21:45 --> 02:21:48
top-down trees growing over,

02:21:47 --> 02:21:50
parasitic, top-down octopus trees

02:21:49 --> 02:21:52
growing over other giant trees.

02:21:51 --> 02:21:54
And you've seen the size of some of the trees here.

02:21:53 --> 02:21:57
- So you know, back to Percy Fawcett and exploration,

02:21:56 --> 02:22:00
what do you think it was like for him back then,

02:21:59 --> 02:22:04
100 years ago, God damn, going to the jungle?

02:22:03 --> 02:22:06
- Well the, see the thing is those guys didn't go

02:22:05 --> 02:22:07
with the locals. - [Lex] Yeah.

02:22:06 --> 02:22:09
- They came down here with like mules

02:22:08 --> 02:22:11
and they tried to do it their way.

02:22:10 --> 02:22:12
- [Lex] Yeah. - And so he's

02:22:11 --> 02:22:15
one of the people that wrote about the green hell,

02:22:15 --> 02:22:20
the jungle as the oppressive war zone

02:22:19 --> 02:22:21
where there's nothing to eat

02:22:20 --> 02:22:23
and everything is killing you.

02:22:22 --> 02:22:27
And it's, I think that image

02:22:26 --> 02:22:29
is so wrong

02:22:28 --> 02:22:31
'cause as you saw last night, we could go,

02:22:30 --> 02:22:33
if we went out with JJ right now,

02:22:32 --> 02:22:36
we would machete fish some fish,

02:22:35 --> 02:22:37
we could start a little fire.

02:22:36 --> 02:22:39
We'd do it all in shorts.

02:22:38 --> 02:22:42
Like to JJ, it's green paradise.

02:22:41 --> 02:22:44
And it's intense but if you know what you're doing,

02:22:43 --> 02:22:47
which the local people surely do,

02:22:46 --> 02:22:49
well then just beneath the sand there's turtle eggs

02:22:48 --> 02:22:51
that you can eat.

02:22:50 --> 02:22:52
And inside the nuts on the ground

02:22:51 --> 02:22:54
there's grubs that you can eat.

02:22:53 --> 02:22:56
And if you really needed to,

02:22:55 --> 02:22:58
you could just jump on a caiman and eat that

02:22:57 --> 02:23:00
'cause their tails are pretty full of meat.

02:23:00 --> 02:23:04
And it's like there's actually unending

02:23:03 --> 02:23:06
amounts of food here.

02:23:05 --> 02:23:08
And so it's, they were pretty, you know,

02:23:07 --> 02:23:09
they were a strange bunch.

02:23:08 --> 02:23:11
- If you were able to tune into the, that frequency,

02:23:10 --> 02:23:14
I feel like your, you and JJ.

02:23:13 --> 02:23:15
- [Paul] Yeah. - Are able to tune

02:23:14 --> 02:23:19
to the frequency of the jungle

02:23:18 --> 02:23:21
that is a provider,

02:23:20 --> 02:23:24
not a destroyer of human life, right?

02:23:23 --> 02:23:26
- [Paul] Yeah. - Like I think

02:23:25 --> 02:23:31
to be collaborated with, not fought against.

02:23:30 --> 02:23:32
- Yes, but we're coming at that with a,

02:23:31 --> 02:23:33
with our modern lens

02:23:32 --> 02:23:35
'cause we're coming down here with

02:23:34 --> 02:23:37
I've survived how many infections in the jungle.

02:23:36 --> 02:23:38
- [Lex] Sure. - Where those probably

02:23:37 --> 02:23:39
would've killed me before.

02:23:38 --> 02:23:41
- [Lex] Yeah. - So my dead ass

02:23:40 --> 02:23:44
opinion of the jungle would've been overwhelming

02:23:43 --> 02:23:46
and collective murder as Herzog says.

02:23:47 --> 02:23:49
And so Percy Fawcett was coming down here

02:23:48 --> 02:23:52
with this view of it's trying to kill us at all times.

02:23:51 --> 02:23:54
But we are flying down here

02:23:53 --> 02:23:56
and coming out here with our superior medicines

02:23:55 --> 02:23:59
and our ability to survive infections.

02:23:58 --> 02:24:02
And so it is different for us, it is different.

02:24:01 --> 02:24:04
We're coming at this very, very different,

02:24:03 --> 02:24:07
but Fawcett to me was

02:24:06 --> 02:24:09
like the last of like the real swashbucklers,

02:24:08 --> 02:24:13
like the really batshit crazy explorers

02:24:12 --> 02:24:17
that just went out into the dark spaces on the map.

02:24:17 --> 02:24:20
And it's very hard for me to identify with him.

02:24:19 --> 02:24:25
But with, for instance, Richard Evans Schultes from Harvard,

02:24:24 --> 02:24:27
that's someone where you go, okay,

02:24:26 --> 02:24:29
now we're getting to the point

02:24:28 --> 02:24:32
where I can start to understand.

02:24:31 --> 02:24:33
I mean, just like the conquistadors.

02:24:32 --> 02:24:34
And they tell you the conquistadors showed up and, you know,

02:24:33 --> 02:24:39
they killed, the Spanish killed 2,000 Inca on the first day.

02:24:38 --> 02:24:41
And then they marched to this city and they're like,

02:24:40 --> 02:24:42
when I hear about the,

02:24:41 --> 02:24:45
can you imagine yourself just like slaughtering

02:24:44 --> 02:24:47
a bunch of women and children and soldiers

02:24:46 --> 02:24:49
and then just like drinking some wine

02:24:48 --> 02:24:49
and doing it again tomorrow?

02:24:48 --> 02:24:51
- [Lex] Yes. - Like, I can't actually

02:24:50 --> 02:24:53
wrap my head around that.

02:24:52 --> 02:24:57
- Yeah, it just seems like an entire different world.

02:24:56 --> 02:24:58
- Nah, like different world.

02:24:57 --> 02:25:00
- Different value system.

02:24:59 --> 02:25:01
- [Paul] Different value system.

02:25:00 --> 02:25:03
- A different relationship with violence and life and death.

02:25:02 --> 02:25:05
I think we value life more.

02:25:04 --> 02:25:09
We value, we resist violence more.

02:25:08 --> 02:25:12
- Yeah, like I just, I can't,

02:25:11 --> 02:25:13
like if we saw a car accident,

02:25:12 --> 02:25:15
I feel like if I saw a car accident,

02:25:14 --> 02:25:17
like, you know, or if you see a little bit of war,

02:25:16 --> 02:25:19
some violence, like it affects you.

02:25:18 --> 02:25:22
These people were so comfortable with those things.

02:25:21 --> 02:25:23
- [Lex] Yeah. - It was such

02:25:22 --> 02:25:24
a normal part of their,

02:25:23 --> 02:25:27
the Spartans, the Comanches.

02:25:26 --> 02:25:30
Like, they became so comfortable with war

02:25:29 --> 02:25:34
to the point that it became what they did.

02:25:33 --> 02:25:34
- And they celebrated it too.

02:25:33 --> 02:25:36
- [Paul] They celebrated it.

02:25:35 --> 02:25:37
- And direct violence too.

02:25:36 --> 02:25:40
Like, taking that machete and murdering me,

02:25:39 --> 02:25:43
or if I got to the machete first, me murdering you.

02:25:42 --> 02:25:44
- Not a chance, bitch.

02:25:43 --> 02:25:47
- I, and then I would put it on Instagram and show off.

02:25:46 --> 02:25:48
- [Paul] (indistinct).

02:25:47 --> 02:25:49
(Paul and Lex laugh)

02:25:48 --> 02:25:51
- And the number of DMs I would get

02:25:50 --> 02:25:53
from murdering you with a machete.

02:25:52 --> 02:25:56
- Meanwhile, half the world right now is messaging me,

02:25:55 --> 02:25:59
saying my DMs are filled with take care of Lex,

02:25:58 --> 02:25:59
don't lose Lex.

02:25:58 --> 02:26:01
Make sure Lex comes back safe.

02:26:00 --> 02:26:02
Lex is a national treasure.

02:26:01 --> 02:26:03
We love Lex.

02:26:02 --> 02:26:05
Make sure he holds a snake. - [Lex] Yeah.

02:26:04 --> 02:26:07
- The amount of love that is out there.

02:26:06 --> 02:26:08
- Meanwhile, I emerge from the jungle,

02:26:07 --> 02:26:11
blood around me with a machete

02:26:10 --> 02:26:12
and I take over your Instagram account.

02:26:11 --> 02:26:14
- He's very humble, he doesn't want to hear about the love.

02:26:13 --> 02:26:16
(Paul and Lex laugh)

02:26:15 --> 02:26:19
- All right, so what do you think makes a great explorer?

02:26:18 --> 02:26:22
Whether it's Percy Fawcett, Richard Evans Schultes,

02:26:21 --> 02:26:25
by the way, say who Richard Evans Schultes is.

02:26:24 --> 02:26:26
He's a biologist.

02:26:25 --> 02:26:28
So that's another lens through which to be an explorer.

02:26:27 --> 02:26:30
- [Paul] Yeah. - Is to study

02:26:29 --> 02:26:32
the biology,

02:26:31 --> 02:26:37
the immense diversity of biological life all around us.

02:26:36 --> 02:26:39
- Richard Evan Schultes,

02:26:38 --> 02:26:42
I know about him from reading Wade Davis' book "One River",

02:26:41 --> 02:26:44
which is this big, hefty, you know,

02:26:43 --> 02:26:47
five or 600 page tome about the Amazon.

02:26:46 --> 02:26:48
And it covers two stories.

02:26:47 --> 02:26:51
It's Richard Evan Schultes and I think it's in the '40s,

02:26:50 --> 02:26:54
I think it's like pre-World War II era,

02:26:53 --> 02:26:57
where he's in the Amazon looking for the blue orchid

02:26:56 --> 02:26:58
and the cure for this and that.

02:26:57 --> 02:26:59
And he's pressing plants

02:26:58 --> 02:27:02
and he's going to these Indigenous communities

02:27:01 --> 02:27:04
where they still live completely with the forest.

02:27:03 --> 02:27:08
And they drink ayahuasca and they talk to the gods

02:27:07 --> 02:27:10
and they, he learns about how they believed

02:27:09 --> 02:27:12
that the anaconda came down from the Milky Way

02:27:11 --> 02:27:14
and swam across the land and created the rivers.

02:27:13 --> 02:27:18
And sort of, he came down and even though he was

02:27:17 --> 02:27:21
a Western scientist from Harvard,

02:27:20 --> 02:27:25
he embraced the Indigenous perspective on the world,

02:27:24 --> 02:27:28
on creation, on spirituality.

02:27:27 --> 02:27:31
And he sort of resigned himself

02:27:30 --> 02:27:32
and gave himself fully to that

02:27:31 --> 02:27:35
and spent years and years traveling around

02:27:34 --> 02:27:37
parts of the Amazon that had hardly been explored

02:27:36 --> 02:27:40
and certainly never been explored

02:27:39 --> 02:27:42
in the way he was doing it,

02:27:41 --> 02:27:44
in the ethno-botanical, spiritual way

02:27:43 --> 02:27:48
of what medicinal compounds are contained in these plants

02:27:47 --> 02:27:51
and how do the local Indigenous people use

02:27:51 --> 02:27:52
and understand them?

02:27:51 --> 02:27:56
For example, you know, of 80,000 species of plants

02:27:55 --> 02:27:57
in the Amazon rainforest

02:27:56 --> 02:28:00
and 400 billion trees in the Amazon rainforest,

02:27:59 --> 02:28:04
the statistics of likelihood

02:28:03 --> 02:28:06
that through trial and error

02:28:05 --> 02:28:09
that humans could discover ayahuasca,

02:28:10 --> 02:28:12
it's astronomical,

02:28:11 --> 02:28:15
that one of these trees and a root when put together

02:28:14 --> 02:28:18
allow you to go and access the spirit realm

02:28:18 --> 02:28:22
and see hallucinogenic shapes and talk to the gods.

02:28:21 --> 02:28:27
That's almost enough to inspire

02:28:26 --> 02:28:28
spiritual thought itself.

02:28:27 --> 02:28:30
The fact that trial and error,

02:28:29 --> 02:28:32
it would take like millions of years or something.

02:28:31 --> 02:28:33
It's, I forget what the figure is.

02:28:32 --> 02:28:35
It's incredible, but Richard Evan Schultes was

02:28:34 --> 02:28:38
one of the first people that came down and saw that.

02:28:37 --> 02:28:40
And then "One River" is where Wade Davis comes back,

02:28:39 --> 02:28:42
I believe in the '70s,

02:28:41 --> 02:28:44
and the heartbreak of the book is

02:28:43 --> 02:28:47
that all of these incredibly wild places

02:28:46 --> 02:28:52
with naked, native tribes and these intact belief systems,

02:28:53 --> 02:28:57
Wade Davis comes back and a lot of the same places

02:28:56 --> 02:28:58
that Schultes went,

02:28:57 --> 02:29:02
now there's missionary schools

02:29:01 --> 02:29:04
and they're wearing discarded Nikes

02:29:03 --> 02:29:06
and you know, whatever.

02:29:05 --> 02:29:07
I don't know if there's Nikes in the '70s,

02:29:06 --> 02:29:10
but like Western stuff has made it in.

02:29:09 --> 02:29:14
They've been contacted, domesticated,

02:29:13 --> 02:29:15
forced into Western society.

02:29:14 --> 02:29:19
And you know, a lot of them then forget

02:29:18 --> 02:29:21
he thousands and thousands of years

02:29:20 --> 02:29:24
that have gone into creating

02:29:23 --> 02:29:26
the medicinal, botanical knowledge

02:29:25 --> 02:29:29
that the Indigenous possess about how to cure ear infections

02:29:28 --> 02:29:31
and how to treat illnesses

02:29:30 --> 02:29:35
from the medicinal compounds flowing through these trees

02:29:34 --> 02:29:39
is lost in a single generation with the modernization.

02:29:38 --> 02:29:42
- Yeah, he wrote the "Plants of the Gods:

02:29:41 --> 02:29:46
Their Sacred, Healing and Hallucinogenic Powers".

02:29:45 --> 02:29:47
That is interesting,

02:29:46 --> 02:29:50
you mentioned like how to discover that.

02:29:49 --> 02:29:52
Like, how do you find those incredible plants,

02:29:51 --> 02:29:56
those incredible things that can warp your mind

02:29:55 --> 02:29:57
in all kinds of ways?

02:29:57 --> 02:30:01
Of course physically heal,

02:30:00 --> 02:30:03
but also like take you on a mental journey.

02:30:02 --> 02:30:04
That's interesting.

02:30:03 --> 02:30:06
So you don't think trial and error is possible?

02:30:05 --> 02:30:09
- I was reading about ayahuasca and they're saying

02:30:08 --> 02:30:13
statistically, if, you know, if a bunch,

02:30:12 --> 02:30:15
if you put 1,000 humans in the Amazon

02:30:14 --> 02:30:17
and gave them villages to live in,

02:30:16 --> 02:30:20
'cause humans are communalist species,

02:30:19 --> 02:30:22
it would take tens and tens of thousands of years

02:30:21 --> 02:30:26
or perhaps even centuries before even the possibility.

02:30:25 --> 02:30:27
It's like that thing, you know,

02:30:26 --> 02:30:28
a bunch of chimps on a keyboard,

02:30:27 --> 02:30:29
how could they write Hamlet?

02:30:28 --> 02:30:34
It's like astronomical odds to get to, oh wait,

02:30:34 --> 02:30:36
this and this dosed together.

02:30:35 --> 02:30:38
And so what the local people believe is

02:30:37 --> 02:30:42
that the gods revealed this secret

02:30:42 --> 02:30:47
hrough the jungle to us as a link to the spirit world.

02:30:46 --> 02:30:50
And that that's how we know this,

02:30:49 --> 02:30:52
because if they didn't remember it from their ancestors,

02:30:51 --> 02:30:54
we would have no idea how to get this information

02:30:53 --> 02:30:56
from the wild.

02:30:55 --> 02:30:57
- So

02:30:57 --> 02:31:00
I will likely do ayahuasca.

02:31:01 --> 02:31:06
What do you think exists

02:31:05 --> 02:31:08
in the spirit world

02:31:07 --> 02:31:10
that could be found by taking that journey?

02:31:10 --> 02:31:14
- I think that ayahuasca is,

02:31:13 --> 02:31:17
I can only speak from personal experience.

02:31:17 --> 02:31:20
And for me it was

02:31:20 --> 02:31:25
if your brain is a house you've lived in your entire life

02:31:24 --> 02:31:27
and it's a big house, it's a mansion

02:31:26 --> 02:31:28
and there's many, many rooms

02:31:27 --> 02:31:29
that you didn't even know exist,

02:31:28 --> 02:31:32
hidden rooms behind the bookshelves,

02:31:31 --> 02:31:33
under the floorboards,

02:31:32 --> 02:31:35
rooms that you had no idea were there.

02:31:35 --> 02:31:38
And some of them are fantastic

02:31:37 --> 02:31:40
and some of them are terrifying basements.

02:31:40 --> 02:31:45
And ayahuasca takes you on a journey through that.

02:31:44 --> 02:31:48
At its most effective,

02:31:47 --> 02:31:51
you sit in front of the shaman with the candlelight,

02:31:50 --> 02:31:53
with the sounds of the jungle,

02:31:52 --> 02:31:57
and you drink the substance.

02:31:56 --> 02:32:00
And after that, what happens is

02:32:00 --> 02:32:02
the journey is all inside

02:32:01 --> 02:32:04
and the shaman's supposed to be able

02:32:03 --> 02:32:05
to guide you through that.

02:32:04 --> 02:32:10
But in my experience, you're so deep inside,

02:32:09 --> 02:32:13
like falling through nebulas out in space,

02:32:12 --> 02:32:15
no physical form,

02:32:14 --> 02:32:16
or crawling through the jungle.

02:32:15 --> 02:32:19
Like, it's like you, it's really, really powerful.

02:32:18 --> 02:32:20
Like, it's not like a,

02:32:19 --> 02:32:24
it's not like the recreational drugs that everyone does.

02:32:23 --> 02:32:26
Like where you go, I did mushrooms and I could see,

02:32:25 --> 02:32:27
so I could see music like,

02:32:26 --> 02:32:30
and I was talking to my friends about, no, no, no.

02:32:29 --> 02:32:32
Like, you're face down on the floor usually vomiting,

02:32:31 --> 02:32:35
sometimes shitting, you know,

02:32:34 --> 02:32:39
having dialogues with the creator and that can be,

02:32:38 --> 02:32:42
that can be traumatizing as well as amazing.

02:32:41 --> 02:32:45
- It's a really good way of looking at it.

02:32:44 --> 02:32:47
It's a big house and you get to open doors

02:32:46 --> 02:32:49
that you've never had before

02:32:48 --> 02:32:51
and discover what rooms are there inside you.

02:32:50 --> 02:32:52
You ever think about that?

02:32:51 --> 02:32:54
Like, that there's parts of yourself

02:32:53 --> 02:32:55
you haven't discovered yet,

02:32:54 --> 02:32:57
or maybe you've been suppressing?

02:32:56 --> 02:33:01
How much are you exploring the shadow?

02:33:00 --> 02:33:02
- Oh boy.

02:33:01 --> 02:33:05
- [Lex] So say you, me, Carl Jung and Jordan Peterson are

02:33:04 --> 02:33:06
in a deserted island together.

02:33:05 --> 02:33:08
- Fuck, I didn't even make my bed today.

02:33:07 --> 02:33:09
(Lex laughs)

02:33:08 --> 02:33:11
- There's no bed on an island.

02:33:10 --> 02:33:11
- Great.

02:33:14 --> 02:33:18
I wanna see you and Jordan Peterson do ayahuasca together.

02:33:19 --> 02:33:23
I think I, that's the thing.

02:33:22 --> 02:33:25
Ayahuasca to me, you know,

02:33:24 --> 02:33:27
I've kind of told you about like I've experienced

02:33:26 --> 02:33:29
some things that really made me believe

02:33:28 --> 02:33:32
that there's a benevolent force around us.

02:33:31 --> 02:33:36
But to me, ayahuasca was like a,

02:33:36 --> 02:33:41
was a ride through the scariest parts of the universe

02:33:40 --> 02:33:44
to sort of be like, here's what it could be like, you know?

02:33:43 --> 02:33:48
The, that's where I came up with my idea that, you know,

02:33:47 --> 02:33:51
like deep space or just space, outer space,

02:33:50 --> 02:33:52
it's just the outside of the video game.

02:33:51 --> 02:33:53
And this is it.

02:33:52 --> 02:33:55
Because when I was on ayahuasca,

02:33:54 --> 02:33:58
I was one of the jungle creatures and I wasn't Paul

02:33:57 --> 02:34:00
and I didn't have a name.

02:33:59 --> 02:34:03
And for a long time I saw many things and I was,

02:34:02 --> 02:34:05
I arrived at this spot in the jungle

02:34:04 --> 02:34:06
where there's a big tree and all the animals were there

02:34:05 --> 02:34:08
and they were all, not in words,

02:34:07 --> 02:34:10
not in any language that we can understand,

02:34:09 --> 02:34:12
but they were all discussing what to do

02:34:11 --> 02:34:14
about the threat and the, and it was all,

02:34:13 --> 02:34:15
it was all leaving.

02:34:14 --> 02:34:18
It was all flying up and it was fire

02:34:17 --> 02:34:19
and the jungle was being destroyed.

02:34:18 --> 02:34:20
And it was like, I went,

02:34:19 --> 02:34:24
and then after that it was just space and stars and silence,

02:34:23 --> 02:34:29
like crushing, vacuum silence for years.

02:34:28 --> 02:34:30
And that was terrifying.

02:34:29 --> 02:34:31
That was fucking terrifying.

02:34:30 --> 02:34:33
When I came back and I had hands, man,

02:34:34 --> 02:34:36
I could remember my own name.

02:34:37 --> 02:34:41
- You grounded, things are simpler.

02:34:40 --> 02:34:43
You're back inside the video game.

02:34:42 --> 02:34:44
What are the chances you think

02:34:43 --> 02:34:46
we're actually living in a video game?

02:34:46 --> 02:34:48
- When you say a video game,

02:34:47 --> 02:34:49
it implies that there's a player.

02:34:48 --> 02:34:50
Who's the player?

02:34:49 --> 02:34:51
Is it God?

02:34:50 --> 02:34:52
- No, there's a main player usually,

02:34:51 --> 02:34:53
that's not gonna be God.

02:34:52 --> 02:34:55
God is the thing that creates the video game.

02:34:54 --> 02:34:56
- Oh, so then we're just.

02:34:55 --> 02:34:58
- And there's somebody's, there's NPCs,

02:34:57 --> 02:35:00
like I'm an NPC, you're an NPC.

02:34:59 --> 02:35:02
- Jesus Christ, so I'm the main character.

02:35:01 --> 02:35:04
- Yeah, you created me.

02:35:03 --> 02:35:06
- Is this like Halo where you can kind of kill the NPCs?

02:35:05 --> 02:35:07
'Cause.

02:35:06 --> 02:35:08
(Paul laughs)

02:35:07 --> 02:35:10
- I see how you put the machete behind you.

02:35:09 --> 02:35:13
- Okay, I think I'm just gonna take a stand here.

02:35:12 --> 02:35:14
I think that because people,

02:35:13 --> 02:35:16
I'm just sick of fucking playing it halfway.

02:35:15 --> 02:35:18
I think that because people live indoors

02:35:17 --> 02:35:21
in climate controlled boxes

02:35:20 --> 02:35:23
in cities far away from nature,

02:35:22 --> 02:35:25
they've completely lost track of everything that's real

02:35:24 --> 02:35:26
and they've started to think that we're living

02:35:25 --> 02:35:28
inside of a simulation.

02:35:27 --> 02:35:30
Notice that nobody carrying an alpaca up a mountain thinks

02:35:29 --> 02:35:32
that we're living inside of a video game.

02:35:31 --> 02:35:33
- [Lex] Yeah. - They all know that it's real

02:35:32 --> 02:35:34
- [Lex] Yeah. - Because they've had babies

02:35:33 --> 02:35:36
on the floor of a cold hut.

02:35:35 --> 02:35:37
- [Lex] Yeah. - They understand

02:35:36 --> 02:35:38
the consequences of life.

02:35:37 --> 02:35:40
They understand the fish and how hard it is to get them

02:35:39 --> 02:35:44
and the basic rules of the wind and the rain and the river

02:35:43 --> 02:35:46
and that we all have to play by those.

02:35:45 --> 02:35:48
And that it's, and you talk to a,

02:35:47 --> 02:35:50
talk to a grieving mother

02:35:49 --> 02:35:52
and ask her if she's living inside a video game.

02:35:51 --> 02:35:55
And it's like the people, to me,

02:35:54 --> 02:35:57
this whole thing of, oh, are we living in a simulation?

02:35:56 --> 02:35:59
To me that's a,

02:35:58 --> 02:36:04
that's the infirmary of society starting to that,

02:36:03 --> 02:36:05
to starting to

02:36:06 --> 02:36:08
parody itself.

02:36:07 --> 02:36:10
It's people going, I have no meaning in my life anymore.

02:36:09 --> 02:36:13
So is this even real?

02:36:12 --> 02:36:16
And again, go ask the Sherpa, go ask the Eskimo.

02:36:15 --> 02:36:17
They're not, they're not worried.

02:36:16 --> 02:36:19
- You forget what fundamentally matters in life.

02:36:18 --> 02:36:22
What is the source of meaning in a human life?

02:36:23 --> 02:36:26
If you talk about such subjects.

02:36:25 --> 02:36:29
Nevertheless, you could for a time stroll

02:36:28 --> 02:36:31
in the big philosophical questions.

02:36:30 --> 02:36:34
- [Paul] Sure. - And if you do it

02:36:33 --> 02:36:35
for short enough of time,

02:36:34 --> 02:36:37
you won't forget about the things that matter,

02:36:36 --> 02:36:38
that there is human suffering,

02:36:37 --> 02:36:42
that there is real human joy that is real.

02:36:42 --> 02:36:47
That the, our time in the jungle

02:36:46 --> 02:36:49
was very hard.

02:36:50 --> 02:36:53
- Did you suffer enough to know that it's real?

02:36:52 --> 02:36:56
- Yeah, I, man, I was hoping we were in a video game

02:36:55 --> 02:36:57
that whole time.

02:36:56 --> 02:37:00
- So that's actually a really good way to,

02:36:59 --> 02:37:03
there was this moment that I watched

02:37:02 --> 02:37:06
where you were washing a shirt in this pathetic puddle
