02:37:05 --> 02:37:08
'cause we had no water

02:37:07 --> 02:37:10
and 'cause we had walked all day and tripped all day

02:37:09 --> 02:37:13
and gotten thorns in our hands and our feet and our legs.

02:37:12 --> 02:37:16
And we were lost in the jungle and was nighttime

02:37:15 --> 02:37:19
and we didn't know if a big tree was gonna just fall on us

02:37:18 --> 02:37:21
and mousetrap kill us.

02:37:20 --> 02:37:23
And there's a lot of uncertainty.

02:37:22 --> 02:37:26
But I watched something very special happen to you,

02:37:25 --> 02:37:30
and that was I saw you crouching by the side of this puddle.

02:37:29 --> 02:37:33
It wasn't even a flowing stream, so we couldn't drink it.

02:37:32 --> 02:37:36
And you were just trying to wash the sweat

02:37:35 --> 02:37:38
off of your shirt.

02:37:37 --> 02:37:40
And you looked at me and you just said,

02:37:39 --> 02:37:43
"The only thing that I care about right now is water."

02:37:42 --> 02:37:45
- Mm-hmm. - And I feel like

02:37:44 --> 02:37:48
in that moment we were united in the simple reality

02:37:47 --> 02:37:51
of the fact that we were so thirsty that it hurt

02:37:51 --> 02:37:54
and that it was a little scary.

02:37:55 --> 02:37:57
- Yeah,

02:37:57 --> 02:38:01
it was scary but also there's like a

02:38:04 --> 02:38:08
joy in the interaction with the water

02:38:07 --> 02:38:12
because it cools your body temperature down.

02:38:11 --> 02:38:15
And there's like a faith in that interaction

02:38:14 --> 02:38:18
that eventually we'll find clean water

02:38:17 --> 02:38:21
because water's plentiful on Earth.

02:38:20 --> 02:38:23
It's kind of like a delusional faith.

02:38:22 --> 02:38:24
And eventually we'll find,

02:38:24 --> 02:38:27
and it was just like a little celebration.

02:38:28 --> 02:38:33
I think the cooling aspect of the water,

02:38:32 --> 02:38:35
'cause you know, the body temperature's really high

02:38:34 --> 02:38:38
from traversing the really dense jungle.

02:38:37 --> 02:38:43
And just the cooling was somehow grounding

02:38:42 --> 02:38:45
in a way that nothing else really is.

02:38:44 --> 02:38:47
Yeah, it was a little celebration of life,

02:38:46 --> 02:38:51
of life on Earth, of Earth, of the jungle, of everything.

02:38:50 --> 02:38:53
It was a nice, it was a nice moment.

02:38:52 --> 02:38:54
I think about that.

02:38:53 --> 02:38:56
Had a couple of those.

02:38:55 --> 02:38:58
There was one in the puddle and one in the river.

02:38:57 --> 02:38:59
(Lex laughs)

02:38:58 --> 02:39:02
One was full of delusion and fear

02:39:01 --> 02:39:06
and the other one was full of relief and celebration.

02:39:07 --> 02:39:10
- Yeah, I've, you know,

02:39:09 --> 02:39:12
there's this thing that they say where the,

02:39:11 --> 02:39:16
all the pleasure in life is derived from the transitions.

02:39:15 --> 02:39:19
When you're cold, warm feels good.

02:39:18 --> 02:39:20
When you're hot, cold feels good.

02:39:19 --> 02:39:23
When you're hungry, food feels good.

02:39:22 --> 02:39:27
And when you're that thirsty, water becomes God.

02:39:26 --> 02:39:28
And it's all you want.

02:39:28 --> 02:39:31
And also, the other thing is that when you're,

02:39:30 --> 02:39:34
when we're out there, it felt so good to be so lost

02:39:33 --> 02:39:36
and so tired and so,

02:39:35 --> 02:39:37
like we were doing level,

02:39:36 --> 02:39:41
like how would you describe the physicality

02:39:40 --> 02:39:43
of what we were doing?

02:39:42 --> 02:39:45
The level of physical like exertion?

02:39:44 --> 02:39:49
- Well, it's something that I've haven't trained.

02:39:48 --> 02:39:50
I don't even know how you would train

02:39:49 --> 02:39:51
for that kind of thing.

02:39:50 --> 02:39:53
But it's extremely dense jungle,

02:39:52 --> 02:39:58
so every single step is like completely unpredictable

02:39:57 --> 02:40:01
in terms of the terrain your foot interacts with.

02:40:00 --> 02:40:04
So the different variety of slippery

02:40:03 --> 02:40:07
that is on the jungle floor is fascinating

02:40:06 --> 02:40:09
'cause some things, I mean, the slope matters,

02:40:09 --> 02:40:13
but some roots of trees are slippery, some are not.

02:40:13 --> 02:40:17
Some trees in the ground already rotted through

02:40:16 --> 02:40:18
so if you step through, you're going to

02:40:18 --> 02:40:20
potentially fall through.

02:40:19 --> 02:40:23
So it could be a shallow hole

02:40:22 --> 02:40:24
or it could be a very deep hole

02:40:23 --> 02:40:29
with some leaves and vegetation covering up a hole

02:40:28 --> 02:40:31
where if you fall through, you could break a leg

02:40:30 --> 02:40:34
and completely lose your footing or fall rolling downhill.

02:40:33 --> 02:40:35
And if you roll downhill,

02:40:34 --> 02:40:40
I'm pretty sure there's a 99% probability

02:40:39 --> 02:40:42
that you'll hit a thing with spikes on it.

02:40:41 --> 02:40:43
(Paul laughs)

02:40:42 --> 02:40:47
So there's so many layers of avoiding dangers,

02:40:46 --> 02:40:50
of small dangers and big dangers all around you

02:40:49 --> 02:40:51
with every single step.

02:40:50 --> 02:40:54
So there's like a mental exhaustion that sets in,

02:40:53 --> 02:40:55
like the, just the perception.

02:40:54 --> 02:40:57
And you're just observing you,

02:40:56 --> 02:41:01
you're extremely good at perceiving,

02:41:00 --> 02:41:03
having situational awareness,

02:41:02 --> 02:41:05
of taking the information in that's really important

02:41:04 --> 02:41:07
and filtering out the stuff that's not important.

02:41:06 --> 02:41:09
But even for you, that's exhausting.

02:41:08 --> 02:41:10
And for me it was completely exhausting,

02:41:09 --> 02:41:12
just paying attention,

02:41:11 --> 02:41:14
paying attention to everything around you.

02:41:13 --> 02:41:16
So that exhaustion was surprising

02:41:15 --> 02:41:18
'cause it's like there's moments when you're like,

02:41:17 --> 02:41:20
I don't give a damn anymore.

02:41:19 --> 02:41:22
I'm just gonna step, I'm just going to, like.

02:41:21 --> 02:41:23
- And so that's it.

02:41:22 --> 02:41:24
You go, "I don't care anymore."

02:41:23 --> 02:41:25
And you reach out and you go,

02:41:24 --> 02:41:27
"I'm just gonna lean against this tree."

02:41:26 --> 02:41:28
And then what happened

02:41:27 --> 02:41:30
every time? - You get spikes in you,

02:41:29 --> 02:41:32
yeah, yeah, yeah. - And then you have to care.

02:41:31 --> 02:41:34
- Yeah and then there's just bad luck

02:41:33 --> 02:41:35
because there is wasp nests.

02:41:34 --> 02:41:37
- [Paul] Yeah. - This is just like

02:41:36 --> 02:41:38
a million things.

02:41:37 --> 02:41:39
And that is physically, is mentally,

02:41:38 --> 02:41:42
psychologically exhausting 'cause there's the uncertainty.

02:41:41 --> 02:41:43
When is this gonna end?

02:41:42 --> 02:41:46
It's up in our particular situation,

02:41:45 --> 02:41:48
up and down hills, up and down hills.

02:41:47 --> 02:41:51
Very steep downward, very steep upward, no water,

02:41:50 --> 02:41:52
all this kind of stuff.

02:41:51 --> 02:41:56
And it's the most difficult thing I've ever done

02:41:55 --> 02:41:58
but it's very difficult to describe

02:41:57 --> 02:42:00
what are the parameters that make it difficult

02:41:59 --> 02:42:02
because I run long distances very regular.

02:42:01 --> 02:42:06
I do extremely difficult physical things regularly

02:42:05 --> 02:42:09
that on some surface level could seem

02:42:08 --> 02:42:11
uch more challenging than what we did.

02:42:10 --> 02:42:14
But no, this was another beast.

02:42:13 --> 02:42:15
This is something else.

02:42:14 --> 02:42:18
But it was also raw and real and beautiful

02:42:17 --> 02:42:22
'cause it's like, it's what the explorers did.

02:42:21 --> 02:42:23
- [Paul] Yeah. - It's what Earth is

02:42:22 --> 02:42:25
without humans.

02:42:25 --> 02:42:29
And it, and also just like the massive scale of the trees

02:42:28 --> 02:42:30
around us

02:42:31 --> 02:42:33
was

02:42:33 --> 02:42:39
the humbling size difference between human and tree.

02:42:38 --> 02:42:42
It's both humbling in that like that tree is really old.

02:42:42 --> 02:42:47
It's the time difference, lifetime difference.

02:42:46 --> 02:42:49
And just the scale.

02:42:48 --> 02:42:51
It's like, holy shit.

02:42:51 --> 02:42:55
We live on an Earth that can create those things.

02:42:54 --> 02:42:59
It makes me feel small in every way, that life is short,

02:42:59 --> 02:43:03
that my physical presence on this Earth is tiny.

02:43:02 --> 02:43:05
How vulnerable I am.

02:43:04 --> 02:43:07
All of those feelings were there.

02:43:06 --> 02:43:10
And in that, the physical endurance

02:43:09 --> 02:43:12
of traversing the jungle,

02:43:12 --> 02:43:18
yeah, was the hardest journey that I remember ever taking.

02:43:20 --> 02:43:22
Every step.

02:43:22 --> 02:43:26
And then that made making it out of the jungle

02:43:26 --> 02:43:29
and then made it,

02:43:31 --> 02:43:35
the swim in the water that we could drink,

02:43:36 --> 02:43:38
that was just pure joy.

02:43:40 --> 02:43:45
It was probably one of the happiest moments in my life,

02:43:45 --> 02:43:51
just sitting there with you, Paul, and with JJ in the water,

02:43:52 --> 02:43:56
full darkness, the rain coming down

02:43:55 --> 02:44:00
and all just, us all just laughing,

02:43:59 --> 02:44:02
having made it through that,

02:44:03 --> 02:44:06
having eaten a bit of food before

02:44:05 --> 02:44:08
and the absurdity of the timing of all of it,

02:44:07 --> 02:44:10
that it somehow worked out.

02:44:11 --> 02:44:13
And

02:44:14 --> 02:44:18
how we're just three little humans

02:44:19 --> 02:44:22
itting in a river.

02:44:22 --> 02:44:27
Just our heads emerged barely above water

02:44:27 --> 02:44:29
with jungle all around us.

02:44:28 --> 02:44:30
What a life.

02:44:30 --> 02:44:33
- That was a real adventure.

02:44:32 --> 02:44:34
- [Lex] That was a real adventure.

02:44:33 --> 02:44:36
- That was a real one. - Yeah.

02:44:35 --> 02:44:37
I'll never forget that.

02:44:36 --> 02:44:41
So it's a real honor to have shared that.

02:44:42 --> 02:44:45
Of course, we had very different experiences.

02:44:44 --> 02:44:48
When you saw a caiman in that situation,

02:44:47 --> 02:44:50
you're like, I have to go meet that guy,

02:44:49 --> 02:44:51
that's a friend of mine.

02:44:50 --> 02:44:54
- Well, I mean we were in the river in a thunderstorm,

02:44:53 --> 02:44:56
just our necks above, we're all laughing our asses off.

02:44:55 --> 02:44:58
And I mean, we're in the river with the stingrays

02:44:57 --> 02:45:00
and the black caiman and the piranha

02:44:59 --> 02:45:02
nd all the electric eels and everything,

02:45:01 --> 02:45:04
and it's pitch black out.

02:45:03 --> 02:45:05
And then what were we doing?

02:45:04 --> 02:45:07
We were holding our headlamps up?

02:45:06 --> 02:45:08
- [Lex] Yeah. - And there was

02:45:07 --> 02:45:09
those swirling moths. - [Lex] Yeah.

02:45:08 --> 02:45:11
- The infinity moths all making those geometric patterns.

02:45:10 --> 02:45:15
And it's like, we're just three ridiculous primates.

02:45:14 --> 02:45:17
- [Lex] Mm-hmm. - Three friends in a river

02:45:16 --> 02:45:18
just laughing. - [Lex] Yeah.

02:45:17 --> 02:45:20
- Because we were safer in that river

02:45:19 --> 02:45:23
than we had been in there and we were rejoicing.

02:45:22 --> 02:45:27
- [Lex] Yeah, yeah. - That the thunderstorm was

02:45:26 --> 02:45:30
compared to the war zone that we'd been living in,

02:45:29 --> 02:45:31
the thunderstorm was safe.

02:45:30 --> 02:45:33
And it was, it really was a beautiful moment.

02:45:32 --> 02:45:35
- And also the like very different life trajectories

02:45:34 --> 02:45:39
have taken these three humans into this one place.

02:45:38 --> 02:45:40
- [Paul] Yeah.

02:45:39 --> 02:45:42
- It's like, what? - [Paul] Yeah, true.

02:45:41 --> 02:45:45
- It's this universe that would like,

02:45:44 --> 02:45:48
'cause we're kind of like those moths, you know what I mean?

02:45:47 --> 02:45:52
Like, where we come from some weird place on this Earth

02:45:51 --> 02:45:54
and we'd have all kinds of shit happen to us

02:45:53 --> 02:45:56
and we're all pursuing some shit and some light

02:45:55 --> 02:45:59
and we ended up here together enjoying this moment.

02:45:58 --> 02:46:01
- [Paul] Yeah. - That was something else.

02:46:00 --> 02:46:03
It just felt absurd.

02:46:02 --> 02:46:05
And in that absurdity was this like real human joy

02:46:05 --> 02:46:08
and damn, water tasted good.

02:46:07 --> 02:46:10
- Oh, water's good, man.

02:46:09 --> 02:46:11
Water and those little oranges.

02:46:10 --> 02:46:14
- [Lex] Yeah, yeah. - Those things and then

02:46:13 --> 02:46:16
I would just say like, do you feel like,

02:46:15 --> 02:46:19
I feel like running, like no matter how much I run,

02:46:18 --> 02:46:21
I feel like the, like you run,

02:46:20 --> 02:46:22
you do a workout and then you stop.

02:46:21 --> 02:46:26
Like maybe people who do ultras feel this, but like

02:46:25 --> 02:46:28
I felt like the, we would, we woke up,

02:46:27 --> 02:46:30
it was like, you know, wake up at dawn,

02:46:29 --> 02:46:33
6:00 AM let's start walking, you know?

02:46:32 --> 02:46:34
Break camp, go.

02:46:33 --> 02:46:36
And it's like pretty much you just don't stop all day.

02:46:35 --> 02:46:39
- [Lex] Mm-hmm. - And it's level 10 cardio

02:46:38 --> 02:46:40
all day long

02:46:39 --> 02:46:43
and you're sweating buckets and there's no water.

02:46:42 --> 02:46:44
It's like, you would never

02:46:43 --> 02:46:45
put yourself through that voluntarily.

02:46:44 --> 02:46:47
- [Lex] Mm-hmm. - You couldn't, you'd never,

02:46:46 --> 02:46:49
you would never have the resolve to continue

02:46:48 --> 02:46:52
torturing yourself except for that we were trying

02:46:51 --> 02:46:56
to make it to the, to freedom, to get out.

02:46:55 --> 02:46:58
And it's like the obsession of that,

02:46:57 --> 02:47:02
with the compass and the machete and the navigating, fuck.

02:47:01 --> 02:47:04
- I think there's something to be said about like the fact

02:47:03 --> 02:47:05
hat we didn't think through much of that.

02:47:04 --> 02:47:07
- [Paul] No. - And we just dived into it.

02:47:06 --> 02:47:09
- [Paul] Yeah. - I think there was like,

02:47:08 --> 02:47:11
there we're like laughing, enjoying ourselves

02:47:10 --> 02:47:13
moments before and once you go in you're like, oh shit.

02:47:12 --> 02:47:14
- [Paul] Oh shit.

02:47:13 --> 02:47:16
- And you just come face to face with it.

02:47:15 --> 02:47:17
- [Paul] Yeah.

02:47:18 --> 02:47:20
- I think that's what, you know,

02:47:19 --> 02:47:22
whatever that is in humans that goes to that,

02:47:21 --> 02:47:24
that's what the explorers do.

02:47:23 --> 02:47:27
The, you know, and the best of them do it

02:47:26 --> 02:47:29
o the extreme levels.

02:47:28 --> 02:47:30
- Well I think that what we did was

02:47:29 --> 02:47:34
to a pretty extreme level because we left

02:47:33 --> 02:47:35
he safety of a river,

02:47:34 --> 02:47:39
of knowing where we were and voluntarily got lost

02:47:38 --> 02:47:43
in the Amazon with very little provisions on an,

02:47:42 --> 02:47:45
on a very, now that we're back, I'm,

02:47:44 --> 02:47:47
now that we experienced what we experienced,

02:47:46 --> 02:47:49
I really can't stop thinking about how fucking stupid it was

02:47:48 --> 02:47:51
that we did that. - [Lex] Yeah.

02:47:50 --> 02:47:54
- Because if we had gotten lost, Pico was saying to me

02:47:54 --> 02:47:56
ven if you guys had,

02:47:55 --> 02:47:57
if one of you had broken your leg.

02:47:57 --> 02:48:01
- [Lex] Mm-hmm. - It's, you know,

02:48:00 --> 02:48:03
days in either direction.

02:48:02 --> 02:48:06
Even if they had sent help for us,

02:48:05 --> 02:48:09
help would take how long to scour all that jungle?

02:48:08 --> 02:48:11
Sound doesn't travel.

02:48:10 --> 02:48:13
Even a helicopter, even if they looked for us,

02:48:12 --> 02:48:14
they wouldn't be able to see us.

02:48:13 --> 02:48:16
How would we signal for help?

02:48:15 --> 02:48:19
You can't really build a fire and so it's like

02:48:18 --> 02:48:20
if anything had gone wrong,

02:48:19 --> 02:48:23
if we'd gone a few degrees different to the west,

02:48:22 --> 02:48:25
would've taken us two more days.

02:48:24 --> 02:48:28
If we'd gotten injured,

02:48:27 --> 02:48:31
it'd be carry through that. - [Lex] Yeah.

02:48:30 --> 02:48:33
- And so it somehow, only afterwards am I really going

02:48:32 --> 02:48:36
wow, thank God we got out of this.

02:48:35 --> 02:48:36
(Lex laughs)

02:48:35 --> 02:48:38
Thank God after I see so many people going,

02:48:37 --> 02:48:40
"Make sure nothing happens to Lex Fridman."

02:48:39 --> 02:48:41
- [Lex] Yeah. - I'd be the deadest

02:48:40 --> 02:48:43
motherfucker on Earth if anything happened.

02:48:42 --> 02:48:45
(Paul laughs)

02:48:44 --> 02:48:47
- It somehow works out.

02:48:46 --> 02:48:49
- It does seem to somehow work out.

02:48:48 --> 02:48:50
- Lemme ask you about Jane Goodall,

02:48:49 --> 02:48:52
another explorer of a different kind.

02:48:53 --> 02:48:55
What do you think about her,

02:48:54 --> 02:49:00
about her role in understanding this natural world of ours?

02:48:59 --> 02:49:05
- I think that Jane is like a living historical treasure.

02:49:04 --> 02:49:07
Like, I think somehow she's alive,

02:49:06 --> 02:49:11
but she's already reached that level where it's like

02:49:10 --> 02:49:14
Einstein, Jane Goodall, like there's these incredible minds.

02:49:13 --> 02:49:18
And, you know, growing up as a child,

02:49:17 --> 02:49:20
my parents would read to me because I was so dyslexic

02:49:19 --> 02:49:22
I didn't learn to read until I was quite old.

02:49:21 --> 02:49:26
And my mom was a big Jane Goodall fan.

02:49:25 --> 02:49:27
And all I wanted to hear about was animals

02:49:26 --> 02:49:31
and so I would get read to about this lady named

02:49:30 --> 02:49:33
Jane Goodall, this girl who went to Africa

02:49:32 --> 02:49:35
nd studied chimps and who broke all the rules

02:49:34 --> 02:49:38
and named her study subjects

02:49:37 --> 02:49:40
even though that wasn't what she was supposed to do.

02:49:39 --> 02:49:44
And she became this incredible advocate for Earth

02:49:43 --> 02:49:46
and for ecosystems and for,

02:49:45 --> 02:49:49
and she seemed to realize as her career went on

02:49:48 --> 02:49:53
that teaching children to appreciate nature was the key.

02:49:54 --> 02:49:56
'Cause they're going, you know,

02:49:55 --> 02:49:57
that thing where she says

02:49:56 --> 02:50:01
we don't so much inherit the Earth from our ancestors

02:50:00 --> 02:50:03
but borrow it from our children.

02:50:02 --> 02:50:04
We're just here, we're just passing through.

02:50:03 --> 02:50:06
And so if we destroy it,

02:50:05 --> 02:50:11
we're dimming the lights on the lives of future generations.

02:50:10 --> 02:50:13
And so she's been really, really cognizant of that.

02:50:12 --> 02:50:15
And she's been a light in the darkness.

02:50:14 --> 02:50:18
She's sort of in terms of saying

02:50:17 --> 02:50:21
that animals have personalities and culture

02:50:20 --> 02:50:25
and their own, inalienable rights and reasons for existing

02:50:24 --> 02:50:27
and that human life is valuable.

02:50:26 --> 02:50:28
She's very big on that.

02:50:27 --> 02:50:30
Every day we influence the people around us

02:50:29 --> 02:50:33
and the events of the Earth.

02:50:32 --> 02:50:35
Even if you feel like your life is small and insignificant,

02:50:34 --> 02:50:38
that you do have an impact.

02:50:37 --> 02:50:41
And I think that's a really powerful little candle out there

02:50:40 --> 02:50:43
in the darkness that Jane carries.

02:50:44 --> 02:50:48
- What do you think about her field work with the chimps?

02:50:48 --> 02:50:51
- Badass, the fact that she did what she did

02:50:50 --> 02:50:52
at the age that she did.

02:50:51 --> 02:50:54
- [Lex] Yeah. - At the time that she did.

02:50:53 --> 02:50:57
- [Lex] Yeah. - Is incredible.

02:50:56 --> 02:50:58
It's actually incredible.

02:50:57 --> 02:51:00
She has that explorer gene and she also has

02:50:59 --> 02:51:03
that relentless relentlessness,

02:51:02 --> 02:51:05
is like this incredible quality.

02:51:04 --> 02:51:08
She just, you know, she travels 300 days a year

02:51:07 --> 02:51:10
educating people, talking around the world,

02:51:09 --> 02:51:13
trying to help bolster conservation now

02:51:12 --> 02:51:15
before it's too late. - [Lex] Mm-hmm.

02:51:14 --> 02:51:18
- And traveling 300 days a year is not fun.

02:51:17 --> 02:51:21
Traveling at all can be not fun.

02:51:20 --> 02:51:23
- So I started reading the "River of Doubt" book

02:51:22 --> 02:51:25
you recommended to me on Teddy Roosevelt.

02:51:24 --> 02:51:27
- [Paul] Yeah. - So that guy's

02:51:26 --> 02:51:29
badass on many levels,

02:51:28 --> 02:51:32
but I didn't realize how much of a naturalist he was,

02:51:31 --> 02:51:35
how much of a scholar of the natural world he was.

02:51:34 --> 02:51:40
So that book details his journey into the Amazon jungle.

02:51:41 --> 02:51:44
What do you find inspiring about Teddy Roosevelt

02:51:43 --> 02:51:48
and that whole journey of just saying fuck it,

02:51:47 --> 02:51:51
of going to the Amazon jungle, of taking on that expedition?

02:51:50 --> 02:51:53
- Well, I mean Teddy Roosevelt,

02:51:52 --> 02:51:55
you could write volumes on what's inspiring about him.

02:51:54 --> 02:51:57
I think that, you know, he was

02:51:57 --> 02:51:59
a weak, asthmatic little rich kid

02:51:58 --> 02:52:03
that wasn't physically able, that had no self-confidence.

02:52:02 --> 02:52:08
And he was very, and he had pretty severe depression.

02:52:07 --> 02:52:09
He had tragedy in his life.

02:52:08 --> 02:52:11
And he was very,

02:52:11 --> 02:52:13
at least for me,

02:52:12 --> 02:52:15
he's been one of the people, like in the,

02:52:14 --> 02:52:17
one of the first historical figures who,

02:52:17 --> 02:52:22
where he wrote about the struggle to overcome those things

02:52:21 --> 02:52:26
and to make himself from being

02:52:25 --> 02:52:28
a weak, asthmatic little teenager

02:52:27 --> 02:52:31
to sort of strengthening himself and building muscle

02:52:30 --> 02:52:34
and becoming this barrel-chested lion of a guy

02:52:33 --> 02:52:36
who could be the president, who could be an explorer

02:52:35 --> 02:52:39
and one of the rough riders.

02:52:38 --> 02:52:43
And he's just, everything he does is so

02:52:42 --> 02:52:46
hyperbolically, you know, incredible.

02:52:45 --> 02:52:48
To come out of war and have the other people you fought with

02:52:47 --> 02:52:51
go, "He, this guy has no fear."

02:52:50 --> 02:52:52
- [Lex] Yeah. - I mean, he must have

02:52:51 --> 02:52:54
just been a psychopath and had no fear.

02:52:53 --> 02:52:57
And then proving it further was that thing

02:52:56 --> 02:52:59
where he was gonna give a speech to a bunch of people

02:52:58 --> 02:53:01
and he got shot in the chest.

02:53:00 --> 02:53:02
- [Lex] Yeah, and kept speaking.

02:53:01 --> 02:53:05
- It went through his spectacle case and through his speech,

02:53:04 --> 02:53:09
and even though the bullet was lodged in his chest,

02:53:08 --> 02:53:12
this man said, "Don't hurt the guy that shot me."

02:53:11 --> 02:53:15
I believe he asked him, "Why'd you do it?"

02:53:14 --> 02:53:17
And then as he's bleeding and in the rain said,

02:53:16 --> 02:53:19
"No, no, no, I'm not going the hospital,

02:53:18 --> 02:53:22
I'm gonna keep going with the speech."

02:53:21 --> 02:53:24
What a badass, that's incredible.

02:53:23 --> 02:53:27
- But going to the jungle on many levels is

02:53:26 --> 02:53:31
really difficult for him at that time.

02:53:30 --> 02:53:33
There's so many things that could,

02:53:32 --> 02:53:36
so many more things even than now that can kill you.

02:53:35 --> 02:53:37
All the different infections, everything.

02:53:36 --> 02:53:38
- [Paul] Yeah. - And the lack of knowledge,

02:53:37 --> 02:53:40
just the sheer lack of knowledge.

02:53:39 --> 02:53:44
So that truly is an expedition,

02:53:44 --> 02:53:47
a really, really challenging expedition.

02:53:46 --> 02:53:49
So there's lessons about what it takes

02:53:48 --> 02:53:53
to be a great explorer from that, the perseverance.

02:53:52 --> 02:53:56
How important do you think is perseverance in exploration,

02:53:55 --> 02:53:57
especially through the jungle?

02:53:56 --> 02:53:58
- I think it's all there is.

02:53:57 --> 02:54:00
If you hear about the people,

02:53:59 --> 02:54:03
and I think that is a tremendous metaphor for life

02:54:02 --> 02:54:06
because whether you hear about that plane that crashed

02:54:05 --> 02:54:08
in the Andes and the people were alone and freezing

02:54:07 --> 02:54:10
and they had to eat each other,

02:54:09 --> 02:54:13
and some of them made it out.

02:54:12 --> 02:54:15
Some of them kept the fire burning.

02:54:14 --> 02:54:19
And Teddy Roosevelt voluntarily, after being president,

02:54:18 --> 02:54:24
threw himself into the Amazon rainforest and survived.

02:54:23 --> 02:54:28
Came so close to dying, but survived.

02:54:27 --> 02:54:30
And so perseverance is all of it.

02:54:29 --> 02:54:34
I mean, that's, I think that's our quality as a human.

02:54:33 --> 02:54:36
- So they also mapped, so on the biology side,

02:54:35 --> 02:54:38
it's interesting. - [Paul Yeah.

02:54:37 --> 02:54:39
- But they mapped and documented

02:54:38 --> 02:54:41
a lot of the unknown geography and biodiversity.

02:54:40 --> 02:54:43
What does it take to do that?

02:54:42 --> 02:54:44
So when I see you move about the jungle,

02:54:43 --> 02:54:47
you're always like, you're capturing a creature,

02:54:46 --> 02:54:49
take a picture, write down like so you can

02:54:49 --> 02:54:53
find new creatures, find new things about the jungle,

02:54:52 --> 02:54:54
document them,

02:54:53 --> 02:54:57
sort of a scientific perspective on the jungle.

02:54:56 --> 02:55:00
But back then there was even less known,

02:54:59 --> 02:55:02
much less known about the jungle.

02:55:01 --> 02:55:04
So what do you think it takes to document,

02:55:03 --> 02:55:08
to map that world, a new, unexplored wilderness?

02:55:07 --> 02:55:13
- I mean they're clearly pressing botanical specimens.

02:55:13 --> 02:55:15
They're probably shooting birds.

02:55:14 --> 02:55:16
And Roosevelt

02:55:15 --> 02:55:20
knew how to preserve those specimens.

02:55:19 --> 02:55:23
I mean, he really was a naturalist, so he knew exactly.

02:55:22 --> 02:55:24
So if he's seeing these animals,

02:55:23 --> 02:55:27
to them, whereas we'll take a picture and identify it,

02:55:26 --> 02:55:28
they were harvesting specimens,

02:55:27 --> 02:55:30
taking them with them, drying them out.

02:55:31 --> 02:55:33
For them, it was totally different.

02:55:32 --> 02:55:35
And it could be the first, you know?

02:55:34 --> 02:55:37
There's, I don't know, I forget what JJ said.

02:55:36 --> 02:55:40
There's something like 70 species of ant birds here.

02:55:39 --> 02:55:43
And it's like, so how likely are you to be the first person

02:55:42 --> 02:55:45
to ever see this one species of bird?

02:55:44 --> 02:55:47
And so for them.

02:55:46 --> 02:55:48
(Paul mimics gunshot)

02:55:47 --> 02:55:49
And it's, you have this bird,

02:55:48 --> 02:55:53
and so perfectly preserving that specimen.

02:55:52 --> 02:55:55
And I think a lot of non-scientific people don't realize

02:55:54 --> 02:55:58
that every species from blue whale to elephant

02:55:57 --> 02:56:00
o blue jay to sparrow,

02:55:59 --> 02:56:01
whatever, whatever it is,

02:56:00 --> 02:56:03
whatever species we have on record,

02:56:02 --> 02:56:04
there are scientific specimens

02:56:03 --> 02:56:06
and the first people to see them shot them.

02:56:05 --> 02:56:08
- [Lex] Mm-hmm. - And that's,

02:56:07 --> 02:56:12
the museums are filled with these catalogs,

02:56:11 --> 02:56:14
preserved birds that these explorers brought back

02:56:13 --> 02:56:17
from New Guinea and South America and Africa

02:56:16 --> 02:56:19
nd then put into these drawers.

02:56:18 --> 02:56:23
And now we labeled them and we said,

02:56:22 --> 02:56:24
this is, you know. - [Lex] Yeah.

02:56:23 --> 02:56:25
- This is red and green macaw,

02:56:24 --> 02:56:29
this is scarlet macaw, this is brown-crested ant bird.

02:56:28 --> 02:56:32
And this is, and it's just, they're just categorized.

02:56:31 --> 02:56:33
- That book of birds you have,

02:56:32 --> 02:56:37
like, encyclopedia of birds. - Yo,

02:56:36 --> 02:56:40
the human achievement in these pages.

02:56:39 --> 02:56:41
- For people listening,

02:56:40 --> 02:56:45
Paul's just flipping through a huge number of pages.

02:56:44 --> 02:56:48
These are just, is this in the Amazon or just in Peru?

02:56:47 --> 02:56:51
- This is just here, this is birds of Peru.

02:56:50 --> 02:56:52
(Lex laughs)

02:56:51 --> 02:56:56
- Dude, pages on pages of toucans and aracaris

02:56:55 --> 02:56:58
and hummingbirds and ant birds

02:56:57 --> 02:57:03
and smokey brown woodpecker and tropical screech owl,

02:57:02 --> 02:57:04
which we just heard, by the way.

02:57:03 --> 02:57:06
It was just, it's endless.

02:57:05 --> 02:57:07
Who knew there were so many birds?

02:57:06 --> 02:57:08
I had no idea there was so many birds.

02:57:07 --> 02:57:10
- Documenting all of that.

02:57:10 --> 02:57:14
I mean, there's also, which we got to experience

02:57:13 --> 02:57:16
and you, you're pretty good at also is

02:57:15 --> 02:57:18
actually making,

02:57:17 --> 02:57:20
understanding and making the sounds of the different birds.

02:57:19 --> 02:57:21
- [Paul] Yeah. - What's your favorite

02:57:20 --> 02:57:22
bird song to make?

02:57:22 --> 02:57:27
- Undulated tinamou because in the crepuscular hours

02:57:26 --> 02:57:28
of dawn and dusk,

02:57:27 --> 02:57:30
they're usually the ones that make up

02:57:29 --> 02:57:34
what is considered by many to be the anthem of the Amazon.

02:57:34 --> 02:57:38
- Can you do a little bird for us?

02:57:37 --> 02:57:41
(Paul whistles)

02:57:40 --> 02:57:43
- That's what a undulated tinamou sounds like.

02:57:42 --> 02:57:45
And it's usually like, oh,

02:57:44 --> 02:57:46
it is getting to be afternoon.

02:57:45 --> 02:57:48
And it's kind of, it's almost like hearing

02:57:47 --> 02:57:49
church bells on a Sunday.

02:57:48 --> 02:57:51
It's like you just, there's something about it.

02:57:50 --> 02:57:53
You go ah, there he is.

02:57:53 --> 02:57:56
- And like you were saying, it's a reminder.

02:57:55 --> 02:57:57
Oh, that's a friend of mine.

02:57:56 --> 02:57:58
- [Paul] Yeah. - Surrounded by friends.

02:57:57 --> 02:58:00
- I have so many friends here.

02:58:00 --> 02:58:03
- What does take to survive out here?

02:58:03 --> 02:58:08
What are some basic principles of survival in the jungle?

02:58:07 --> 02:58:09
- Cleanliness.

02:58:10 --> 02:58:13
I mean really, but we talked about this,

02:58:12 --> 02:58:15
but like, you know, keeping,

02:58:14 --> 02:58:18
I have so many holes in my skin right now.

02:58:17 --> 02:58:19
- [Lex] Yeah. - Look, I have a mosquito.

02:58:18 --> 02:58:20
There we go.

02:58:20 --> 02:58:24
I have so many spots that I've scratched off of my skin

02:58:23 --> 02:58:27
because a mosquito bites me and then I scratch it.

02:58:26 --> 02:58:31
Or the other big one is that I worry that I have a tick.

02:58:30 --> 02:58:35
Not deliberately, not with my thinking brain,

02:58:34 --> 02:58:39
but my simian brain just wants to find and remove ticks

02:58:38 --> 02:58:41
and so I scratch.

02:58:40 --> 02:58:42
And then if my fingernails get too long,

02:58:41 --> 02:58:45
I remove my skin and then those beget,

02:58:44 --> 02:58:46
those get infected in the jungle.

02:58:45 --> 02:58:51
So staying hyper clean, using soap,

02:58:50 --> 02:58:52
like basic stuff.

02:58:51 --> 02:58:56
Keeping order to your bags, order to your gear.

02:58:55 --> 02:58:57
Things in dry bags.

02:58:56 --> 02:58:59
Make sure, you know, we did,

02:58:58 --> 02:59:03
we explained that we got in the river during a thunderstorm.

02:59:02 --> 02:59:05
We didn't explain why we did that

02:59:04 --> 02:59:07
because the thunderstorm came when we had eaten dinner,

02:59:06 --> 02:59:09
but we hadn't set up our tents.

02:59:08 --> 02:59:12
And so we decided to cover our bags with our boats

02:59:11 --> 02:59:13
that we had been carrying,

02:59:12 --> 02:59:16
our pack wraps that we'd been carrying in our backpacks.

02:59:15 --> 02:59:18
so all of our gear would stay dry.

02:59:17 --> 02:59:19
- [Lex] Mm-hmm. - So the only thing

02:59:18 --> 02:59:21
we could do is either sit in the rain and be cold

02:59:20 --> 02:59:24
or sit in the river and be warm.

02:59:23 --> 02:59:26
And so keeping our gear dry,

02:59:25 --> 02:59:30
momentary discomfort for future, you know,

02:59:29 --> 02:59:34
that was, that to me was an incredibly smart calculation

02:59:33 --> 02:59:35
to make is you really just,

02:59:34 --> 02:59:37
you gotta be smart out here.

02:59:36 --> 02:59:40
You can't, you know, not running out of a headlamp

02:59:39 --> 02:59:41
while you're out on the trail

02:59:40 --> 02:59:43
and being stuck in that darkness.

02:59:42 --> 02:59:45
- [Lex] Yeah. - It really takes

02:59:44 --> 02:59:47
just being a little bit on your toes.

02:59:46 --> 02:59:51
And I find that necessity of being on your toes

02:59:50 --> 02:59:52
is a place that I like to live in.

02:59:51 --> 02:59:54
It's just the right amount of challenge here.

02:59:53 --> 02:59:56
- So keeping the gear organized and all of that,

02:59:55 --> 02:59:59
but also being willing to sort of improvise.

02:59:58 --> 03:00:00
I've seen you improvise very well

02:59:59 --> 03:00:03
'cause there's so much unknowns, there's so many,

03:00:02 --> 03:00:05
so much chaos and dynamic aspects

03:00:04 --> 03:00:08
that like planning is not going to prevent you

03:00:07 --> 03:00:12
from having to face that at the end of the day.

03:00:11 --> 03:00:15
- No, it's been really funny watching you

03:00:14 --> 03:00:19
sort of shed your planning brain.

03:00:18 --> 03:00:20
(Lex laughs)

03:00:19 --> 03:00:21
Like day one. - [Lex] Yeah.

03:00:20 --> 03:00:23
- It was very much like, so are we gonna,

03:00:22 --> 03:00:25
and then I could tell, I could see your,

03:00:24 --> 03:00:27
I could see your brow sort of furrow when you,

03:00:26 --> 03:00:29
I would go, "I don't know what time we're gonna get there."

03:00:28 --> 03:00:30
And you'd go, "Well, just tell me."

03:00:29 --> 03:00:31
And I'd be like, "I don't know

03:00:30 --> 03:00:33
hat the jungle's gonna let us do."

03:00:32 --> 03:00:33
- [Lex] Yeah. - You know?

03:00:32 --> 03:00:36
Let's do, let's record the podcast tomorrow.

03:00:35 --> 03:00:38
Okay, but we, if it, you know, if it rains,

03:00:37 --> 03:00:40
if it gets windy, if a (indistinct) comes,

03:00:39 --> 03:00:42
if there's a jaguar with rabies,

03:00:41 --> 03:00:44
like anything could happen.

03:00:45 --> 03:00:48
Landslides, like anything, literally.

03:00:47 --> 03:00:50
- This tree, I mean the thing you mentioned,

03:00:49 --> 03:00:53
trees falling, that's a thing in the jungle.

03:00:52 --> 03:00:54
- That's a major thing in the jungle.

03:00:53 --> 03:00:55
- Holy shit.

03:00:54 --> 03:00:56
First of all, a lot of trees fall.

03:00:55 --> 03:00:58
- [Paul] Yeah. - And they fall quickly

03:00:57 --> 03:00:59
and they could just kill you.

03:00:58 --> 03:01:01
- They fall quickly, they're huge.

03:01:00 --> 03:01:03
We're talking about trees

03:01:02 --> 03:01:06
that are like the size of school buses stacked.

03:01:05 --> 03:01:09
- [Lex] Yeah. - And connected to other trees

03:01:08 --> 03:01:12
with vines so that when they fall,

03:01:11 --> 03:01:15
this millennium tree, this 1,000 year old tree, boom,

03:01:14 --> 03:01:18
it shakes the ground, pulls down other trees with it.

03:01:17 --> 03:01:22
So if you're anywhere near that for a few acres,

03:01:21 --> 03:01:23
you're getting smashed.

03:01:22 --> 03:01:24
That's the end of you.

03:01:23 --> 03:01:26
And so the jungle, at any moment that you're out there

03:01:25 --> 03:01:28
could just decide to delete you.

03:01:27 --> 03:01:30
And then the leafcutter ants and the army ants

03:01:29 --> 03:01:31
and the flies and everything,

03:01:30 --> 03:01:32
you'll be digested in three days.

03:01:31 --> 03:01:34
You'll be gone, gone, no bones, nothing.

03:01:33 --> 03:01:36
- Who do you think would eat most of you?

03:01:37 --> 03:01:42
- I would hope that like a king vulture with a colorful face

03:01:41 --> 03:01:43
would just like get in there.

03:01:42 --> 03:01:44
- [Lex] Dramatically go in there,

03:01:43 --> 03:01:45
just eat the heart. - Like right in the ass.

03:01:44 --> 03:01:46
Just like nature's metal,

03:01:45 --> 03:01:48
just like when they like walk in through the elephant's ass.

03:01:47 --> 03:01:50
I'd want that on camera trap.

03:01:49 --> 03:01:52
I think that would be a great way to go.

03:01:51 --> 03:01:54
- And would slowly look up and just kind of smile on camera.

03:01:53 --> 03:01:56
- Yeah, just rip out your intestines and just shake it.

03:01:55 --> 03:01:58
- [Lex] Yeah. - Just victorious

03:01:57 --> 03:01:59
over your dead body.

03:01:58 --> 03:02:01
- Well, but also honor a friend.

03:02:00 --> 03:02:02
That's another way to honor. - Yeah, sure.

03:02:01 --> 03:02:04
But you know, you just, you look so, you know,

03:02:03 --> 03:02:06
your white, naked ass laying there in the jungle,

03:02:05 --> 03:02:07
you'd be like face down and shit.

03:02:06 --> 03:02:09
(Paul laughs)

03:02:08 --> 03:02:11
- That's why you always have to look good.

03:02:10 --> 03:02:12
Any moment, a tree could fall on you

03:02:11 --> 03:02:14
and a vulture just swoops in and eats your heart.

03:02:13 --> 03:02:15
- [Paul] That's right.

03:02:16 --> 03:02:19
- We talked about "Alone," this show a bit.

03:02:18 --> 03:02:22
- Yo, Rock House. - Yeah, who is,

03:02:21 --> 03:02:23
what do you think about that guy?

03:02:22 --> 03:02:25
Rock House, Roland Welker from season seven.

03:02:24 --> 03:02:26
He built a rock house.

03:02:25 --> 03:02:30
He killed a muskox with bow and arrow

03:02:30 --> 03:02:33
and then finished it with a knife.

03:02:32 --> 03:02:35
(Paul growls)

03:02:34 --> 03:02:37
- And had the GoPro mount to, you know?

03:02:36 --> 03:02:39
- [Lex] Yeah. - To document it.

03:02:38 --> 03:02:41
That's a really mind-blowing.

03:02:40 --> 03:02:43
I mean, so for people who don't know that show

03:02:42 --> 03:02:45
is you're supposed to survive as long as possible.

03:02:44 --> 03:02:47
On season seven of the show,

03:02:46 --> 03:02:49
they literally said you can only win it

03:02:48 --> 03:02:50
if

03:02:50 --> 03:02:53
you survive 100 days.

03:02:53 --> 03:02:58
And that's, there's a lot of aspects of that show

03:02:57 --> 03:02:59
that's difficult,

03:02:58 --> 03:03:00
one of which is it's in the cold.

03:03:00 --> 03:03:03
The other is they get just a handful of supplies,

03:03:02 --> 03:03:05
no food, nothing, none of that,

03:03:04 --> 03:03:08
so they have to figure all of that out.

03:03:07 --> 03:03:12
And this is probably one of the greatest performers

03:03:11 --> 03:03:14
on the show, Roland Welker.

03:03:13 --> 03:03:16
He built a rock house shelter.

03:03:15 --> 03:03:18
So what, I mean, what does survival entail?

03:03:17 --> 03:03:19
It's building a shelter,

03:03:20 --> 03:03:24
fire, catching food, staying warm,

03:03:23 --> 03:03:27
getting enough energy to sort of keep doing the work.

03:03:26 --> 03:03:28
It takes a lot of work.

03:03:27 --> 03:03:29
Like building the rock house,

03:03:28 --> 03:03:33
I read that it took 500 calories an hour from him.

03:03:33 --> 03:03:36
- [Paul] Whoa. - So he had to feed himself,

03:03:35 --> 03:03:37
right, quite a lot.

03:03:36 --> 03:03:40
You're lifting 200 pound boulders

03:03:41 --> 03:03:46
and still the guy lost I read 44 pounds,

03:03:45 --> 03:03:48
which is 20% of his body weight.

03:03:47 --> 03:03:50
So that's survival.

03:03:49 --> 03:03:52
What lessons,

03:03:51 --> 03:03:54
what inspiration do you draw from him?

03:03:55 --> 03:03:57
- I think he was fun to watch

03:03:56 --> 03:04:01
because he had this indomitable spirit.

03:04:00 --> 03:04:05
He was just, he wasn't there to commune with nature.

03:04:04 --> 03:04:06
He was there to win.

03:04:05 --> 03:04:09
And he was like, to me that's the pioneer mentality.

03:04:08 --> 03:04:12
He just, he was just, he goes, "I'm a hunting guide.

03:04:11 --> 03:04:14
I'm out here, I'm gonna win that money.

03:04:13 --> 03:04:15
I'm gonna survive through the winter."

03:04:14 --> 03:04:16
He wasn't worried.

03:04:15 --> 03:04:18
I feel like so many people are like,

03:04:17 --> 03:04:20
they worry, second guessing themselves.

03:04:19 --> 03:04:21
Am I in a video game?

03:04:20 --> 03:04:22
I don't know.

03:04:21 --> 03:04:23
What's my, you know,

03:04:22 --> 03:04:25
just questioning their entire existential identity.

03:04:24 --> 03:04:27
And this guy was like, you know what?

03:04:26 --> 03:04:28
There's a muskox over there.

03:04:27 --> 03:04:30
- [Lex] Mm-hmm. - I'm gonna shoot it.

03:04:29 --> 03:04:32
I'm gonna stab it and then I'm gonna make a pouch

03:04:31 --> 03:04:34
out of its ballsack and I'm gonna live off that

03:04:33 --> 03:04:36
for the next few months and win a half a million dollars.

03:04:35 --> 03:04:40
And that's an amazing amount of pragmatic optimism

03:04:39 --> 03:04:41
that I just enjoyed. - [Lex] Yeah.

03:04:40 --> 03:04:42
- And every time he would go,

03:04:41 --> 03:04:44
"We gotta get back to Rock House."

03:04:43 --> 03:04:45
And it became. - [Lex] Yeah.

03:04:44 --> 03:04:47
- Even though he is all alone,

03:04:46 --> 03:04:49
it was, he had a big smile on his face.

03:04:48 --> 03:04:52
And what made that season so great was that it was him

03:04:51 --> 03:04:53
and then it was Callie.

03:04:52 --> 03:04:57
And Roland had, you know, the muscle

03:04:56 --> 03:04:58
and could make Rock House.

03:04:57 --> 03:05:01
And then Callie was the opposite.

03:05:00 --> 03:05:04
She was this girl who, yeah, she could hunt with her bow

03:05:03 --> 03:05:08
and she knew how to fish and she wasn't using raw power.

03:05:07 --> 03:05:10
But what was so endearing about her was

03:05:09 --> 03:05:12
that how much she loved being out there,

03:05:11 --> 03:05:17
as hard as it was and as isolationist as it was,

03:05:16 --> 03:05:18
she was smiling every time,

03:05:17 --> 03:05:21
every time the show cut to her, she was like,

03:05:20 --> 03:05:24
"Hey everybody, it's morning.

03:05:23 --> 03:05:26
Can you believe the frost?"

03:05:25 --> 03:05:29
Like, you've been out there for 100 days.

03:05:28 --> 03:05:32
Amazing, I think it was really an amazing show

03:05:31 --> 03:05:34
of that the game is all here.

03:05:33 --> 03:05:35
The game of life.

03:05:34 --> 03:05:37
The game of alone and the game of life

03:05:36 --> 03:05:38
because it's the same thing.

03:05:37 --> 03:05:40
- Yeah, she maintained that sort of silliness,

03:05:39 --> 03:05:42
the goofiness all through it.

03:05:41 --> 03:05:43
- [Paul] Yeah. - When the conditions got

03:05:42 --> 03:05:46
really tough and she had a very different perspective.

03:05:45 --> 03:05:50
As you know, Roland didn't want any of the spirituality.

03:05:49 --> 03:05:52
He was very pragmatic. - [Paul] Yeah.

03:05:51 --> 03:05:53
- And from Callie's,

03:05:52 --> 03:05:56
very spiritual connection to the land.

03:05:55 --> 03:05:59
She said something like she wanted

03:05:58 --> 03:06:02
not only to take from the land, but to give back.

03:06:01 --> 03:06:03
- [Paul] Yeah. - I mean there's this kinda.

03:06:02 --> 03:06:05
- [Paul] Yeah. - Poetic, spiritual connection

03:06:04 --> 03:06:09
to the land as such a dire contrast to Roland.

03:06:08 --> 03:06:10
And, but she's still a badass.

03:06:09 --> 03:06:12
I mean, to survive, no matter what,

03:06:11 --> 03:06:14
no matter the kind of personality you have,

03:06:13 --> 03:06:16
you have to be a badass.

03:06:15 --> 03:06:19
I think she took a

03:06:18 --> 03:06:22
porcupine quill from her shoulder.

03:06:21 --> 03:06:24
- That was crazy 'cause it,

03:06:23 --> 03:06:25
I think it went in. - [Lex] Yeah.

03:06:24 --> 03:06:27
- Somewhere completely different

03:06:26 --> 03:06:28
and it migrated to her shoulder.

03:06:27 --> 03:06:30
- [Lex] Yeah. - And the way I understood

03:06:29 --> 03:06:32
that is because they have, I said that's impossible.

03:06:31 --> 03:06:33
- [Lex] Yeah. - Because I remember that,

03:06:32 --> 03:06:35
she's like pulling up her shirt and she's like,

03:06:34 --> 03:06:36
there's something.

03:06:35 --> 03:06:37
And then she like pushes it out.

03:06:36 --> 03:06:38
- [Lex] Yeah. - And I remember like,

03:06:37 --> 03:06:40
I was like, hold up, hold up, hold up, hold up.

03:06:39 --> 03:06:42
How? - [Lex] Yeah.

03:06:41 --> 03:06:44
- And it was because the barbs, once it goes in,

03:06:43 --> 03:06:47
as you move and flex your body,

03:06:46 --> 03:06:51
it moves a little bit each time and it gets, migrate, like.

03:06:50 --> 03:06:51
- [Lex] Yeah. - I didn't even think

03:06:50 --> 03:06:53
of that shit. - Plus, if I remember

03:06:52 --> 03:06:56
correctly, I think she caught two porcupines.

03:06:55 --> 03:06:59
The second one was like rotting or something, or infected,

03:06:58 --> 03:07:01
it had an infected body, whatever.

03:07:00 --> 03:07:03
- Had the spots on it, yeah. - [Lex] Yeah.

03:07:02 --> 03:07:04
- She chose not to eat it.

03:07:03 --> 03:07:06
- No and then she chose not to eat it at first

03:07:05 --> 03:07:08
and then she decided to eat it eventually.

03:07:07 --> 03:07:09
- [Paul] Yeah, I forgot that.

03:07:08 --> 03:07:12
- Yeah, and she, that was an insane

03:07:12 --> 03:07:15
sort of really thoughtful,

03:07:15 --> 03:07:18
focused, collected decision,

03:07:17 --> 03:07:20
waiting a day and then saying, "Fuck it,

03:07:19 --> 03:07:21
I need this fat." - [Paul] I need this.

03:07:21 --> 03:07:25
- And that was the other thing is like, fat is important.

03:07:24 --> 03:07:26
- [Paul] Oh yeah.

03:07:25 --> 03:07:28
- It's like meat is not enough.

03:07:27 --> 03:07:29
You learn about like what are

03:07:28 --> 03:07:32
the different food sources there?

03:07:31 --> 03:07:36
Apparently there's like rabbit starvation is a thing

03:07:35 --> 03:07:38
because when you have too much lean meat,

03:07:37 --> 03:07:40
it doesn't nourish the body.

03:07:39 --> 03:07:42
Fat is the thing that nourishes the body,

03:07:41 --> 03:07:45
especially in cold conditions.

03:07:45 --> 03:07:50
So that's a thing. - Yeah, she was incredible

03:07:49 --> 03:07:55
and I thought as brash and sort of fun as Roland was,

03:07:55 --> 03:08:01
she represented a much more beautiful take on it.

03:08:00 --> 03:08:03
And it was really heartbreaking when she lost

03:08:02 --> 03:08:06
'cause I mean, and like you said, still a badass.

03:08:05 --> 03:08:07
- [Lex] Yeah. - It's kind of like

03:08:06 --> 03:08:09
Forrest Griffin versus Stephan Bonner.

03:08:08 --> 03:08:10
- [Lex] Yeah. - Like, it was like

03:08:09 --> 03:08:11
it doesn't matter who won.

03:08:10 --> 03:08:13
- [Lex] Yeah. - You guys beat the shit

03:08:12 --> 03:08:14
out of each other, like.

03:08:13 --> 03:08:16
- [Lex] And she didn't really lose, right?

03:08:15 --> 03:08:17
- No. - She got evaced.

03:08:17 --> 03:08:22
- [Paul] Yeah. - Because her toe was going.

03:08:21 --> 03:08:24
- Frostbite. - Frostbite, 100 days.

03:08:23 --> 03:08:26
You think you can do 100 days?

03:08:26 --> 03:08:30
- Honestly, I've done a, I've.

03:08:29 --> 03:08:31
(Lex laughs)

03:08:30 --> 03:08:33
I'm 18 years in the Amazon, man.

03:08:32 --> 03:08:36
- [Lex] Yeah. - I just, at this point it's,

03:08:36 --> 03:08:38
I could. - [Lex] Yeah.

03:08:37 --> 03:08:42
- I wouldn't sign up for another 100 days, you know?

03:08:41 --> 03:08:43
- [Lex] Yeah. - At this point,

03:08:42 --> 03:08:44
I don't have that to prove.

03:08:43 --> 03:08:46
I've survived in the wild and

03:08:46 --> 03:08:50
I wouldn't want to voluntarily take 100 days

03:08:49 --> 03:08:52
away from everyone I know.

03:08:51 --> 03:08:55
- Yeah, the loneliness aspect is tough.

03:08:54 --> 03:08:56
- We're not meant for that.

03:08:55 --> 03:08:58
I really love the people I have in my life

03:08:57 --> 03:09:00
and I wouldn't,

03:08:59 --> 03:09:01
and you see it on the show.

03:09:00 --> 03:09:02
A lot of the people. - [Lex] Yeah.

03:09:01 --> 03:09:04
- Big, tough ex-Navy SEALs

03:09:03 --> 03:09:06
who are survival experts who know what they're doing.

03:09:05 --> 03:09:07
They get out there and they go,

03:09:06 --> 03:09:09
"You know what?

03:09:08 --> 03:09:11
I miss my family." - [Lex] Yeah.

03:09:10 --> 03:09:12
- And they go, "It's not worth it."

03:09:11 --> 03:09:14
They have this existential realization.

03:09:13 --> 03:09:15
They go, "We only got,

03:09:14 --> 03:09:17
I only got so many years here.

03:09:16 --> 03:09:19
Like let's, this is crazy.

03:09:18 --> 03:09:20
It's just some money, fuck it."

03:09:19 --> 03:09:21
And they go home.

03:09:21 --> 03:09:22
- You know what's funny?

03:09:21 --> 03:09:25
'Cause you sometimes feeling yourself in the jungle

03:09:24 --> 03:09:25
when you're alone.

03:09:24 --> 03:09:30
And there's another guy, Jordan Jonas,

03:09:29 --> 03:09:35
Hobo Jordo, he's the season six winner.

03:09:34 --> 03:09:38
And he said that the camera made him feel less lonely.

03:09:37 --> 03:09:41
I've heard of him from multiple channels.

03:09:40 --> 03:09:46
One of the things is he spent all of his 20s in,

03:09:47 --> 03:09:51
living in Siberia with the tribes out there.

03:09:50 --> 03:09:53
- [Paul] Whoa.

03:09:52 --> 03:09:55
- Herzog, "Happy People".

03:09:54 --> 03:09:58
- [Paul] Mm. - And so he actually talked

03:09:57 --> 03:10:03
about that it's one of the loneliest time of his life

03:10:02 --> 03:10:04
because when he went up there,

03:10:03 --> 03:10:07
he didn't speak Russian and he needed to learn the language.

03:10:06 --> 03:10:09
And even though you have people around you,

03:10:08 --> 03:10:10
when you don't speak their language,

03:10:09 --> 03:10:12
it feels really, really lonely.

03:10:11 --> 03:10:14
And he felt less lonely on the show

03:10:13 --> 03:10:15
'cause he had the camera

03:10:14 --> 03:10:17
nd he felt like he could talk to the camera.

03:10:16 --> 03:10:19
- [Paul] Interesting. - There is an element

03:10:18 --> 03:10:21
when you have in these harsh conditions,

03:10:20 --> 03:10:23
if you like record something,

03:10:22 --> 03:10:27
you feel like you're talking to another human through it

03:10:26 --> 03:10:28
even if it's just a recording.

03:10:27 --> 03:10:31
I sometimes feel that like,

03:10:30 --> 03:10:33
maybe 'cause I imagine a specific person

03:10:32 --> 03:10:34
that will watch it

03:10:33 --> 03:10:36
and I feel like I'm talking to that person.

03:10:36 --> 03:10:41
- Well, I noticed that when things got especially hard,

03:10:40 --> 03:10:43
and they did get especially hard

03:10:42 --> 03:10:45
when we were out in the wilderness,

03:10:45 --> 03:10:51
that you would begin filming

03:10:50 --> 03:10:54
to share that struggle.

03:10:53 --> 03:10:57
But I also think that I've used that at times

03:10:56 --> 03:11:00
where yeah, you go, well maybe if I,

03:10:59 --> 03:11:02
'cause if you can tell someone else about it,

03:11:01 --> 03:11:04
then you're on the hero's journey.

03:11:03 --> 03:11:07
And then it sort of has to make you braver

03:11:06 --> 03:11:08
and it changes how you, 'cause you go,

03:11:07 --> 03:11:11
I'm cold and I'm tired and I'm hungry

03:11:10 --> 03:11:12
and this hurts and that hurts

03:11:11 --> 03:11:14
and I don't know when we're gonna make it

03:11:13 --> 03:11:15
and how is this gonna go?

03:11:14 --> 03:11:16
And all of a sudden you go,

03:11:15 --> 03:11:19
well guys, we're here and we're going that way.

03:11:18 --> 03:11:20
(Lex laughs)

03:11:19 --> 03:11:23
And then you're like, well I gotta keep going.

03:11:22 --> 03:11:24
- [Lex] Yeah. - 'Cause you're like,

03:11:23 --> 03:11:25
they're still out there if you forget.

03:11:24 --> 03:11:26
- You have to step up.

03:11:25 --> 03:11:28
That's one of the reasons I want a family.

03:11:27 --> 03:11:30
I think when you have kids. - [Paul] Yeah.

03:11:29 --> 03:11:31
- You have to be like,

03:11:30 --> 03:11:34
you have to be the best version of yourself like for them.

03:11:33 --> 03:11:37
- All my friends with kids that I've seen them go through,

03:11:36 --> 03:11:39
where until you have a family,

03:11:38 --> 03:11:42
you're just playing around, man.

03:11:41 --> 03:11:44
I mean, you could do important work.

03:11:43 --> 03:11:47
You can have skin in the, in other games,

03:11:46 --> 03:11:48
but it's, once you have

03:11:47 --> 03:11:51
a little tribe of humans that depends on you.

03:11:50 --> 03:11:53
- [Lex] Yeah. - If you take that seriously,

03:11:52 --> 03:11:55
if you want to do that right,

03:11:54 --> 03:11:58
it's one of the hardest things you could do.

03:11:57 --> 03:12:01
And it just, it just changes everything.

03:12:02 --> 03:12:06
- How has your life changed since we last met?

03:12:05 --> 03:12:08
- Speak about changing everything.

03:12:08 --> 03:12:12
- So you've been, for people who don't know,

03:12:11 --> 03:12:17
pushing Junglekeepers forward into uncharted territories,

03:12:16 --> 03:12:20
saving more and more and more and more rainforests.

03:12:19 --> 03:12:22
There's a lot I could ask you about that.

03:12:21 --> 03:12:24
There's a lot of stories to be told there.

03:12:23 --> 03:12:26
It's a fight, it's a battle,

03:12:25 --> 03:12:28
it's a battle to protect this

03:12:27 --> 03:12:33
beautiful area of rainforest, of nature.

03:12:33 --> 03:12:36
But since we last met, you've made,

03:12:35 --> 03:12:39
you've continued to make a lot of progress.

03:12:38 --> 03:12:43
So what's the story of Junglekeepers?

03:12:42 --> 03:12:45
Leading up to the moment we met and after

03:12:44 --> 03:12:47
and everything you're going, doing right now?

03:12:46 --> 03:12:49
- 18 years ago when I first came to the jungle,

03:12:49 --> 03:12:55
I was a kid from New York who always dreamed

03:12:55 --> 03:12:59
since I was six years old, maybe even younger,

03:12:58 --> 03:13:02
of going to a place where animals were everywhere

03:13:01 --> 03:13:04
and there's big trees and skyscrapers of life.

03:13:03 --> 03:13:08
And so being dyslexic and not fitting in in school

03:13:07 --> 03:13:11
and reading about Jane Goodall and having

03:13:10 --> 03:13:13
"Lord of the Rings" be one of the things I grew up on,

03:13:12 --> 03:13:15
I just chose to come to the Amazon.

03:13:14 --> 03:13:17
And the first person I met was

03:13:16 --> 03:13:20
this local, Indigenous conservationist named

03:13:19 --> 03:13:22
Juan Julio Durand,

03:13:21 --> 03:13:25
who was trying to protect this remote river,

03:13:24 --> 03:13:27
the Las Piedras River,

03:13:26 --> 03:13:30
which in history, apparently Fawcett referenced

03:13:29 --> 03:13:34
either the Las Piedras, but he called it Tahuamanu

03:13:33 --> 03:13:35
and said, "Don't go there."

03:13:34 --> 03:13:36
(Lex laughs)

03:13:35 --> 03:13:37
"You'll surely die from tribes."

03:13:36 --> 03:13:39
- [Lex] Yeah. - And so there's very few

03:13:38 --> 03:13:42
references to this river in history.

03:13:41 --> 03:13:44
It's stayed very wild because it's been a place

03:13:43 --> 03:13:46
that the law hasn't made it,

03:13:45 --> 03:13:48
the government hasn't really extended to,

03:13:47 --> 03:13:50
like, you know, we're sort of past the police limit.

03:13:49 --> 03:13:53
And so JJ was out here ages ago

03:13:52 --> 03:13:56
trying to protect this river before it was too late.

03:13:55 --> 03:13:57
And when I met him,

03:13:56 --> 03:14:00
I was just a barely out of high school kid

03:13:59 --> 03:14:02
with a dream of just seeing the rainforest,

03:14:01 --> 03:14:06
let alone seeing a giant anaconda

03:14:05 --> 03:14:08
or having any sort of meaningful experience

03:14:07 --> 03:14:10
or contribution to the narrative.

03:14:09 --> 03:14:14
And somehow over all the years that we began

03:14:13 --> 03:14:16
working together and sparked a friendship

03:14:15 --> 03:14:19
and began exploring and going on expeditions

03:14:18 --> 03:14:22
and bringing people to the rainforest

03:14:21 --> 03:14:24
and asking them for help

03:14:23 --> 03:14:27
and manifesting the hell out of this insane dream

03:14:26 --> 03:14:28
that we had.

03:14:27 --> 03:14:29
I mean, we didn't even have a boat.

03:14:28 --> 03:14:31
We would take logs down the river.

03:14:30 --> 03:14:33
We would have to cut a tree down every

03:14:32 --> 03:14:35
time we wanted to return to civilization.

03:14:34 --> 03:14:37
We'd have to cut down a balsa tree and float down the river.

03:14:36 --> 03:14:39
- [Lex] Float down the river on it, yeah.

03:14:38 --> 03:14:40
- It was, it's madness.

03:14:39 --> 03:14:42
Like, it's madness, it's pure madness.

03:14:41 --> 03:14:45
And I don't know what made us keep going,

03:14:44 --> 03:14:47
but along the way, people showed up who cared

03:14:46 --> 03:14:49
and who wanted to help.

03:14:48 --> 03:14:52
And if it was a movie, it wouldn't even necessarily be

03:14:51 --> 03:14:54
a good movie 'cause you'd go, oh, please,

03:14:53 --> 03:14:56
you're just telling me that you just kept doing the thing

03:14:55 --> 03:14:58
and just magically people showed up.

03:14:57 --> 03:15:00
But yeah, that's what happened.

03:14:59 --> 03:15:01
That's exactly the way it went.

03:15:00 --> 03:15:04
We kept doing the thing that we loved.

03:15:03 --> 03:15:06
We said, "It doesn't matter if we don't have funding

03:15:05 --> 03:15:10
or a boat or gasoline or friends or anything."

03:15:09 --> 03:15:12
We just kept going.

03:15:11 --> 03:15:15
And along the way we found someone who could help us

03:15:14 --> 03:15:17
tart a ranger program.

03:15:16 --> 03:15:19
And then we found Dax Dasilva,

03:15:18 --> 03:15:23
who helped us fund the beginning of Junglekeepers.

03:15:22 --> 03:15:26
And then people like Mohsin and Stefan who were there

03:15:25 --> 03:15:28
making sure that this thing actually took flight

03:15:27 --> 03:15:29
off the ground.

03:15:28 --> 03:15:32
And then right around the time that we were wondering

03:15:31 --> 03:15:33
what was gonna happen

03:15:32 --> 03:15:35
and if we're all gonna have to quit and get real jobs

03:15:34 --> 03:15:38
and if we could actually save the rainforest

03:15:37 --> 03:15:39
from the destruction that was coming,

03:15:40 --> 03:15:44
Lex Fridman sends me a DM

03:15:43 --> 03:15:47
and honestly changed the entire narrative.

03:15:46 --> 03:15:49
Because up until then we had been,

03:15:49 --> 03:15:52
we'd been playing in the minor leagues,

03:15:51 --> 03:15:55
pretending, trying real, real hard.

03:15:54 --> 03:15:59
And the listeners of your show

03:15:58 --> 03:16:02
in the moments after you published your episode

03:16:01 --> 03:16:06
with our conversation began showing up in droves

03:16:05 --> 03:16:08
and supporting Junglekeepers,

03:16:07 --> 03:16:10
putting in five, 10, 100, 1,000.

03:16:09 --> 03:16:12
We started getting these donations

03:16:11 --> 03:16:14
and the incredible team that I work with,

03:16:13 --> 03:16:16
we all went into hyperdrive.

03:16:15 --> 03:16:18
Everybody, everybody started going nuts.

03:16:17 --> 03:16:21
We all started spending 16 hour days working

03:16:20 --> 03:16:24
to try and deal with the tidal wave

03:16:23 --> 03:16:26
that Lex sent towards us.

03:16:25 --> 03:16:29
Just 'cause so many people knew that we were doing this,

03:16:28 --> 03:16:31
that was an Indigenous-led fight to protect

03:16:30 --> 03:16:35
his incredibly ancient, virgin rainforest

03:16:34 --> 03:16:36
before it was cut.

03:16:35 --> 03:16:37
And people resonated with that.

03:16:36 --> 03:16:42
And so we got this huge swell of support.

03:16:41 --> 03:16:43
And this year, we've protected

03:16:42 --> 03:16:46
thousands and thousands of more acres of rainforest

03:16:45 --> 03:16:48
because of that swell of support.

03:16:47 --> 03:16:51
- So current 50,000 acres, what's the goal?

03:16:50 --> 03:16:54
What's the approach to saving this rainforest?

03:16:53 --> 03:16:59
- Since we printed this, it's gone up to 66,000 acres.

03:16:58 --> 03:16:59
It's,

03:17:00 --> 03:17:05
and as you know, in each of those little acres are

03:17:04 --> 03:17:07
millions and millions of animal heartbeats

03:17:06 --> 03:17:08
and societies of animals.

03:17:07 --> 03:17:13
And the goal here is that we're between Manu National Park,

03:17:14 --> 03:17:16
Alto Purus National Park,

03:17:15 --> 03:17:17
the Tambopata Reserve.

03:17:16 --> 03:17:18
We're in a region that's known as

03:17:17 --> 03:17:21
the biodiversity capital of Peru,

03:17:20 --> 03:17:25
one of the most biodiverse parts of the Western Amazon.

03:17:24 --> 03:17:27
And we're fighting along the edge

03:17:26 --> 03:17:30
of the Trans-Amazon Highway.

03:17:29 --> 03:17:33
And so it's just a small group of local people

03:17:32 --> 03:17:35
and some international experts who have come together

03:17:34 --> 03:17:40
and used these incredibly out of side of the box strategies

03:17:39 --> 03:17:43
to sort of crowdfund conservation,

03:17:42 --> 03:17:46
to go, look, we know that this incredible life is here.

03:17:45 --> 03:17:47
We have the scientific evidence,

03:17:46 --> 03:17:49
we have the national park system.

03:17:48 --> 03:17:53
If we can protect this before they cut it down,

03:17:52 --> 03:17:55
we could do something of global significance.

03:17:54 --> 03:17:57
All these jaguars, all these monkeys,

03:17:56 --> 03:17:59
all these undescribed medicines,

03:17:58 --> 03:18:02
the uncontacted tribes that we share this forest with,

03:18:01 --> 03:18:04
could all be protected.

03:18:03 --> 03:18:07
And people have stepped up and begun to make that happen.

03:18:06 --> 03:18:08
And there's people from all over the world

03:18:07 --> 03:18:09
and it's incredible.

03:18:10 --> 03:18:12
- But what's the approach?

03:18:11 --> 03:18:16
So trying to, with donations, to buy out

03:18:15 --> 03:18:19
more and more of the land and then protect it.

03:18:18 --> 03:18:20
- So the approach is

03:18:19 --> 03:18:22
that currently the government favors extractors.

03:18:21 --> 03:18:27
So if you're a gold miner or an illegal logger,

03:18:26 --> 03:18:30
or you just want to cut down and burn a bunch of rainforests

03:18:29 --> 03:18:34
and set up a cacao farm, the government's fine with that.

03:18:33 --> 03:18:35
It doesn't matter.

03:18:34 --> 03:18:37
You're not really breaking the law if you destroy nature.

03:18:36 --> 03:18:40
- So as long as you're producing something from the land,

03:18:39 --> 03:18:42
they don't see it as a loss

03:18:41 --> 03:18:44
the nature was destroyed permanently.

03:18:43 --> 03:18:45
- Yeah, it's just wilderness.

03:18:44 --> 03:18:47
It's sort of just beyond the scope of,

03:18:46 --> 03:18:48
it's not, doesn't,

03:18:47 --> 03:18:52
or the local people that technically own the land out here,

03:18:51 --> 03:18:53
the local Indigenous people,

03:18:52 --> 03:18:55
for instance, we fought this year to help

03:18:54 --> 03:18:57
the community of Puerto Nuevo,

03:18:56 --> 03:18:59
who's been fighting for 20 years

03:18:58 --> 03:19:01
to have government recognized land.

03:19:00 --> 03:19:03
These are Indigenous people in the Amazon

03:19:03 --> 03:19:06
fighting to protect their own land.

03:19:05 --> 03:19:08
And you know what it was that was holding 'em back?

03:19:07 --> 03:19:10
They didn't understand

03:19:10 --> 03:19:15
how the system of legal documents worked

03:19:14 --> 03:19:16
to certify that titled land.

03:19:15 --> 03:19:18
They didn't really have the funding to go

03:19:17 --> 03:19:21
from their very, very remote community into the offices.

03:19:20 --> 03:19:23
And so Junglekeepers helped them with that.

03:19:22 --> 03:19:25
And so really all we're doing is

03:19:24 --> 03:19:28
helping local people protect the forest

03:19:27 --> 03:19:29
hat is their world.

03:19:29 --> 03:19:33
That's it. - If people donate,

03:19:32 --> 03:19:35
how will that help?

03:19:35 --> 03:19:38
- If people donate to Junglekeepers,

03:19:37 --> 03:19:39
what you're doing is you're helping

03:19:40 --> 03:19:42
someone like JJ,

03:19:41 --> 03:19:46
who's an Indigenous naturalist who has the vision,

03:19:45 --> 03:19:48
who has seen forest be destroyed,

03:19:47 --> 03:19:49
he's trying to protect it before it's too late.

03:19:48 --> 03:19:53
You're saving mahogany trees, ironwood trees,

03:19:52 --> 03:19:56
kapok trees, skyscrapers of life,

03:19:56 --> 03:19:59
just monkeys, birds, reptiles,

03:19:58 --> 03:20:00
amphibians, birds, mammals,

03:19:59 --> 03:20:03
this entire Avatar on Earth world of rainforest

03:20:02 --> 03:20:07
hat produces a fifth of the oxygen we breathe

03:20:06 --> 03:20:09
and the water we drink,

03:20:08 --> 03:20:10
this incredible thing.

03:20:09 --> 03:20:12
As far as I know, it's the most direct way to protect that.

03:20:11 --> 03:20:13
And so the fact that,

03:20:12 --> 03:20:15
the fact that we've, you know,

03:20:14 --> 03:20:18
we have large funders who give us, you know,

03:20:17 --> 03:20:20
$100,000 to protect this huge swath of land.

03:20:19 --> 03:20:23
And that goes through things like this

03:20:22 --> 03:20:24
and through Instagram, it, you know,

03:20:23 --> 03:20:27
it goes directly to the local conservationists

03:20:26 --> 03:20:30
who work with the loggers to protect that land

03:20:29 --> 03:20:32
before it's cut.

03:20:31 --> 03:20:34
But one of the most impactful things that has happened

03:20:33 --> 03:20:38
this year in the wake of our last conversation was

03:20:37 --> 03:20:41
that I got an email from a mother and she said,

03:20:40 --> 03:20:43
"You know, I'm a single mom and I work a few jobs

03:20:42 --> 03:20:45
and I can't afford to give you a ton of money,

03:20:44 --> 03:20:50
but me and my kids look at your Instagram often after dinner

03:20:49 --> 03:20:53
and they really want to protect the heartbeats.

03:20:52 --> 03:20:55
They really wanna protect the animals and the rainforest.

03:20:54 --> 03:20:58
And so we do, we give $5 a month to Junglekeepers."

03:20:57 --> 03:21:01
And it was, to me that was so impactful

03:21:00 --> 03:21:03
because I used to be that little kid,

03:21:02 --> 03:21:04
worried about the animals.

03:21:03 --> 03:21:08
And I saw how a few million raindrops can create a flood.

03:21:07 --> 03:21:11
- Yeah, I ask

03:21:10 --> 03:21:14
that people donate to Junglekeepers.

03:21:13 --> 03:21:16
You guys are legit.

03:21:16 --> 03:21:19
That money's going to go a long way.

03:21:18 --> 03:21:21
Junglekeepers.org.

03:21:20 --> 03:21:26
If you somehow were able to raise very large,

03:21:25 --> 03:21:29
so the raindrops would make a waterfall,

03:21:28 --> 03:21:31
a very large amount of money.

03:21:30 --> 03:21:33
I don't know what that number is.

03:21:32 --> 03:21:35
Maybe $10 million, 20 million,

03:21:36 --> 03:21:41
30 million, what are the different milestones along the way

03:21:40 --> 03:21:43
that could really help,

03:21:43 --> 03:21:49
help you on the journey of saving the rainforest?

03:21:48 --> 03:21:53
- If we did, if let's just say some company organization

03:21:52 --> 03:21:54
or if enough people donated it,

03:21:53 --> 03:21:56
let's just say we got that 30 million,

03:21:55 --> 03:22:00
that money would go directly into stopping logging roads,

03:21:59 --> 03:22:03
into creating a corridor,

03:22:02 --> 03:22:05
a biological corridor that connects

03:22:04 --> 03:22:09
the uncontacted Indigenous reserves with other tribal lands,

03:22:08 --> 03:22:11
with Manu National Park, with the Tambopata,

03:22:10 --> 03:22:15
which establishes essentially the largest protected area

03:22:14 --> 03:22:17
in the Amazon rainforest.

03:22:16 --> 03:22:18
And what makes this groundbreaking is

03:22:17 --> 03:22:20
that we're not doing this in the traditional way.

03:22:19 --> 03:22:22
We're doing this, take it to the people.

03:22:22 --> 03:22:25
And that's what's been so exciting is that, you know,

03:22:24 --> 03:22:26
when he started this,

03:22:25 --> 03:22:28
when JJ started this 30 years ago,

03:22:27 --> 03:22:29
he had no idea.

03:22:28 --> 03:22:31
His father wanted him to be a logger.

03:22:30 --> 03:22:33
He didn't have shoes until he was 13 years old.

03:22:32 --> 03:22:36
He grew up bathing in the river.

03:22:35 --> 03:22:41
He had no idea that a bunch of crazy foreigner scientists

03:22:40 --> 03:22:41
were gonna show up

03:22:40 --> 03:22:45
and some guy in a James Bond suit was

03:22:44 --> 03:22:46
gonna come down here with microphones

03:22:45 --> 03:22:49
and that all of a sudden the world would know

03:22:48 --> 03:22:51
that he was on this quest to protect

03:22:50 --> 03:22:54
his incredible ecosystem and all those little aliens.

03:22:53 --> 03:22:56
- Well, that's all the important thing to remember,

03:22:55 --> 03:22:58
that the people that are cutting down the forest,

03:22:57 --> 03:23:01
the loggers are also human beings that have families.

03:23:00 --> 03:23:03
- [Paul] Yeah. - They're basically trying

03:23:02 --> 03:23:05
to survive and they're desperate

03:23:04 --> 03:23:07
and they're doing the thing that will bring them money.

03:23:06 --> 03:23:08
And so they're just human beings.

03:23:07 --> 03:23:12
At the core of it, if they have other options,

03:23:11 --> 03:23:17
they will probably choose to give their life

03:23:16 --> 03:23:19
to saving the community, to

03:23:20 --> 03:23:24
first and foremost providing for their family,

03:23:24 --> 03:23:28
and after that, saving the community,

03:23:27 --> 03:23:30
helping the community flourish.

03:23:29 --> 03:23:34
And I think probably a lot of them love the rainforest.

03:23:33 --> 03:23:35
They grew up in the rainforest.

03:23:34 --> 03:23:37
- Yeah, I mean, look at Pico.

03:23:36 --> 03:23:38
- [Lex] Yeah.

03:23:37 --> 03:23:38
- Pico used to be a logger,

03:23:37 --> 03:23:40
full-time logger, long-time logger.

03:23:40 --> 03:23:43
Now he loves conservation.

03:23:42 --> 03:23:45
He goes (speaks foreign language)

03:23:44 --> 03:23:46
(Lex laughs)

03:23:45 --> 03:23:48
He's like, you know? - Yeah.

03:23:47 --> 03:23:51
It's all about just providing people options.

03:23:50 --> 03:23:53
There's some dark stuff on the

03:23:52 --> 03:23:56
gold mine stuff you've talked about.

03:23:55 --> 03:23:57
You showed me parts of the rainforest

03:23:56 --> 03:23:59
where the gold mines are

03:23:58 --> 03:24:03
and they're just kind of erasing the rainforest.

03:24:02 --> 03:24:05
- [Paul] Yeah. - Sort of at the edges.

03:24:04 --> 03:24:07
That's when the mining happens

03:24:06 --> 03:24:09
and it's this ugly,

03:24:10 --> 03:24:14
ugly process of they're just destroying the jungle

03:24:13 --> 03:24:17
just for the surface layer of the sand

03:24:16 --> 03:24:20
or whatever that they process to collect

03:24:19 --> 03:24:23
just little bits of gold.

03:24:22 --> 03:24:27
And there's also very dark things that happen

03:24:26 --> 03:24:30
along the way as the communities around the gold mines

03:24:29 --> 03:24:32
are created so that the entirety of the moral system

03:24:31 --> 03:24:36
that emerges from that has things like prostitution,

03:24:35 --> 03:24:40
where one third of the women that are

03:24:40 --> 03:24:45
drawn into that sex trafficking, prostitution are

03:24:44 --> 03:24:48
minors under, you know, under 17 years old,

03:24:47 --> 03:24:50
13 to 17 year old.

03:24:49 --> 03:24:53
There's just a lot of really, really dark stuff.

03:24:52 --> 03:24:54
- I think that we have

03:24:56 --> 03:25:02
a rare chance to do something against that darkness.

03:25:02 --> 03:25:07
I think that this is an example of local people who have

03:25:07 --> 03:25:11
taken action, done good work,

03:25:10 --> 03:25:13
been good to the people that have visited,

03:25:13 --> 03:25:18
harnessed a certain amount of international momentum.

03:25:17 --> 03:25:22
And now we're on the cusp of doing something historic.

03:25:21 --> 03:25:27
And so for the children in the communities along this river,

03:25:28 --> 03:25:33
it won't be being a prostitute in a gold mine.

03:25:32 --> 03:25:36
It'll be becoming a trained ranger.

03:25:35 --> 03:25:39
Like last month, our ranger coordinator

03:25:38 --> 03:25:42
and one of our female rangers went to Africa

03:25:42 --> 03:25:44
for a ranger conference.

03:25:43 --> 03:25:45
And it's like we're beginning to,

03:25:44 --> 03:25:48
this is someone from a little, tiny village

03:25:47 --> 03:25:49
with thatched huts up river.

03:25:48 --> 03:25:51
She went to Africa to talk about being

03:25:50 --> 03:25:53
a professional conservation ranger.

03:25:52 --> 03:25:56
And it's like that's changing lives.

03:25:55 --> 03:25:58
And her daughters then,

03:25:57 --> 03:26:00
she's married to Ignacio, the guy.

03:25:59 --> 03:26:01
- [Lex] Mm-hmm. - She, like her,

03:26:00 --> 03:26:04
their kids are gonna grow up seeing their parents

03:26:03 --> 03:26:06
walking around with the emblem on and go,

03:26:05 --> 03:26:10
"Oh, I wanna", and then people like Pico and Pedro

03:26:09 --> 03:26:11
and all these guys that work here are gonna go,

03:26:10 --> 03:26:14
"Well, we have to protect this forest."

03:26:13 --> 03:26:16
And then they start getting fascinated about the snakes

03:26:15 --> 03:26:19
and then they start caring about the turtle eggs

03:26:18 --> 03:26:21
and then all of a sudden they have a way of life.

03:26:20 --> 03:26:22
And nobody needs to go be, nobody can,

03:26:21 --> 03:26:24
nobody needs to go steal anybody's kids

03:26:23 --> 03:26:26
to be a prostitute in a gold mine.

03:26:25 --> 03:26:27
That's horrible.

03:26:26 --> 03:26:31
And so it's really a, it's a win-win for the animals,

03:26:30 --> 03:26:33
for the river, for the rainforest, for people.

03:26:32 --> 03:26:35
We're improving, it's biocentric conservation.

03:26:34 --> 03:26:37
It's just making everything better.

03:26:36 --> 03:26:41
- Yeah, I've read in an article that said

03:26:40 --> 03:26:46
an estimated 1,200 girls between ages of 12 and 17 are

03:26:45 --> 03:26:48
forcibly drafted into child prostitution

03:26:48 --> 03:26:51
around the communities in the gold mines.

03:26:50 --> 03:26:53
At least one third of the prostitutes

03:26:52 --> 03:26:54
in the camp are underage.

03:26:53 --> 03:26:57
- Oh. - The girls had ended up

03:26:56 --> 03:26:58
in the camp after receiving a tip

03:26:57 --> 03:27:01
that there were restaurants looking for waitresses

03:27:00 --> 03:27:03
and willing to pay top dollar.

03:27:02 --> 03:27:04
They jumped on a bus together

03:27:03 --> 03:27:06
and came down to the rainforest.

03:27:05 --> 03:27:09
What they found was not what they were expecting.

03:27:08 --> 03:27:11
The mining camp restaurants served food

03:27:10 --> 03:27:13
for only a few hours a day.

03:27:12 --> 03:27:16
The rest of the time it was the girls themselves

03:27:15 --> 03:27:17
who were on the menu.

03:27:16 --> 03:27:19
Literally at the end of the road

03:27:18 --> 03:27:21
and without the money to return home,

03:27:20 --> 03:27:23
the girls would soon become trapped in prostitution.

03:27:24 --> 03:27:28
- It's interesting to me that the most

03:27:29 --> 03:27:33
devastating destruction of nature,

03:27:32 --> 03:27:37
the complete erasure of the rainforest,

03:27:36 --> 03:27:39
burned to the ground,

03:27:38 --> 03:27:40
sucked through a hose,

03:27:39 --> 03:27:44
spit out into a disgusting mercury puddle,

03:27:43 --> 03:27:47
like the complete annihilation of life on Earth

03:27:46 --> 03:27:50
goes hand in hand with the complete annihilation

03:27:49 --> 03:27:51
of a young life.

03:27:51 --> 03:27:55
It's like, it's all based around the same thing.

03:27:54 --> 03:27:56
It's the light versus the dark.

03:27:55 --> 03:27:59
That's, it's the destruction and the chaos

03:27:58 --> 03:28:02
versus a move towards order and hope

03:28:02 --> 03:28:04
and

03:28:04 --> 03:28:06
it is incredibly dark

03:28:05 --> 03:28:09
and this region is heavy with it.

03:28:10 --> 03:28:14
- Well, I'm glad you're fighting for the light.

03:28:15 --> 03:28:19
Is there like a milestone in the near future

03:28:18 --> 03:28:20
that you're working towards,

03:28:19 --> 03:28:22
like financially in terms of donations?

03:28:22 --> 03:28:24
- There is.

03:28:23 --> 03:28:28
In the next year and a half, as you saw in your time here,

03:28:27 --> 03:28:32
there's roads working around

03:28:31 --> 03:28:34
the Junglekeepers' concessions.

03:28:33 --> 03:28:35
All the work that the local people are doing

03:28:34 --> 03:28:38
to protect this land is trying to be dismantled

03:28:37 --> 03:28:40
by international corporations

03:28:39 --> 03:28:44
that are subcontracting logging companies here.

03:28:43 --> 03:28:49
And really what we need is $30 million in the next two years

03:28:48 --> 03:28:51
to protect the whole thing.

03:28:50 --> 03:28:54
You've seen the ancient mahogany trees,

03:28:53 --> 03:28:55

03:28:54 --> 03:28:57

that was crawling by us,

like literally by our shoulders,

as we're talking, a bat flies through,

That's one of my favorite snakes.

Look at that.

- [Lex] And it's going on some long journey that's gonna.
