03:35:18 --> 03:35:24
So that little snake is one of the millions

03:35:23 --> 03:35:28
of life forms, heartbeats that you're trying to protect.

03:35:27 --> 03:35:29
- Exactly.

03:35:30 --> 03:35:33
To me, I,

03:35:33 --> 03:35:36
after almost 20 years down here,

03:35:35 --> 03:35:38
the people here have become my friends,

03:35:37 --> 03:35:41
the caiman on the river, the monkeys.

03:35:40 --> 03:35:42
I, when I fall asleep at night,

03:35:41 --> 03:35:44
I think about all the different heartbeats,

03:35:43 --> 03:35:47
all the different little creatures here

03:35:46 --> 03:35:50
that when they bulldoze this forest,

03:35:49 --> 03:35:53
when they chop down these trees, that they vanish,

03:35:52 --> 03:35:55
that we take away their world.

03:35:54 --> 03:35:59
And in that very evolutionary, historical sense

03:35:58 --> 03:36:03
of remembering the primordial soup,

03:36:02 --> 03:36:05
it's like this, these, this little creature

03:36:04 --> 03:36:07
surviving out here somehow.

03:36:06 --> 03:36:09
And we have the chance to save it.

03:36:08 --> 03:36:11
And even if you don't care about the little creature

03:36:10 --> 03:36:12
on the pale blue dot,

03:36:12 --> 03:36:15
each of these little creatures contributes

03:36:14 --> 03:36:18
to this massive orchestral hole that creates

03:36:18 --> 03:36:21
climactic stability on this planet.

03:36:20 --> 03:36:24
And the Amazon is one of the most important parts of that.

03:36:23 --> 03:36:27
And each of these little guys is playing a role in there.

03:36:26 --> 03:36:30
- So one of the other fascinating life forms is

03:36:29 --> 03:36:33
other humans, but living a very different kind of life.

03:36:32 --> 03:36:36
So uncontacted tribes,

03:36:35 --> 03:36:38
what do you find most fascinating about them?

03:36:38 --> 03:36:42
- What I find most fascinating about the uncontacted tribes

03:36:41 --> 03:36:44
is that while me and you are sitting here

03:36:43 --> 03:36:46
with microphones and a light,

03:36:45 --> 03:36:49
somewhere out there in that darkness,

03:36:48 --> 03:36:53
in that direction, not so far away as the crow flies,

03:36:53 --> 03:36:58
there are people sitting around a fire in the dark,

03:36:57 --> 03:37:01
probably with little more than a few leaves over their heads

03:37:01 --> 03:37:07
who don't even have the use of stone tools,

03:37:06 --> 03:37:11
who only have metal objects that they've stolen

03:37:10 --> 03:37:15
from nearby communities.

03:37:14 --> 03:37:20
They're living such primitive, isolated nomadic lives

03:37:19 --> 03:37:22
in the modern world,

03:37:21 --> 03:37:24
and they're still living naked out in the jungle.

03:37:24 --> 03:37:27
It's truly incredible, it's truly remarkable.

03:37:26 --> 03:37:31
And I think that it's

03:37:30 --> 03:37:33
because they can't advocate for themselves,

03:37:32 --> 03:37:36
they can't protect themselves, it's sort of like, well,

03:37:35 --> 03:37:39
we can let them get shot up by loggers

03:37:38 --> 03:37:42
and get their, let their land get bulldozed while they hide.

03:37:41 --> 03:37:45
They have no idea that their world is being destroyed.

03:37:45 --> 03:37:48
But they're sort of the scariest

03:37:47 --> 03:37:51
and most fascinating thing out there right now

03:37:50 --> 03:37:51
in the jungle.

03:37:50 --> 03:37:52
- What do you think they're,

03:37:51 --> 03:37:54
'cause you've spoken about them being dangerous.

03:37:53 --> 03:37:58
What do you think their relationship with violence is?

03:37:57 --> 03:37:59
- [Paul] I think there's. - Why is violence

03:37:58 --> 03:38:03
part of their approach to the external world?

03:38:02 --> 03:38:05
- So from the best I understand it,

03:38:04 --> 03:38:09
that at the turn of the century, industrial revolution,

03:38:09 --> 03:38:15
we had sudden immense need for rubber

03:38:14 --> 03:38:18
for hoses and gaskets and wires and tires

03:38:17 --> 03:38:19
and the war machine.

03:38:18 --> 03:38:22
And the only way to get rubber was to come down

03:38:21 --> 03:38:25
to the Amazon rainforest and get the local people

03:38:24 --> 03:38:28
who knew the jungle to go out into the jungle

03:38:27 --> 03:38:31
and cut rubber trees and collect the latex.

03:38:30 --> 03:38:34
And Henry Ford tried doing Fordlandia,

03:38:33 --> 03:38:35
tried having rubber plantations,

03:38:34 --> 03:38:37
but leaf blight killed it.

03:38:36 --> 03:38:40
And so you had this period of horrendous extraction

03:38:39 --> 03:38:43
in the Amazon where the rubber barons were coming down

03:38:42 --> 03:38:46
and just raping and pillaging the tribes

03:38:45 --> 03:38:49
and making them go out to tap these trees.

03:38:48 --> 03:38:51
And the uncontacted tribes said no.

03:38:51 --> 03:38:55
They had their six foot long longbows,

03:38:54 --> 03:38:58
seven foot long arrows with giant bamboo tips.

03:38:57 --> 03:39:01
And they moved further back into the forest and they said,

03:39:00 --> 03:39:04
"We will not be conquered."

03:39:03 --> 03:39:06
And since that time, they've been out there

03:39:05 --> 03:39:09
and it's confusing because in a way,

03:39:08 --> 03:39:11
they're still running scared a century later.

03:39:10 --> 03:39:13
And their grandparents would've told them, you know,

03:39:12 --> 03:39:16
the outside world, everyone you see in the outside world is

03:39:15 --> 03:39:19
trying to kill you, so kill them first.

03:39:18 --> 03:39:21
So can you blame them for being violent?

03:39:20 --> 03:39:22
No.

03:39:21 --> 03:39:24
Is this river still wild

03:39:23 --> 03:39:26
because loggers were scared to go here?

03:39:25 --> 03:39:30
For a long time, for almost a century late.

03:39:29 --> 03:39:33
That's why this forest is still here, yes.

03:39:32 --> 03:39:36
And so is it a human rights issue that we protect

03:39:35 --> 03:39:39
he last people on Earth that have no government,

03:39:38 --> 03:39:44
no affiliation, no language that we can explain?

03:39:43 --> 03:39:46
We don't know what their medicinal plant knowledge is.

03:39:45 --> 03:39:47
We don't know their creation myths.

03:39:46 --> 03:39:49
We know nothing about them.

03:39:48 --> 03:39:52
And they're just out there right now with bows and arrows

03:39:51 --> 03:39:54
living in the dark, surviving in the jungle,

03:39:53 --> 03:39:57
naked without even spoons.

03:39:56 --> 03:39:59
Forget about the wheel, forget about iPhones.

03:39:58 --> 03:40:02
They got nothing and they're making it work.

03:40:01 --> 03:40:04
- We don't know their creation myths.

03:40:04 --> 03:40:08
So they have a very primitive existence.

03:40:08 --> 03:40:12
Well, do you think their values,

03:40:14 --> 03:40:17
first of all, do you think their nature is similar to ours?

03:40:16 --> 03:40:21
And how do their values differ from ours?

03:40:21 --> 03:40:24
- This is complicated because

03:40:25 --> 03:40:30
the anthropologist in me wants to say

03:40:29 --> 03:40:34
that they have a historical reason for the violent life

03:40:33 --> 03:40:36
that they have.

03:40:35 --> 03:40:40
You know, they experienced incredible generational trauma

03:40:39 --> 03:40:41
some time ago.

03:40:40 --> 03:40:43
And that, and because they've been living isolated

03:40:42 --> 03:40:45
in the jungle, that has permeated to become their culture.

03:40:44 --> 03:40:47
They've become a culture of violence.

03:40:47 --> 03:40:53
But yet the contacted, modern Indigenous communities

03:40:53 --> 03:40:57
that we work with, that are my friends that work here,

03:40:56 --> 03:41:00
just the other day we were speaking to one of them

03:40:59 --> 03:41:02
who was pulling spikes out of your hand

03:41:01 --> 03:41:06
while he was explaining that he tried to help them.

03:41:05 --> 03:41:08
The brothers, (speaks foreign language),

03:41:08 --> 03:41:10
he tried to help them.

03:41:09 --> 03:41:11
He tried to give them a gift.

03:41:10 --> 03:41:12
And what did they do?

03:41:11 --> 03:41:14
They shot him in the head.

03:41:13 --> 03:41:16
- Yeah, he said, "They're our brothers."

03:41:15 --> 03:41:18
And he tried to give them a

03:41:19 --> 03:41:22
bananas. - [Paul] Mm-hmm, plantains.

03:41:21 --> 03:41:24
- Plantains, boat full of plantains.

03:41:23 --> 03:41:25
And they shot at him.

03:41:24 --> 03:41:26
- They shot three arrows at him

03:41:25 --> 03:41:28
and one of them actually hit him in the skull

03:41:27 --> 03:41:29
and put him in the hospital,

03:41:28 --> 03:41:32
and he got helicopter evacuated from his community.

03:41:33 --> 03:41:35
And so he's brave for surviving,

03:41:34 --> 03:41:39
but he's a lucky survivor.

03:41:38 --> 03:41:41
They are incredibly accurate

03:41:40 --> 03:41:43
with those bamboo-tipped arrows

03:41:42 --> 03:41:44
and those arrows are seven feet long.

03:41:43 --> 03:41:46
So when you get hit by one,

03:41:45 --> 03:41:49
they come at a velocity that can rip through you.

03:41:48 --> 03:41:51
And the range

03:41:50 --> 03:41:55
on a shotgun is

03:41:54 --> 03:41:57
way shorter than the range on a longbow.

03:41:57 --> 03:42:02
You're talking about a couple hundred meters on a longbow.

03:42:01 --> 03:42:04
And they're deadly accurate.

03:42:03 --> 03:42:06
They can take spider monkeys out of a tree.

03:42:05 --> 03:42:09
And so there's stories of loggers,

03:42:08 --> 03:42:12
and I've seen the photos of the bodies of loggers

03:42:11 --> 03:42:15
who attract, who attacked one of the tribes.

03:42:14 --> 03:42:16
And the tribes hadn't done anything

03:42:15 --> 03:42:18
but these loggers came around a bend,

03:42:17 --> 03:42:19
they started shooting shotguns at the tribe,

03:42:18 --> 03:42:22
and the tribes scattered into the forest.

03:42:21 --> 03:42:24
And as the loggers' boat went around a bend,

03:42:23 --> 03:42:26
they just started flying arrows,

03:42:25 --> 03:42:28
took out the boat driver, boat skidded to the side

03:42:27 --> 03:42:30
and then everybody was standing in the river

03:42:29 --> 03:42:31
and you can't run.

03:42:30 --> 03:42:33
And the tribe just descended on them

03:42:32 --> 03:42:35
and just porcupined them full of arrows.

03:42:35 --> 03:42:38
- Shotgun versus bow.

03:42:37 --> 03:42:39
There's a shotgun shell here, by the way.

03:42:39 --> 03:42:42
- [Paul] Yeah. - From the loggers.

03:42:41 --> 03:42:45
- Mm-hmm, yeah, we picked that up yesterday.

03:42:44 --> 03:42:46
Was that yesterday?

03:42:45 --> 03:42:48
- That was, I don't know.

03:42:47 --> 03:42:48
- [Paul] I don't know,

03:42:47 --> 03:42:49
I think it was yesterday. - One of the things

03:42:48 --> 03:42:54
that happens here is time loses meaning

03:42:53 --> 03:42:57
in some kind of deep way that

03:42:58 --> 03:43:02
it does when you're in a big city in the United States,

03:43:01 --> 03:43:04
for example, and there's schedules and meetings

03:43:03 --> 03:43:05
and all this kind of stuff.

03:43:04 --> 03:43:09
It transforms the meaning, your experience of time,

03:43:08 --> 03:43:11
your interaction with time, the role of time, all of this.

03:43:13 --> 03:43:17
I've forgotten time

03:43:17 --> 03:43:21
and I've forgotten the existence of the outside world.

03:43:20 --> 03:43:22
- And how does that feel?

03:43:26 --> 03:43:28
- It feels more honest.

03:43:27 --> 03:43:31
It also puts in perspective like all the busyness,

03:43:30 --> 03:43:32
all the,

03:43:33 --> 03:43:37
it kind of takes the ant out of the ant colony and says,

03:43:36 --> 03:43:40
"Hey, this, you're just an ant

03:43:39 --> 03:43:43
and this is just an ant colony.

03:43:42 --> 03:43:45
And there's a big world out there."

03:43:45 --> 03:43:50
Yeah, it's a chance to be grateful,

03:43:49 --> 03:43:52
to celebrate this Earth of ours

03:43:51 --> 03:43:56
and the things that make it worth living on,

03:43:55 --> 03:43:58
including the simple things that make

03:43:57 --> 03:44:01
the individual life worth living, which is water,

03:44:00 --> 03:44:05
and then food and the rest is just details.

03:44:04 --> 03:44:07
Of course, the friendships and social interaction.

03:44:06 --> 03:44:10
That's a really big one, actually.

03:44:09 --> 03:44:11
That one I'm taking for granted

03:44:10 --> 03:44:14
'cause I didn't get a chance yet to really spend time alone.

03:44:13 --> 03:44:17
- [Paul] Hmm. - And when I came here,

03:44:16 --> 03:44:20
I've gotten a chance to hang out with you.

03:44:19 --> 03:44:22
And there's a kind of camaraderie,

03:44:21 --> 03:44:26
there's a friendship there that if that's broken,

03:44:25 --> 03:44:28
that's a tough one too.

03:44:28 --> 03:44:32
And you spent quite a lot of time alone in the jungle.

03:44:31 --> 03:44:34
You ever get alone out here?

03:44:34 --> 03:44:37
- Yeah, yeah.

03:44:36 --> 03:44:39
I mean, the first 15 years we were doing this,

03:44:38 --> 03:44:42
we, there would be times that JJ would be

03:44:41 --> 03:44:44
busy in town with his family.

03:44:43 --> 03:44:47
And I would, for sheer love of the rainforest,

03:44:46 --> 03:44:49
I would have to come alone out here.

03:44:48 --> 03:44:51
And we didn't have running,

03:44:50 --> 03:44:53
I didn't have running water, I didn't have lights.

03:44:52 --> 03:44:56
All I had was a couple of candles in the darkness

03:44:55 --> 03:44:58
and a tent and I was 20-something years old,

03:44:57 --> 03:45:00
living in the Amazon by myself.

03:44:59 --> 03:45:02
Your boat sunk.

03:45:01 --> 03:45:05
And yeah, it's incredibly lonely.

03:45:04 --> 03:45:07
I had to learn through experience

03:45:06 --> 03:45:09
'cause I thought there was a period,

03:45:08 --> 03:45:11
I think when you're, you know, you're young,

03:45:10 --> 03:45:13
as a young man, I had this thing like

03:45:12 --> 03:45:16
I wanted to prove that I could be like the explorers.

03:45:15 --> 03:45:19
I wanted to prove that I could handle the elements,

03:45:18 --> 03:45:20
that I could go out alone,

03:45:19 --> 03:45:24
that I could have these deep, connective moments

03:45:23 --> 03:45:25
with the jungle.

03:45:24 --> 03:45:27
And it's like, I did that and that's great.

03:45:27 --> 03:45:30
And you know what the kid from "Into the Wild" learned

03:45:29 --> 03:45:32
right before he died in that bus?

03:45:31 --> 03:45:35
That if you don't have somebody to share it with,

03:45:34 --> 03:45:36
it doesn't matter.

03:45:40 --> 03:45:44
- But on some kind of like,

03:45:43 --> 03:45:45
even just

03:45:46 --> 03:45:49
deep, human level,

03:45:49 --> 03:45:53
like even if you have somebody to share it with,

03:45:54 --> 03:45:58
you ever just get alone out here?

03:45:57 --> 03:46:02
Just like this sense of like existential dread

03:46:01 --> 03:46:03
of like what, you know,

03:46:02 --> 03:46:05
the jungle has a way of

03:46:06 --> 03:46:10
not caring about any individual organism

03:46:09 --> 03:46:12
because it just kinda turns,

03:46:11 --> 03:46:16
it's like, it makes you realize

03:46:15 --> 03:46:19
that life is finite quite intensely.

03:46:18 --> 03:46:20
- Hmm.

03:46:23 --> 03:46:27
For me, it's comforting being out here

03:46:26 --> 03:46:30
'cause I find the rat race, the national narrative,

03:46:29 --> 03:46:35
the need to make money, the, to worry about war,

03:46:34 --> 03:46:37
to be outraged about the newest thing that

03:46:36 --> 03:46:40
politician said and what that actor did,

03:46:39 --> 03:46:44
and it just, there's always just this just unending

03:46:43 --> 03:46:45
sort of media storm.

03:46:44 --> 03:46:49
And everyone's worried and everyone's trying to optimize

03:46:48 --> 03:46:52
their sunlight exposure and find the solution

03:46:51 --> 03:46:54
and buy the right, new thing.

03:46:53 --> 03:46:57
And to me coming out here,

03:46:57 --> 03:47:00
first of all, I mean something out here

03:46:59 --> 03:47:01
'cause I can help someone.

03:47:00 --> 03:47:03
I can help people, I can help these animals.

03:47:02 --> 03:47:05
And so I find my meaning out here.

03:47:06 --> 03:47:11
But also, you know, there's the losing the madness

03:47:10 --> 03:47:12
over the mountains.

03:47:11 --> 03:47:16
It's nature has always and for many people been

03:47:15 --> 03:47:17
where things make sense.

03:47:16 --> 03:47:20
And to me, I think I'm a simple analog type of person

03:47:19 --> 03:47:23
that it makes sense that when it rains,

03:47:22 --> 03:47:27
you get in the river to stay warm and you know,

03:47:26 --> 03:47:30
you wait for the dawn and you see a little tree snake

03:47:29 --> 03:47:32
and you see it just,

03:47:31 --> 03:47:34
it makes more sense.

03:47:33 --> 03:47:38
And I think that the overwhelming, teeming complexity

03:47:37 --> 03:47:41
that is inside the ant mound of society can be

03:47:40 --> 03:47:43
dizzying for some people.

03:47:42 --> 03:47:44
And I think that maybe it's the dyslexia,

03:47:43 --> 03:47:46
maybe it's just that I love nature.

03:47:45 --> 03:47:46
But

03:47:48 --> 03:47:52
now if I, when I land in JFK,

03:47:52 --> 03:47:57
I feel like a frightened animal on like a.

03:47:56 --> 03:47:58
(Lex laughs)

03:47:57 --> 03:48:02
It is as if you released like a,

03:48:01 --> 03:48:05
some animal that had never seen it onto like,

03:48:04 --> 03:48:06
into Times Square.

03:48:05 --> 03:48:08
And you could just imagine this dog with its ears back

03:48:07 --> 03:48:11
running away from taxis and just cowering from the noise.

03:48:10 --> 03:48:14
And it's just hustle and bustle and people are brutal.

03:48:13 --> 03:48:17
And how much you want it for, get in the car, you know?

03:48:16 --> 03:48:19
Screaming over the intercom and just everything,

03:48:18 --> 03:48:21
everything sensory changes.

03:48:20 --> 03:48:23
And let's get home, okay, let's go.

03:48:22 --> 03:48:25
You got a meeting, you gotta get to the next place.

03:48:24 --> 03:48:27
You gotta give a talk, you gotta sign.

03:48:27 --> 03:48:31
Out here, when we finish up here, what are we gonna do?

03:48:30 --> 03:48:33
We're gonna eat some food, maybe go catch a crocodile.

03:48:32 --> 03:48:34
(Lex laughs)

03:48:33 --> 03:48:35
Go walk around the jungle at night.

03:48:34 --> 03:48:38
Like it's slower, it makes sense.

03:48:37 --> 03:48:39
- [Lex] Yeah. - And there's that again,

03:48:38 --> 03:48:42
there's that deep meaning of that here,

03:48:41 --> 03:48:44
where we can be the guardians for good.

03:48:43 --> 03:48:47
We can be, we can hold that candle up

03:48:46 --> 03:48:50
and know for sure that we're protecting the trees

03:48:49 --> 03:48:50
from being destroyed.

03:48:49 --> 03:48:54
And it's that simple thing of just, this is good.

03:48:54 --> 03:48:58
There you go. - [Lex] It's simple.

03:48:57 --> 03:48:59
- In society, I feel like everyone's

03:48:58 --> 03:49:01
always losing their minds and forgetting

03:49:00 --> 03:49:03
the most basic of fundamental truths.

03:49:02 --> 03:49:06
And out here, you can't really argue with them.

03:49:06 --> 03:49:10
You know, when we needed water, it was like shit,

03:49:09 --> 03:49:12
if we don't get water, we're fucked.

03:49:11 --> 03:49:15
And that's to me, that's where the camaraderie comes from.

03:49:14 --> 03:49:17
Because no matter what, we'll be,

03:49:16 --> 03:49:19
we could go to the most fancy ass restaurant

03:49:18 --> 03:49:22
hrough the biggest, most famous people in the world.

03:49:21 --> 03:49:25
Doesn't matter, we still remember what it was like

03:49:24 --> 03:49:26
standing around in the jungle going,

03:49:25 --> 03:49:30
"Fuck, we're scared and we don't have water."

03:49:29 --> 03:49:33
We got reduced to the simplest form of humans.

03:49:32 --> 03:49:34
And that's something.

03:49:33 --> 03:49:37
And we survived, and that's cool.

03:49:36 --> 03:49:39
- And you take all the,

03:49:38 --> 03:49:41
all those people in their nice dresses

03:49:40 --> 03:49:43
in those fancy restaurants and you put them

03:49:42 --> 03:49:44
in those conditions,

03:49:43 --> 03:49:46
they're all gonna want the same thing, just water and.

03:49:45 --> 03:49:47
- Yes. - [Lex] It's all

03:49:46 --> 03:49:50
the same thing. - All the beautiful people.

03:49:49 --> 03:49:53
- How has your view of your own mortality evolved

03:49:52 --> 03:49:55
over your interaction with the jungle?

03:49:54 --> 03:49:57
How often do you think about your death?

03:49:58 --> 03:50:03
- Well, I don't anymore 'cause the,

03:50:02 --> 03:50:05
I've come to believe that there is a benevolent

03:50:06 --> 03:50:10
god, spirit, creator taking care of us.

03:50:09 --> 03:50:11
And

03:50:12 --> 03:50:15
I don't think about my own death.

03:50:14 --> 03:50:16
We have a little bit of time here

03:50:15 --> 03:50:19
and we clearly know nothing about what we're doing here.

03:50:19 --> 03:50:24
And it seems like we just have to do the best we can.

03:50:24 --> 03:50:28
And so I just, it doesn't scare me.

03:50:27 --> 03:50:30
I've come close to dying a lot of times

03:50:30 --> 03:50:34
and I just don't think I,

03:50:33 --> 03:50:37
you don't wanna have a bad death, first of all.

03:50:36 --> 03:50:39
You don't wanna

03:50:38 --> 03:50:40
be a statistic.

03:50:39 --> 03:50:40
You don't wanna find out.

03:50:39 --> 03:50:42
You don't wanna like try out a,

03:50:41 --> 03:50:45
be the first to try out a new product and oops,

03:50:44 --> 03:50:46
it crushed you.

03:50:45 --> 03:50:48
You know, that's a terrible way to go.

03:50:47 --> 03:50:49
Or the people that used to, you know,

03:50:48 --> 03:50:50
in the Gold Rush they were using mercury

03:50:49 --> 03:50:53
and they were all getting, or lead, it was lead poisoning.

03:50:52 --> 03:50:55
And it's like, oh, you know,

03:50:54 --> 03:50:57
a few million people died that way.

03:50:56 --> 03:51:00
And it's like, you wanna, you want a good death, you know?

03:50:59 --> 03:51:02
You wanna, staring down the eyes of a tiger

03:51:01 --> 03:51:04
or hanging off the edge of a cliff,

03:51:03 --> 03:51:05
saving somebody's life, something,

03:51:04 --> 03:51:07
something worthy, warrior's death.

03:51:06 --> 03:51:12
- Mm-hmm, riding a 16-foot black caiman, just.

03:51:11 --> 03:51:14
- Boots on, screaming.

03:51:13 --> 03:51:18
Yeah, that would be fun, that'd be a good one.

03:51:18 --> 03:51:20
- A lot of people say that you carry

03:51:19 --> 03:51:22
the spirit of Steve Irwin

03:51:23 --> 03:51:28
your heart, in the way you carry yourself in this world.

03:51:27 --> 03:51:31
I mean, he, that guy was full of joy.

03:51:31 --> 03:51:35
- If I have a percentage of Steve Irwin, I would be honored.

03:51:34 --> 03:51:39
But that guy, I think there's only one Steve.

03:51:38 --> 03:51:40
I think that he was,

03:51:39 --> 03:51:45
he occupied his own strata of just shining light.

03:51:44 --> 03:51:48
Everything was positive, enthusiasm,

03:51:47 --> 03:51:49
love and happiness,

03:51:48 --> 03:51:51
and save the animals and do better

03:51:50 --> 03:51:53
and let's make it fun.

03:51:52 --> 03:51:57
And that was so infectious that it sort of transcended

03:51:57 --> 03:51:59
his TV show.

03:51:58 --> 03:52:01
It transcended his conservation work.

03:52:00 --> 03:52:04
It transcended business and entrepreneurship.

03:52:03 --> 03:52:08
It just, through sheer magnetism and enthusiasm,

03:52:07 --> 03:52:11
he just, I mean, everyone knew who Steve was.

03:52:10 --> 03:52:13
Everyone loved Steve.

03:52:12 --> 03:52:15
We still all love Steve.

03:52:14 --> 03:52:19
And so it's just amazing what one spirit can do.

03:52:18 --> 03:52:23
So if anybody, you know, makes that comparison,

03:52:22 --> 03:52:25
I get really uncomfortable because to me,

03:52:24 --> 03:52:28
Steve Irwin is like just the GOAT.

03:52:27 --> 03:52:30
And so I'm okay with that.

03:52:31 --> 03:52:35
- Well, I at least agree with that comparison.

03:52:34 --> 03:52:36
Having spent time with you,

03:52:35 --> 03:52:39
there's just an eternal flame of joy

03:52:39 --> 03:52:42
and adventure too,

03:52:42 --> 03:52:44
just pulling you.

03:52:45 --> 03:52:48
A dark question, but do you think you might meet

03:52:47 --> 03:52:49
he same end,

03:52:48 --> 03:52:52
giving your life in some way to something you love?

03:52:53 --> 03:52:55
- That is a dark question,

03:52:54 --> 03:52:58
but I think most likely I'll get whacked by loggers.

03:52:58 --> 03:53:02
I think that loggers or gold miners will take me out.

03:53:01 --> 03:53:05
I don't picture myself going from animals, but.

03:53:06 --> 03:53:09
- That would be heartbreaking too.

03:53:08 --> 03:53:11
- Yeah, it would but yeah, at the same time though,

03:53:10 --> 03:53:13
like the Kurt Cobain value of that,

03:53:12 --> 03:53:15
if I died doing what I love, to protect the river,

03:53:14 --> 03:53:16
that'd be so worth so much more.

03:53:15 --> 03:53:18
A lot, like we'd get the 30 million if I died tomorrow,

03:53:17 --> 03:53:19
for sure.

03:53:18 --> 03:53:21
So we've already talked about this with my friends.

03:53:20 --> 03:53:23
I'm like, if I get whacked. - [Lex] Yeah.

03:53:22 --> 03:53:25
- Do the foundation, make the documentary,

03:53:24 --> 03:53:27
protect the river, protect the heartbeats.

03:53:26 --> 03:53:29
Call it "The Heartbeats",

03:53:28 --> 03:53:31
"Junglekeepers: the Heartbeats", you know?

03:53:30 --> 03:53:34
Be ready for it because these things do happen.

03:53:33 --> 03:53:35
People get pissed if you get in their way

03:53:34 --> 03:53:39
and as many happy people as and whose lives we're changing,

03:53:38 --> 03:53:42
there's also gonna be some jealous, shitty, upset people

03:53:41 --> 03:53:44
who are mad that they can't make prostitutes

03:53:43 --> 03:53:46
out of young girls and keep destroying the planet.

03:53:45 --> 03:53:49
And so they might just erase you.

03:53:49 --> 03:53:51
Me.

03:53:51 --> 03:53:53
- Well, I hope you,

03:53:54 --> 03:54:00
like a Clint Eastwood character, just impossible to kill.

03:53:59 --> 03:54:01
- I hope so.

03:54:00 --> 03:54:01
- I like how you squinted your eyes.

03:54:00 --> 03:54:05
(Paul and Lex laugh)

03:54:05 --> 03:54:07
On cue.

03:54:07 --> 03:54:10
Who do you think will play you in a movie?

03:54:09 --> 03:54:12
- God, somebody with the right nose.

03:54:11 --> 03:54:13
- Yeah.

03:54:12 --> 03:54:16
- Somebody who can live up to this (indistinct).

03:54:15 --> 03:54:18
- Yeah, all right, Italian. - [Paul] Yeah.

03:54:18 --> 03:54:20
- It's funny.

03:54:19 --> 03:54:24
Do you think of yourself as Italian or human, American?

03:54:23 --> 03:54:26
- That's the thing I don't, you know,

03:54:26 --> 03:54:32
my life has been the United Nations of whatever.

03:54:31 --> 03:54:34
Like I just, every, to me, I just, I don't,

03:54:33 --> 03:54:35
that's the other thing.

03:54:34 --> 03:54:38
You go back to society and everyone's obsessed with race.

03:54:37 --> 03:54:41
To me, I'm like, look, leopards have black babies

03:54:40 --> 03:54:44
and yellow babies, one mother, like, they're all leopards.

03:54:43 --> 03:54:49
And I'm so colorblind and race blind and everything else.

03:54:48 --> 03:54:50
I've lived in India.

03:54:49 --> 03:54:52
My friends are Peruvian.

03:54:51 --> 03:54:55
My family, we got Italian, Filipino, just everything.

03:54:55 --> 03:54:58
And so I've, I'm so immersed in it

03:54:57 --> 03:55:03
hat when I find it very jarring and disconcerting

03:55:02 --> 03:55:08
how much time we spend talking about different religions

03:55:07 --> 03:55:09
and just the differences in humans.

03:55:08 --> 03:55:12
I'm like, dude, we're talking about whether or not

03:55:11 --> 03:55:15
our ecosystems are gonna be able to provide for us.

03:55:14 --> 03:55:17
We're talking about nuclear,

03:55:16 --> 03:55:18
what we're talking about this some pretty serious shit

03:55:18 --> 03:55:20
on the table.

03:55:19 --> 03:55:22
And we're over here arguing over like shades of gray

03:55:21 --> 03:55:24
of, it's so trivial. - [Lex] Yeah.

03:55:23 --> 03:55:26
- And that shit drives me crazy.

03:55:25 --> 03:55:28
And as does the outrage where it's like, no,

03:55:27 --> 03:55:30
you have to care.

03:55:29 --> 03:55:32
I've been criticized for not caring enough about that.

03:55:31 --> 03:55:35
And I'm like, I'm gonna,

03:55:35 --> 03:55:38
who cares what the hell I am?

03:55:37 --> 03:55:39
Who gives a shit, what the hell?

03:55:38 --> 03:55:41
I'm a human, we're all human. - [Lex] Yeah.

03:55:40 --> 03:55:44
- It's not that easy, but it's kind of fun sometimes.

03:55:43 --> 03:55:47
And we're at a better time

03:55:46 --> 03:55:49
and hit like when you think about like the Middle Ages,

03:55:48 --> 03:55:51
like even if you were a king,

03:55:50 --> 03:55:52
you still didn't have it that good.

03:55:51 --> 03:55:54
You didn't have pineapples in the winter.

03:55:53 --> 03:55:56
You didn't even know what the fuck a pineapple was.

03:55:55 --> 03:55:59
We have pineapples whenever we want them.

03:55:59 --> 03:56:03
We can fly on planes to other countries.

03:56:02 --> 03:56:04
- By the way, let's clarify.

03:56:03 --> 03:56:07
We, you mean a large fraction of the world.

03:56:07 --> 03:56:12
You know, I mentioned to you one of the biggest things

03:56:12 --> 03:56:16
I've noticed when I immigrated from the Soviet Union

03:56:15 --> 03:56:18
to the United States is the

03:56:18 --> 03:56:21
how plentiful bananas and pineapples were.

03:56:20 --> 03:56:22
- [Paul] Yeah. - The fruit section,

03:56:21 --> 03:56:24
the produce section of the,

03:56:23 --> 03:56:27
didn't have to wait in line at the grocery store,

03:56:26 --> 03:56:29
could just eat as many bananas and pineapples

03:56:28 --> 03:56:32
and cherries and watermelon as you want.

03:56:31 --> 03:56:35
That's, not everybody has that.

03:56:34 --> 03:56:37
- No, that's true, not everybody has that, but.

03:56:38 --> 03:56:41
- But everybody could be that king now, pineapple.

03:56:40 --> 03:56:44
- But a growing number of people today.

03:56:43 --> 03:56:46
- Can feast on pineapple.

03:56:45 --> 03:56:48
- Can feast on pineapple and have toasters

03:56:47 --> 03:56:52
and new, distracting apps all the way until the grave.

03:56:51 --> 03:56:54
- That's the thing that I also noticed is

03:56:53 --> 03:56:58
I don't think so much about politics when I'm here, or.

03:56:57 --> 03:56:59
- We haven't even talked about it.

03:56:58 --> 03:57:01
- We haven't, don't talk about the stupid

03:57:02 --> 03:57:05
ifferences between humans.

03:57:04 --> 03:57:07
- [Paul] Nah. - Except to just kind of laugh

03:57:06 --> 03:57:09
at the absurdity of it on occasion.

03:57:08 --> 03:57:11
- I've been too busy trying to survive glaciers

03:57:10 --> 03:57:14
and jungles and avalanches and all kinds of shit.

03:57:14 --> 03:57:17
- Do you think nature is brutal

03:57:16 --> 03:57:18
as Warner Herzog showed it?

03:57:17 --> 03:57:19
Or is it beautiful?

03:57:21 --> 03:57:27
- I think the brutality of nature is the chaos.

03:57:26 --> 03:57:31
And I think that we are the only ones in it

03:57:30 --> 03:57:33
hat are capable of organizing

03:57:32 --> 03:57:35
in the direction of order and light.

03:57:36 --> 03:57:41
So yes, there are gonna be hyenas tearing each other apart.

03:57:40 --> 03:57:44
Yes, there's gonna be war-torn nations

03:57:43 --> 03:57:47
and poor, starving children, but

03:57:46 --> 03:57:49
we as humans have the power

03:57:50 --> 03:57:55
to work towards something more organized than that.

03:57:54 --> 03:57:58
- So there is a force within nature

03:57:57 --> 03:58:02
that's always searching for order, for good.

03:58:01 --> 03:58:04
- It's kind of a unifying theory if you think about it.

03:58:03 --> 03:58:06
I mean, all of the chaos of history

03:58:05 --> 03:58:09
and the wars and the chaos of nature,

03:58:08 --> 03:58:13
we, through technology and organization,

03:58:12 --> 03:58:13
there's so many people,

03:58:12 --> 03:58:15
more people today than ever before I think

03:58:14 --> 03:58:16
who are so concerned,

03:58:15 --> 03:58:20
who realize that the incredible power,

03:58:19 --> 03:58:22
like what Jane Goodall says about, you know,

03:58:21 --> 03:58:23
how you can affect the people around you,

03:58:22 --> 03:58:25
how you can do good in the world,

03:58:24 --> 03:58:27
how you can change the narrative of conservation

03:58:27 --> 03:58:29
from one of loss and darkness

03:58:28 --> 03:58:31
to one of innovation and light.

03:58:30 --> 03:58:33
Like, we can do incredible things.

03:58:32 --> 03:58:36
We are the masters as humans.

03:58:36 --> 03:58:39
And I think that we're at,

03:58:38 --> 03:58:41
on the cusp of sort of understanding

03:58:40 --> 03:58:42
the true potential of that.

03:58:41 --> 03:58:46
Like I just think that more than ever, people,

03:58:45 --> 03:58:49
people have harnessed this ability to do good in the world

03:58:48 --> 03:58:53
and be proud of it and just change

03:58:52 --> 03:58:57
the darkness into something else.

03:58:57 --> 03:59:01
- When you have lived here

03:59:00 --> 03:59:04
and taken in the ways of the Amazon jungle,

03:59:03 --> 03:59:07
how have your views of God, you mentioned,

03:59:07 --> 03:59:11
how have your views of God changed?

03:59:10 --> 03:59:13
Who is God?

03:59:12 --> 03:59:15
- I've come to believe that, again,

03:59:14 --> 03:59:17
back to that Christ wasn't a Christian,

03:59:16 --> 03:59:20
Muhammad wasn't a Muslim, and Buddha wasn't a Buddhist.

03:59:19 --> 03:59:23
That like the game,

03:59:22 --> 03:59:27
the game is love and compassion

03:59:26 --> 03:59:31
and the universe is chaotic and dangerous

03:59:30 --> 03:59:33
and nature is chaotic and dangerous.

03:59:32 --> 03:59:36
But we, if this is some sort of a biological video game,

03:59:35 --> 03:59:38
that our reality,

03:59:39 --> 03:59:42
that the test is can we be good?

03:59:42 --> 03:59:45
And we go through it every day.

03:59:44 --> 03:59:46
Can you be good to your parent?

03:59:45 --> 03:59:48
Can you be good to your partner?

03:59:47 --> 03:59:49
Can you be good to your coworkers?

03:59:48 --> 03:59:51
Can, it's so difficult.

03:59:50 --> 03:59:53
And we see how people can cheat

03:59:52 --> 03:59:55
and steal and hurt and destroy

03:59:57 --> 04:00:02
and the incredible impact that it has on the world,

04:00:01 --> 04:00:06
the returning, exponential impact

04:00:07 --> 04:00:11
hat one act of kindness, one act of good can do.

04:00:11 --> 04:00:12
And so

04:00:14 --> 04:00:16
I see nature as God.

04:00:15 --> 04:00:21
I see the religions as different cultural manifestations

04:00:20 --> 04:00:23
of the same truth,

04:00:24 --> 04:00:27
the same creative force.

04:00:28 --> 04:00:31
Maybe me and you have the same beliefs

04:00:30 --> 04:00:34
and your aliens are my angels.

04:00:34 --> 04:00:37
- Well, thank you for being

04:00:37 --> 04:00:42
one of the humans trying to do good in this world.

04:00:41 --> 04:00:47
And thank you for bringing me along for some adventure

04:00:46 --> 04:00:50
and I believe more adventure awaits.

04:00:50 --> 04:00:54
- Thank you for being enough of a psychopath

04:00:53 --> 04:00:59
to actually just sign on to come into the Amazon rainforest

04:00:58 --> 04:01:01
in a suit.

04:01:00 --> 04:01:04
And a year ago when you told me that you were gonna do this,

04:01:03 --> 04:01:06
I truly didn't believe you.

04:01:05 --> 04:01:07
So for being a man of your word

04:01:06 --> 04:01:10
and for the incredible work you do to connect humans

04:01:09 --> 04:01:13
and to create dialogue and to do good in the world

04:01:12 --> 04:01:14
and for all the adventures that we've had,

04:01:13 --> 04:01:16
thank you so much.

04:01:15 --> 04:01:17
- Thank you, brother.

04:01:16 --> 04:01:18
- Lex, thanks man.

04:01:19 --> 04:01:22
- Thanks for listening to this conversation

04:01:21 --> 04:01:24
with Paul Rosolie.

04:01:23 --> 04:01:25
To support this podcast,

04:01:24 --> 04:01:28
please check out our sponsors in the description.

04:01:27 --> 04:01:30
And now let me leave you with some words

04:01:29 --> 04:01:32
from Joseph Campbell.

04:01:31 --> 04:01:35
The big question is whether you are going to be able

04:01:34 --> 04:01:39
to say a hearty yes to your adventure.

04:01:39 --> 04:01:44
Thank you for listening and hope to see you next time.
